## Note to Accredited Entities on the use of the funding proposal template

• Accredited Entities should provide summary information in the proposal with cross-reference to annexes such as feasibility studies, gender action plan, term sheet, etc.

• Accreditations are valid for 5 years. Any changes to the project description or any other material provided in the funding proposal. Updates to the funding proposal and/or annexes must be reflected within this timeframe.

• The total number of pages for the funding proposal (excluding annexes) should not exceed 20 pages. Materials exceeding the prescribed length will not be assessed within the usual service standard time frame.

• The recommended font is Arial, size 11.

• Under the GCF’s Information Disclosure Policy, project and programme funding proposals will be disclosed on the GCF website simultaneously with the submission to the Board, subject to any confidentiality requirements. ## A.21. Executive summary (max 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

### Climate change problem
1. Smallholder farmers in Africa are more vulnerable to climate change than ever as temperature increases, extreme weather events, droughts, and changes in rainfall patterns affect their livelihoods and food security while they have little ability to adapt.
2. The average annual rate of decline in agricultural productivity growth in Africa has fallen by 34% since 1961, a larger decline than any other region. As agriculture employs


---
**Page 7**
---

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 3

54.6% of the workforce in Sub-Saharan Africa and 95% of cropland is rainfed1. African farmers are disproportionately vulnerable to climate change. Rising temperature and erratic rainfall patterns will cause more frequent droughts, floods, and heatwaves. These extreme weather events will increase pest pressure on staple breeding grounds for pests and diseases which will further reduce crop yields. The International Monetary Fund forecasts that crop yields in sub-Saharan Africa will decline by 5 to 17 percent by 2030. Insect pests caused at least $30 billion of damage to crops in Sub-Saharan Africa in 2019.

Exacerbating these challenges, smallholder farmers often have limited access to resources like fertilizers, improved seeds, and irrigation systems. This makes it difficult for them to adapt to changing weather patterns or invest in new technologies that can help them increase their yields. In addition, the majority of public climate finance goes to projects with smallholder farmers, who produce around 60 percent of food in Africa.

Crop failures and declining yields will significantly reduce farmers' income, pushing them deeper into poverty. Food insecurity is already a major problem for many African countries. The effects of climate change are felt more acutely by the poor; for example, droughts in recent years have caused widespread food insecurity in East African countries like Ethiopia and Kenya. In 2019, 35 percent of Kenyan households were food insecure.

The Yale Climate Connections finds that of the 30 deadliest climate events ever in Africa, six happened in 2022 and 2023.

## The Problem and Intervention

### Acumen Resilient Agriculture Fund II (ARAF II or the Fund)

ARAF is an adaptation-focused blended finance venture capital fund that seeks to improve the climate resilience of smallholder farmers and the agriculture value chain, while also generating financial returns. ARAF II builds on the success of its predecessor, ARAF I, which aims to continue executing ARAF's investment impact strategy by identifying and scaling ecosystems enabling companies (SECs) in Africa that are addressing climate change challenges. These SECs have demonstrated increased productivity enablers, as well as their ability to adapt to and absorb the impacts of climate change, leading to improved livelihoods for farmers and their communities.

ARAF II is a USD500MM fund co-financed from development finance institutions (DFIs), foundations, family offices and High Net Worth Individuals (HNWIs) to deploy into 18-20 companies across six Select East and West African countries. ARAF II continues to engage with Kenya, Tanzania, Ethiopia, and Senegal in hopes of scaling potential investments in these countries. The Fund is currently investing USD50MM in ARAF I; it has developed an impressive advance pipeline of dozens of companies across these geographies.

### ARAF I has demonstrated the attractiveness of investing in African agribusinesses as a path towards improving climate resilience and generating financial returns for investors. ARAF II builds on this success by further promoting food security. ARAF seeks to continue implementing the same strategy and to invest across the following sectors:

- **Aggregator Platforms:** agribusinesses that provide bundled solutions including climate resilient inputs and/or affordable credit bundled with services such as extension services and trainings, and off take agreements to private buyers.
- **Digital Platforms:** Information & Communication Technology (ICT) agribusinesses, leveraging the efficiency, scale, and precision of digital tools to provide bundled solutions tailored to farmer needs. For instance, this could be a platform that provides climate information, weather forecasts, market price information, or extension support like video trainings, weather forecast information or market prices information to improve farmers' productivity and mitigate weather impacts.

## Innovative Financial Solutions: Provide farmers with access to innovative financial solutions (i.e., affordable credit for seeds, pesticides, insurance subsidies, etc.), enabling them to diversify their income base by investing in additional productive assets and making it affordable for them to buy farm inputs.

### 1. ARAF II intends to focus on investing in, and scaling, early-to-mid-stage agribusinesses. The majority of the investments will be made through a Pre-Series A round, with several of ARAF I’s more recent investments at that stage delivering impressive returns. ARAF II is also looking for opportunities to invest in later-stage companies, particularly those that were aligned with the ARAF mission and would have a direct impact on smallholder farmers but needed additional capital to scale their operations. The fund will be investing in a mix of agribusinesses as the fund expands its geography into North Africa, where the deals are anticipated to become larger in size, scale, and complexity than those available in these markets.

### 2. ARAF II aspires to scale the gains achieved by ARAF I, as follows:

- **ARAF I was sponsored by Acumen and anchored through the Green Climate Fund. The fund reached first close in 2019 and started investing in the same year. Over the last 5 years, ARAF I has completed 13 investments across 8 countries with a total of $47 million invested to date.**
- **As of Dec 2023, ARAF II portfolio companies have a combined total of 1,748 employees. As of the same date, ARAF I’s portfolio companies have increased their employment by 90% post ARAF I’s initial investment. Most of the growth in employment has been driven by the expansion of existing businesses into new markets, economic outcomes, local institutional knowledge and strengthening local supply and distribution channels required to scale these businesses.**
- **ARAF I’s investments have already generated strong impact beyond financial returns, with farmers reporting significant improvements in various aspects of their livelihoods. ARAF I’s portfolio companies have reached 1.7MM farmers, and on course to meet, and exceed, its impact target of 2MM farmers reached and 3MM lives impacted by the end of 2024. The impact assessment conducted with ARAF I’s portfolio companies, obtained through surveys of farmers engaging with ARAF I portfolio companies*, indicates that of:**
    - **80% reported increases in farm productivity.**
    - **90% reported increased income.**
    - **85% improved reported quality of life.**

### 3. Additionally, the investments undertaken by ARAF II portfolio companies have strengthened the climate resilience of their businesses. Of the 45% of the farmers who were assessed as resilient (up from a baseline of 23%), implying an increased ability to cope with the growing challenges posed by climate hazards, as a direct result of these investments, they reported: improved access to inputs and services (e.g., credit, support infrastructure (water and mechanization), and premium markets. The interaction of these elements within their businesses has led to significant improvements in their livelihoods. ARAF II’s investment into their land and diversification of their income. ARAF II has firsthand experience of portfolio companies that have been able to scale their operations, increase the productivity of their farms, provide credit to smallholder farmers on credit, has helped their clients 3x their crop yield and increase their cows’ milk production by up to 20%. These investments have also led to a significant reduction in water use, pest resistance, extremely productive, flowering day-old chicks and 6-week old birds to smallholder farmers, have helped farmers increase and diversify their income, while reducing income volatility.

### Climate benefits

9. Research indicates that smallholder farmers in Africa are adapting to climate change by introducing new crop varieties, planting planning seasons; diversifying crop production; introducing crop rotation; storing and utilizing crop residue as emergency feed; as well as seedling energy crops; planting early maturing crop varieties; intercropping; using improved water management techniques such as rainwater harvesting and drip irrigation.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 5

Various factors including educational level, farm size, gender of the head of the household, size of the household, etc., impacted smallholder farmers' adoption of adaptation practices.

The results of studies of smallholder farmers in the Central Rift Valley in Ethiopia showed that increases in household income and access to credit were positively associated with increased climate information, and access to extension services had a positive association with the adoption of climate change adaptation strategies.12

Similar findings have been reported from other countries. In Kenya, for example, higher levels of motivation for implementing adaptation practices was to improve financial security through increased yields and income.

Secure land tenure systems, education around the effects of climate change, access to sustainable agricultural technologies, and improved access to credit are among the most important enabling factors that can enable farmers' adoption of climate adaptation practices. Lastly, limited government support with farm loans, lack of extension services, and poor infrastructure were cited as major barriers to smallholder farmers.

Education on climate smart agriculture practices were shown to be the main barriers limiting farmer adoption of climate change adaptation strategies in Ethiopia.

10 ARAF expects that by supporting smallholder farmers with integrated packages of improved inputs, finance, training and extension services, information, and markets, farmers will increase yields, decrease yield volatility, elevate income levels, and improve their livelihoods. The package includes a combination of inputs such as seeds, fertilizer, enabling them to plan their spending and consumption more effectively, fostering savings and asset building to mitigate the effects of climate change on their livelihoods. Through these investments, ARAF is aims to provide smallholder farmers with a combination of:

- access to improved inputs, including climate-resilient seeds
- access to credit for purchasing farm equipment and innovative seeding products and credit models that enable farmers make ongoing improvements to their farmland and investments to their productive capacity
- timely access to climate and weather-related information to enable proper planning and decision making for protecting crops from adverse effects of climate change, including droughts, floods, and pests
- access to insurance products, including weather, livestock, and yield insurance, to improve their ability to absorb climate shocks
- access to training on sustainable agricultural knowledge and extension services to improve their adoption of adaptation practices
- access to infrastructure, including irrigation and mechanization, to improve productivity and profitability
- access to premium markets to improve their income and profitability, which allows them build savings, make on-farm investments (soil testing, irrigating), purchase better inputs and crop insurance.

11. The interventions facilitated by ARAF's its portfolio companies are expected to result in:

- increased adoption of climate smart agriculture amongst farmers, which positively impacts their ability to adapt to the effects of climate change
- enhanced agricultural productivity through investments in soil testing, high-quality inputs, and water management solutions
- improved resilience against weather-related shocks, including droughts, floods, and pestilence.
- reduced post-harvest losses due to direct links to processors and market connections.
- increased income and productivity for smallholder farmers
- increased investment in agricultural productivity by farmers given increased income, including by expanding area under cultivation and protecting soil quality

B. PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION

### B.1 Climate context (max. 1000 words, approximately two pages)

#### CLIMATE CONTEXT OF ARAF'S PROPOSED COUNTRIES

1. Globally, scientists and researchers have recorded rising temperatures and increases in the atmospheric concentration of CO2, CH4 and other gases. It is widely accepted that these phenomena are due to human activities, particularly the burning of fossil fuels. The resulting climate change has already had significant adverse results for life on the planet, with far-reaching social, economic and political impacts.
2. Agriculture is important for the growth and socio-economic development across Africa as the sector employs 65% -70% of Africa's labor force and contributes 30%-40% to GDP on average. However, productivity remains low compared to other regions due to a combination of factors including poor infrastructure, limited access to markets, low levels of irrigation and low utilization of modern inputs.
3. Temperature and precipitation are the two primary climatic indicators impacting agriculture in Africa, particularly crop yields. Both have increased over time with significant variations across different countries, regions, and seasons of both drought and flooding. The Center for Research on the Epidemiology of Disasters (CRED), relying on data from the Global Climate Risk Index (GRI) compiled by the World Meteorological Organization (WMO), found that Africa has been the most affected continent in terms of climate change impacts over the period from 2005 to 2011. According to the GRI, Africa accounts for 33% of droughts occurring globally.
4. Eastern and Western Africa are projected to be most economically vulnerable to climate change impacts. In a low warming scenario (RCP8.5), the average temperature in Eastern Africa is expected to increase by 9.9°C between 2010-2040.

#### Impact of climate change in ARAF's investment region

**Morocco**

Morocco has been experiencing more frequent and intense floods, like the ones affected the region of Quirika, the province of Guelmim, the province of Sidi Ifni, Grand Casablanca, Tangier, and the Ghala Valley. The 2010 flood in Agadir-Souss Massa dealt a severe blow to agriculture, causing damage to the 2010-2011 crop year. Hundreds of hectares of cereals, vegetables, and fodder crops were devastated. The bad weather severely damaged the

## Water supply systems of livestock farmers, the existing hydropower infrastructure, and certain rural roads and tracks in Morocco. Overall, the assessment of the damage caused by the 2019 floods was $47 million.

### Rainfed agriculture still accounts for 86% of agricultural lands, with highly variable crop yields that are closely correlated with precipitation levels. Rainfall shocks explain close to 37% of the variability of Morocco's total output over the last three decades. The droughts in 2015 and 2019 were particularly severe, as they occurred during a dry spell that amplified the recession induced by the COVID-19 pandemic[2]. The 2020 crop year recorded a 34% decline from the 30-year average. The impact of this low-rainfall volume was exacerbated by its poor and irregular spatial distribution over time, with rainfall deficits reaching up to 85% in some regions during critical growth periods (about 40 days). The rainfall deficit affected all cereal regions. In Chaouia and Haouz, the rainfall deficit was 100% on average, while in Saida, Priz, and the north, the deficit varied between 30 and 45%.

### Egypt

#### In terms of the number of people, Egypt, Libya, Morocco and Tunisia are the most exposed countries to sea level rise. Among MENA countries, Egypt is particularly exposed with several coastal cities at risk of inundation. In the Nile Delta, the average rate of land subsidence has been estimated between 0.5 mm/year (1972–2003) and 0.8 mm/year (1964–2003). The sea level rise is projected to reach a maximum value of about 0.75mm (median sea level rise scenario for SSP-85) and additional subsidence up to 0.25cm, threatening around 2 million people in the Nile Delta by 2100.

#### Annual temperatures in Egypt have increased at an average rate of 0.1°C per decade between 1961 and 2013, with the rate of temperature increase being higher during winter (increasing a 0.52°C per decade). Surface temperatures in Egypt and elsewhere in the Nile River Basin have increased by an average of 0.16°C to 0.4°C until the end of this century[3]. The mean annual rainfall has decreased from about 78 mm/year in 1960–1989 to 52 mm/year in Egypt mostly relies on the River Nile for water, which is mainly used in agriculture, municipal and industrial irrigation, and hydropower generation. The river also provides drinking water for millions of Egyptians living in the Egyptian highlands.

#### The effects of climate variability, primarily rainfall variability in the Ethiopian highlands and lake Chad basin, have been felt by Egypt through changes in the flow regime of the Nile River and large implications for water resources in Egypt. In last 30 years, 10 drought events with marked water flow deficit were recorded in Egypt after one month lag from their onset in the East African countries: 1972, 1978, 1982, 1983, 1984, 1986, 1990, 1995, 1998 and 2002. This climate variability has resulted in a decrease in crop yields, especially wheat, maize, and rice, and an increase in food prices.

### Nigeria

#### The wet season could be postponed in the south and are reduced in the north. Nigeria is susceptible to both floods and droughts. Flood incidents have already had catastrophic impacts on the country's development, diminishing decades of progress made since independence[4].

## Côte d'Ivoire

### 10. Smallerholder farmers in Ivory Coast face significant challenges due to climate change, as evidenced by recent events such as the ENDO-induced droughts of 2022-2023[1]. These droughts substantially reduced crop production, particularly affecting staples like cocao, thereby diminishing incomes for smallholder farmers. Climate change has also led to increased frequency and severity of flooding in Ivory Coast[2], which threatens cocoa production in Ivory Coast[3]. Such events underscore the necessity for climate-resilient practices and adaptation strategies to support smallholder farmers.

### 11. Smallerholder farmers have grappled with a series of significant climatic events over the past decade, profoundly impacting their agricultural practices and livelihoods. Notable occurrences include the recent ENDO-induced droughts of 2022-2023[4], which resulted in diminished crop yields and incomes for smallholder farmers, particularly in regions such as Bouaké[5]. Moreover, climate change has been identified as a major risk factor in Senegal. Rainfall is the most significant climate change risk in Senegal, accounting for 41% of the average annual natural hazard risk index (NHI) and 30% of the total NHI[6]. Climate-induced challenges have translated into reduced crop yields and livestock productivity, intensifying economic hardships and food insecurity for farmers[7]. Additionally, the region is experiencing prolonged droughts and heatwaves in semi-arid areas which further cripple crop yields and livestock productivity.

# B

The yield of improved food insecurity, decreased livestock productivity, and disrupted market access for farmers**1**. The vulnerability of Senegal's agricultural sector, which employs 70% of the workforce and contributes 17% to GDP**2**, underscores the urgency for climate-resilient agricultural practices, early warning systems, and market development strategies. This is particularly important in light of the increasing frequency and intensity of drought-related disasters, as evidenced by recent events and its heavy dependence on rainfall; underscores its significance as a key sector for implementing effective adaptation strategies and resilience-building measures in agriculture through ARAF**3**.

## Ghana

Ghana is vulnerable to increasing aridity, droughts and extreme rainfall events and flooding due to climate change. It is especially vulnerable to floods and droughts in the Northern Savannah belt and to storm surges, landslides, earthquakes, pest infestations and wildfires in its coastal region. Between 1991 and 2011 Ghana had seven major food crises, with the most recent occurring between 2010-2011. The floods of 2010 affected more than 265,000 people in the three northern regions, with nearly 100,000 requiring assistance to restore their livelihoods. Additionally, these floods were preceded by a period of drought that destroyed most food crops. In 2010, Ghana experienced its worst drought since independence, affecting more than two million households and resulting in crop losses estimated at $500 million. The impact on livestock was also significant, with the loss of over one million head of cattle.

## Ethiopia

### Smallholder farmers in Ethiopia are highly vulnerable to the impacts of climate change, which are exacerbating existing food insecurity**4**. The 2016 El Niño event, coupled with a prolonged drought and subsequent ENSO-induced droughts, have had devastating effects on crop production and the livelihoods of smallholder farmers**5**. In the eastern parts of Ethiopia, drought following El Niño forced 95% to 100% of crop failure**6**. Crop failures in the central highlands were estimated at 70%, while the western regions experienced a 30-40% loss due to El Niño events**7**. The 2016 El Niño event had severe droughts and consequent economy-wide impacts, causing a 50% reduction of maize production in the central highlands, with an estimated loss of over one million quintals in Oromia, Somali and Tigray regions**8**. Droughts have also led to substantial declines in livestock populations across Ethiopia, with estimates suggesting that droughts have increased the frequency and intensity of droughts, floods and storms in Ethiopia, with crop production expected to decline by up to 25%, by 2050**9**. Pastoralists and agro-pastoralists are particularly vulnerable, as their livelihoods are highly dependent on rainfall.

### Floods, droughts, storms surge, landslides, earthquakes, pest infestations and wildfires:
Temperatures in Ghana have risen by approximately 0.5°C (an average of 0.03–0.21°C per decade). The number of very hot days has increased by over 40%, with hot nights (maximum temperature increasing by 20% per year).

- Ghana's agriculture is vulnerable to climate change. General crop failure is projected to occur approximately once every five years in the northern region due to delayed or diminished rainfall.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE B

## Current adaptation practices across ARAF II investment geographies

14. Smallerholder farmers are gradually adapting their practices to deal with the challenges and impacts of climate change, leveraging government, NGO and private sector support, as well as their own knowledge systems. The principal adaptation strategies employed by smallerholder farmers in Africa fall within the following 4 categories: crop variety selection; irrigation; water and soil management; insurance schemes; migration and culture; agriculture and weather services.

15. Crop diversification is the most commonly used adaptation strategy. Other crop management adaptation strategies include the utilization of drought-tolerant varieties, changing planting dates, planting early maturity crops, planting improved varieties, intercropping, and using cover crops. The main challenges to these practices are the occurrence of droughts, changes in precipitation levels, rise in average temperatures, and threats to freshwater resources. In addition, there is a lack of access to irrigation infrastructure and water management facilities. As previously mentioned, irrigation coverage in Africa is extremely low; smallerholder farmers in Africa are relying on hand-dug wells or shallow boreholes for their irrigation needs. While the use of drip irrigation systems with established irrigation schemes, the technology can sometimes be inadequate or go unrepaid for long periods of time. Other adaptation strategies include: new fertilization practices for crops, protecting soils, mixed farming (crop-livestock), and agroforestry. These are all aimed at improving soil fertility and water retention, better soil management practices; providing early warning and meteorological forecasts and related information; and improving the efficiency of irrigation systems by reducing evaporation losses through mulching or covering a larger amount of water and the planting of native vegetation cover. Lastly, crop insurance is a vital adaptation strategy for smallholder farmers.

Specialization methods employed in communities in Senegal and Ethiopia include:

16. Serengi[1], utilization of climate information systems for precise planning and decision-making, including seasonal, ten-day, and daily forecasts[2]. Crop diversification and selection involve the adoption of drought-tolerant and short-cycle crop varieties[3]. Soil management techniques such as organic fertilizers, mulching, and composting are

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 22

B

employment to improve soil fertility and moisture retention. Water management strategies include the use of stone bunds and improved irrigation practices. Agroforestry and silvopastoral systems are implemented through intercropping and assisted natural regeneration to enhance biodiversity and mitigate temperature impacts. Livelihood diversification is promoted by encouraging farmers to grow a variety of crops, including climate-resilient species. Additionally, the government's support for climate index insurance by providing a 50% subsidy "aids farmers in managing risks associated with climate change." These measures are aimed at enhancing the resilience of Senegalese farmers, augmenting agricultural productivity, and mitigating environmental degradation.

### Ethiopia: 17th

These include terracing for water and soil conservation, utilized by 36.7% of farmers, "as well as adjustments in planting dates," favored by 50.3% of farmers, to align with shifting rainfall patterns. Additionally, 49% of farmers have adopted the use of organic fertilizers such as manure and compost, while 28% have used fertilizer to bolster yields. Other strategies include such as income diversification, livestock diversification, participation in value chains, and crop diversification. Farmers are also encouraged to adopt climate-resilient maize crop varieties, cultivating drought-tolerant crops like Ensete1, and tree planting are also prevalent. Implementations across various agro-ecological zones in Ethiopia, these strategies are influenced by factors including age, family size, education level, and access to extension services.

### Morocco: 20th

However, the rate of farmers' adoption of these strategies is limited. An analysis of 65 peer-reviewed research papers on farmers’ adaptation strategies to climate change written between 2001 and 2020 (with over 40% published in 2019) found that only 38% of the studies focused on Morocco, while 70% of Moroccan crops only feature within 2.5% of studies, irrigation schemes in 13.6%, livelihood diversification in 13.6%, the use of improved seeds and varieties in 4.9%, and insurance schemes in 3.1%. Some specific cases showing the limitation of adaptation practices include:

- The modernization of irrigation systems and investment in on-farm equipment in Morocco between 2008 and 2018 led to a 5.5x increase in areas under drip irrigation, resulting in a 92% increase in agricultural value during the period.
- Barley stocks close to 37% of the variability of Morocco's total output over the medium term.

### Egypt: 24th

Since 2010, the Egyptian government has provided support to facilitate change by building and managing infrastructure projects. The government also encourages farmers to adopt climate-resilient crops through donors to implement adaptation measures in the Nile Delta, such as developing more heat- and salinity-resistant (salt-tolerant) crops, improving water management practices, and promoting the use of drip irrigation systems with friendly practices. # GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 23

## Factors constraining farmers' adoption of adaptation strategies

19. Farmers' adoption of adaptation strategies is constrained by several factors including education and access to information, access to agricultural extension services, access to credit and markets[15]. Policy recommendations include: increasing the availability of climate risk insurance; providing financial incentives for farmers to adopt climate-resilient practices; enhancing surveillance efforts; facilitating access and affordability to improved varieties; better education and training for farmers; quicker and more affordable channels for dissemination information to farmers[16]. These are aligned with the Fund's investment strategy and the services that the companies ARAF seeks to invest in would provide to farmers.

## The proposed solution: AgriA Resilient Agriculture Fund (ARAF II)

20. ARAF II is a proposal US$513M Impact venture capital fund focused on providing adaptation finance to support the resilience of smallholder farmers across 6 African countries and improve food security. ARAF II continues to engage with Kenya, Tanzania, Ethiopia, and Senegal in hopes of their potential inclusion amongst the Fund's target countries[17]. The proposed fund will be structured as a limited liability company that would provide bundled solutions of comprehensive and/or compelling products and services to farmers in a way that induces change. ARAF II is expected to leverage its experience with ARAF I, which has successfully demonstrated the commercial viability of the sector by investing in, developing and scaling sustainable climate-resilient agricultural practices[18]. The Fund will continue ARAF I's successful investment strategy and plans to invest along the same 3 themes: aggregator platforms, digital platforms and innovative financial solutions.

## Aggregator Platforms: ARAF II seeks to continue to invest in opportunities that are focused on any combination of the following themes and strategies: yield optimization, premium pricing, regenerative agriculture, efficient energy enabling practices, and/or value chain development. These investments will be leveraged by processors and/or distributors, who need to secure a substantial quantity and/or specific quality of production, and as such are compelled to offer farmers comprehensive support along the value chain.

### Climate risk vulnerability

The climate crisis makes smallholder farmers across Africa extremely susceptible to the expected climate hazards of increased temperatures, increased incidences of drought, increased incidences of flooding, crop diseases, and pests. As a result, these farmers will need to make necessary amendments to their farming practices; however, most of these solutions are out of their reach. Early yield optimization is one such example where smallholder farmers can benefit from climate-resilient technology; they may also not be aware of the benefits of certain inputs, including climate resistant seed varieties. If they do, they may be unable to afford them. SHFs also have little to no access to infrastructure – both in terms of mechanisms for transporting their produce and storage facilities – which makes it difficult for them to manage water resources. Finally, SHFs typically transact through multiple intermediaries rather than directly with buyers.

## B

### Adaptation strategies and expected results:
Investing in aggregator platforms is expected to generate the following adaptation benefits for SHFs:

- Improved crop yields and, in turn, improved and/or resistant crop varieties that are more suited to the changing climatic conditions of a region.
- Increased adoption of climate-smart agricultural practices.
- Increased access to climate information and advisory services to promote good soil and water management practices.
- Increased market connectivity and direct linkages to drive profitability of smallholder farmers in responding to climate change.
- Improved soil quality and fertility (e.g., soil biodiversity and soil moisture), enabling resilience to insects, pests, and diseases as well as reducing the incidence of associated pollution.
- Improved pricing for SHFs creating an opportunity for increased income and potentially, profitability, given the interaction between increased yields and increased pricing.
- Increased market access and direct market substitution.

### Adaptation impact being targeted:
Direct and indirect impact on smallholder farmers and their families through the provision of climate resilient agricultural technologies that improve their ability to protect and improve their yields, as well as the opportunity to earn higher prices through direct market connection, ultimately driving greater resilience.

## 2. Digital Platforms: AMAP intends to invest in enterprises that utilize technology solutions to efficiently provide comprehensive end-to-end supply chain services; or precision and/or predictive information to smallholder farmers to support their production. These entities typically have widespread impact since they can quickly acquire traction in various regions, and can be scaled up through the use of digital platforms, including e-commerce, electronic trading marketplaces, mechanization platforms, farmer advisory services, data and analytics, IoT, robotics and automation.

### Climate risk vulnerability:
Smallholder farmers' dependency on and vulnerability to weather makes them extremely susceptible to the impacts of climate change, such as increased temperatures, increased incidences of drought, increased incidences of flooding, unpredictable rain fall patterns and changing plant cycles. To adapt to these emerging scenarios, SHFs's need for access to information and advisory services is critical; however, many of these solutions are out of their reach because SHFs do not have access to adequate information or expertise.

### A study conducted on smallholder farmers in Ghana indicated that farmers had adopted the use of digital agriculture technologies / tools (SATs), specifically radio, television and mobile phones, to access climate adaptation information. These include weather forecasts, crop calendars, market prices, agronomic practices, CarbonNutrients® Energy smart practices, and InstitutionMarket smart practices. Furthermore, farmers typically access these services through a combination of direct service providers (DAS) and agriculture extension officers. Despite the adoption of digital technologies by SHFs, there are significant challenges in terms of digital literacy rates, inadequate support from DAS providers and agriculture extension officers. These are the very challenges that digital platforms are working to address by providing SHFs with peerless connections to the production cycle through their use of the latest technology.

### Adaptation strategies and expected results:
Investing in digital platforms is expected to generate the following adaptation benefits for SHFs:

- Prescriptive use of water and land resources

- Optimized use of inputs, resulting in lower use of fertilizer and pesticides and reduced pollution
- Facilitates access to credit
- Improved connectivity across the value chain for greater efficiency, just-in-time availability of inputs and credit
- Direct market connection leads to improved income and profitability for SHFs
- Improved transparency and traceability across the value chain

Adaptation impact being targeted:
Direct and indirect impact on vulnerable smallholder farmers and their families through the provision of climate resilient inputs and knowledge that improve their ability to protect and improve their yields, as well as the opportunity to achieve higher prices through direct market connection, ultimately driving greater resilience.

## Innovative Financial Solutions
ARIF II intends to seek in invest companies that provide farmers with the structure to afford their products and services that they need to improve their long-term productivity, whether through input credit or insurance. ARIF II will also seek innovative approaches to help farmers make the necessary investments into their production, which would otherwise be out of reach for them. These investments could include inputs such as seeds, fertilizers, and irrigation systems, but also cover the impact of climate shocks; some of the products included embedded insurance and weather-indexed insurance.

Climate risk vulnerability: SHFs have limited financial capacity to afford climate resilient seeds, which may have to be repurchased every season; other quality input, equipment, or technology that is needed for their production. As a result, they are unable to invest in these inputs to afford their products and services leave SHFs vulnerable to climate risks associated with reduced precipitation and water scarcity, drought and flood. Their ability to afford these inputs is also limited by the fact that they may not be able to access credit or insurance during a crisis event; but they could also receive no assistance (beyond governmental support) to cover the loss and support their ability to continue to invest in and protect their production capacity.

Adaptation strategies and expected results:
Investing in innovative financial solutions is expected to generate the following adaptation benefits for SHFs:

- Increased investments in long-term productivity capacity given access to financing for SHFs and/or farming cooperatives
- Maintain yields during unfavorable weather conditions due to resistant crop varieties and irrigation systems that are more resilient to climate change
- Reduced vulnerability of SHFs to climate risks associated with reduced precipitation, water scarcity, drought and flood
- Ongoing investments into farm productivity creating increased resilience

How ARIF II will support SHFs: Direct investment in farmers and SME acquisition of, or access to, cold storage equipment, including solar-powered models

Adaptation impact being targeted:
Direct and indirect impact on vulnerable smallholder farmers and their families through the provision of financing strategies that provide them with the structure to afford inputs that improve their ability to invest in, and protect, their productive capacity, enabling the opportunity for compounding returns on investment over the long-term.

## CLIMATE FINANCE CONTEXT

Estimates indicate that African countries will require at least USD2.67T to implement their respective Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs), which have been prepared by 90% of African countries in response to the Paris


---
**Page 30**
---

B

Agreements which detail country mitigation and adaptation priorities aimed at addressing climate change. This translates to US$278 billion required annually in the decade between 2020 and 2030. African governments have committed US$46 per annum, representing 10% of the requirement, leaving a climate financing need funding for Africa of US$259 billion. The majority of this is needed to support mitigation efforts, which are subject to large existing debt and limited fiscal capabilities. According to Climate Policy Initiative, Africa's climate finance needs 'must largely come from international public sources and domestic and international private actors.'

Africa’s climate finance needs are concentrated towards mitigation, which accounts for 60%123, however it is recognized that adaptation is also critical. Adaptation accounted for US$5 billion in the decade between 2020 and 2030, with agriculture being the largest sector at 48%, followed by water at 17%, infrastructure and buildings at 12% and preparedness at 10%.124

Average annual climate flows of US$6.5 billion (over 2015 and 2020), severely short of the climate financing needs identified above. Mitigation accounts for 39% of the inflows, with adaptation accounting for 12%, and dual-purposes accounting for 49%. Investment flows were dominated by public sector participation in climate finance lags other regions in the world, including developing regions, which have participated ranging from 37% for South Asia to 49% for Latin America and the Caribbean. Highlighting the urgent need of private sector participation in climate finance, the report125 seeks a framework towards leveraging FCF first cost capital to crowd-in private sector investors. According to CPI analysis, regional financing gaps are US$60 billion for Sub-Saharan Africa receiving 12% of its financing needs; West Africa receiving 21%, and North Africa receiving 35%.126

Financing instruments used include loans (low-cost project debt) and project-level investment (project debt), grants, project-level equity, and blended finance. The majority of the sources are public sector (80% of total) and private sector. Loans had the largest concentration, and accounted for 55% of the instruments utilized, and grants accounted for 39%.127 In adaptation financing, grants accounted for 45%, low-cost loans accounted for 30%, and equity accounted for 25%. The report highlights that blended finance is a key instrument in climate change funding. Adaptation and AfDfA, the areas of AfDA’s intervention attract minimal equity inflow, which is necessary for developing countries to access private sector capital. Grants are more appropriate than loans as they provide sustainable financial and impact performance compared to grants, which are better applied towards deskilling early-stage projects or supporting non-commercially viable initiatives.

## ARAF: ITS COMPLEMENTARY TO EXISTED PROJECTS

### 25. ARAF seeks to replicate the strategy implemented by AFRE1, investing in sustainable agribusiness models that provide access to credit and insurance for smallholder farmers to improve smaller farmer resilience and adaptation to climate change.
- By investing in agri-businesses to improve SHF resilience we aim to enhance smaller farmer productivity, income and livelihoods, as well as strengthen the resilience of the entire agriculture value chain. ARAF's investments are aligned with the private sector's interest in developing commercially viable businesses, and there was demand from public and private investors for this investment thesis.
- Farmers' organizations, cooperatives, and other smallholder farmer groups have been strengthened by public companies to strengthen the climate resilience of farmers. As of the same date, ARAF's portfolio companies had reached 10 million farmers in Africa.

- ARAF's investment into innovative financial solutions is also aligned with the World Bank's strategy of increasing access to finance for smallholder farmers. The African Climate Risk Insurance Facility (ACRIF), which will provide concessional financing to African countries to drive higher climate adaptation, has been established by the World Bank and the African Development Bank. ## B

### Equity capital: Climate adaptation and agriculture financing has low equity participation, as a result small and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs) are often unable to access the finance needed to improve their climate resilience, upgrade infrastructure and drive long-term growth. Loans and grants account for more than 75% of the financials available123; for example the Africa Rural Climate Adaptation Finance Mechanism (ARCAFIM) grant established by International Finance Corporation (IFC) in partnership with the African Development Bank Group (AfDB), Green Climate Fund (GCF). ARCAFIM's mandate, similar to ARAF II, is focused on improving the adaptive capacity of smallholder farmers and rural communities through the planting of organic orchards and promoting sustainable agricultural practices to foster sustainable development, build resilience and support climate mitigation (FP022).

ARAF II seeks to diversify the investment instruments available to agribusinesses thereby reducing the gap in capital intensity between large-scale commercial farms and smaller scale family-owned enterprises and smallholder companies, thereby improving their ability to secure additional capital. ARAF II's strategy aligns well with some of GCF's current in-country projects, including defending the vulnerable Nile Delta against coastal flooding damage.

o ARAF II's resilience strategy also aligns with other GCF funded public sector projects, as provided below: ARAF intends to continue strengthening its engagement with public sector stakeholders towards ensuring alignment:

a. Defending the vulnerable Nile Delta against coastal flooding damage to improve the resilience of rural communities (FP035)

b. Supporting communities through the planting of organ orchards and promoting sustainable agricultural practices to foster sustainable development, build resilience and support climate mitigation (FP022).

c. Promoting sustainable irrigation to improve the climate resilience of subsistence oasis farming and larger-scale date and olive agriculture within the Boudour Valley (FP042)

d. Supporting farmers in the Salas Plain, in the Fès-Meknès region of Morocco, adapt to water scarcity brought on by climate change (FP036).

### Technical Assistance: ARAF II is also funding a technical assistance facility (the 'TAF'), which aims to further support its equity investments through grants intended to enhance impact potential and support sustainable business growth and development. Based on our experience and track record in ARAF I, ARAF II is well positioned to provide the necessary technical assistance to help smallholder farmers improve their access to inputs leading to the agricultural sector and consequently, smallholder farmers.

### Barriers:

#### Lack of access to climate adaptation capital and practices leaves SFFs most vulnerable to the impacts of climate change: Smallholders, a group often overlooked, face some of the world’s most severe challenges. They are disproportionately affected by climate change in Africa, where 250 million people are malnourished children, and the majority of those living in absolute poverty globally. Despite their struggles, they play an essential role in food production, contributing to global food security through sustainable land-use practices, conserving natural resources, and buffering against the worst impacts of climate change[17]. Across Africa, smallholder farmers are responsible for 80% of agricultural production, with women and youth making up a significant portion of the labor force and producing the majority of food in the region. Unfortunately, their livelihoods are under constant threat from floods, droughts, rising temperatures, sea level rise, aridity, yield loss, and other climate-related impacts[18]. Despite this critical role, smallholder farmers’ support adaptation have been sluggish and inadequate. The UN’s latest Adaptation Gap Report (2022) highlights a glaring gap in funding for adaptation, with only 5% of development finance for developing nations falling drastically short, estimated at 5-10 times lower than what is required[19].

# Lack of information on climate risks, inputs, resilient agri-practices, and financing: Smallholder farmers face challenges in improving their lives due to limited access to quality farming inputs, modern agricultural knowledge, market information, and affordable financial services that match farming sales cycles. Their low income levels make it difficult for them to afford the necessary inputs and services needed to help smallholder farmers from adopting innovative solutions, as they are hesitant to experiment with new (to them) and untested solutions. Smallholder farmers in Sub-Saharan Africa face significant challenges accessing insurance because farmers lack downside protection like insurance. Due to a combination of supply and demand factors, smallholder farmers in Sub-Saharan Africa have historically faced significant barriers in accessing risk-management options.

# 3AFA's extensive experience working with farmers has provided valuable insights into the challenges they encounter in this regard. Our observations indicate that most smallholder farmers are not adequately informed about climate risks or how to mitigate them. This lack of information is reflected by the low levels of claims payouts. Moreover, the fund’s experience has shown that farmers often lack the financial literacy necessary to engage with complex financial products. Additionally, the costs associated with insurance products can be prohibitive for smallholder farmers, who are already struggling to meet their basic needs. The amount of money they may account for does not reflect the actual losses incurred by individual farmers. These observations are further supported by a recent study conducted in Ethiopia that found that only 10% of farmers were aware of insurance products and services available to them. This lack of awareness is compounded by the fact that 47% of the farmers in our portfolio were unaware of insurance altogether. Among the 53% who were aware of insurance, 62% reported not purchasing it due to high premiums, while another 18% cited the lack of financial constraints as a barrier to purchasing insurance.

# Lack of access to formal commodity markets, forcing farmers to rely on exploitative intermediaries and middle markets to sell their crops: Supply chains for agriculture in sub-Saharan Africa are fragmented. In analyzing the supply chain from farm gate to market, we found that smallholder farmers often sold their crops three times before they reached the farmer, moving from national importers to regional distributors to “agents” who then sold to local retailers. This means that farmers received less than 20% of the 30-50 percent markup over import prices across major agricultural inputs, with about one-third to one-half of that captured as margin by the distributors and retailers in the chain. On the other hand, smallholder farmers, across all regions, capture only a fraction of the value added at each stage of the supply chain. Exploitative intermediaries for crop sales, resulting in minimal returns, less than 5% of the final sale price! Lack of knowledge about appropriate crops, coupled with limited market proximity, fosters crop adoption.

# For example, in Ghana, smallholder farmers are often unaware that they can obtain better prices from their selling agents, agro-dealers, aggregators, and food processors alike. Smallholder farmers particularly contend with inadequate market access and unfair pricing practices. Furthermore, the absence of legislation and regulation to protect them from exploitation by intermediaries is a major barrier to smallholder financial institutions.

# In Ethiopia, as few as 10 percent of farmers have market access in some areas because

## Limited availability of public and private financing available to support growth of early-stage agribusinesses: Globally, current climate adaptation costs surpass available international public finance for adaptation by at least 2 to 3 times. By 2030, anticipated adaptation costs for key sectors like agriculture, forestry, and other land use (AFOLU) are estimated to be US$1 trillion annually, while current international public finance for adaptation stands at approximately US$50 billion annually. The private sector is also under pressure to invest in climate change adaptation due to the increasing demand for climate finance despite their critical impact on food security, gender dynamics, biodiversity, and water conservation.

## Macroeconomic factors, including forex risks, affect capital availability and agribusiness operations and operations. Macroeconomic factors, particularly forex risks, significantly influence the capital availability and operational costs of agribusinesses, impacting access to credit, input supply, market services, and overall profitability for agribusinesses. The susceptibility to forex risks is heightened by the exposure of many agribusinesses in Africa to foreign currencies as a result of import dependency, which impacts both the competitiveness of agricultural exports and the cost of imported inputs, impacting the financial health of the agricultural sector.

2. ARIF II intends to focus on countries with supportive policy and regulatory environments, allowing the fund to leverage existing policies and regulations to support climate change adaptation and resilience-building efforts.

### Assumption 5: ARAF will be able to reach smallholder farmers, particularly women, who face barriers to accessing climate-resilient practices, finance, and training.

#### 47. ARAF's Theory of Change recognizes the importance of women in agriculture and that the disproportionate vulnerability of women to climate change arises from a complex interplay of social, economic, and cultural factors. Women are often responsible for food production and care work within their households and communities. In Africa, women farmers represent 80% of those time engaged in agricultural activities.175 Female smallholders are often perceived as more reliable suppliers of food than men because they have a greater understanding of local conditions and farming techniques. These farmers face four critical constraints in agriculture: including limited access to markets due to social norms around women's mobility and time, low timely due to the additional burden of care work outside their farms, lack of access to finance, and insufficient knowledge and skills to produce high-quality crops tailored financing as well as a clear credit; and insufficient technical knowledge to enable high-productivity.

#### 48. These constraints lead to increased vulnerability for women and are compounded by a lack of access to crucial information on climate change and its impacts on agriculture. Women farmers in Africa face additional challenges because their productivity compared to male smallholder farmers. Women-owned agribusinesses in Africa encompass a diverse range of scale, structure, and ownership, categorized into four groups: micro-enterprises, small enterprises, medium-sized enterprises for export, small, medium, and large enterprises for domestic markets, and large ag-enterprise owners. However, occupational and crop segregation often negates many female entrepreneurs to low-value crops such as vegetables, fruits, and flowers. In addition to these economic constraints, women face similar contextual challenges across the African continent, influenced by social and gender norms, including lack of access to land, credit, and extension services, poor infrastructure development, and as well as legal and regulatory barriers176. This underscores the need for targeted interventions to address these structural constraints and empower women in agribusiness value chains.177 ARAF is it anticipated to be built with these concepts in mind and focus on innovative pipeline companies that support farmers to overcome these barriers.

## Portfolio Composition

### Aggregator Platforms

**Figure 2: ARAF portfolio breakdown**

- **Company A**: A horticulture company that promotes regenerative agri practices and connects its out-growers in Kenya with local and export markets. The company enables farmers to become less vulnerable to climate change by increasing crop productivity, decreasing post-harvest losses, improving income, and the production of high-quality produce. It also provides technical support for agricultural crops through the provision of inputs, training, and offtake.

# Digital Platforms

Information, communication, and technology (ICT)-driven businesses leverage digital platforms to link farmers directly with input suppliers and off-takers to bypass exploitative intermediaries.

They also push information like farming knowledge, weather, and market prices to farmers, and connect them with buyers.

- **Company C:** A Ghana-based agtech company that leverages its technology platform to provide smart solutions for the supply chain of agricultural products. It connects farmers directly with markets; this achieves it through its multi-stakeholder platform, which is an end-to-end solution that enables the digitization of the supply chain from farmer to off-takers.

- **Company D:** A Kenyan company that buys fruits and vegetables from farmers and distributes the goods to the local formal and informal distribution channels in Tanzania. The company has developed a digital platform for its operations, which helps it streamline and optimize its supply chain and distribution logistics leading to increased income (up to 20%) and decreased post-harvest losses (less than 1%) by smallholder farmers.

## Innovative Financial Solutions

These companies provide access to formal financial services like affordable savings accounts, microloans, credit, etc., enabling farmers to purchase improved inputs or other productive assets to diversify their incomes, increasing their livelihoods and their resilience against negative impacts of climate change.

- **Company E:** The company specializes in streamlining the design, distribution, and operations of parametric insurance products for smallholder farmers. It provides innovative solutions like weather-related insurance coverage. Their aim is to support farmers who are significantly affected by weather variability by simplifying insurance access and increasing access to crucial coverage.

- **Company F:** The company leverages in the direct sale, financing, and distribution sales support for solar-powered water pumps, primarily targeting farmers in East and West Africa. These water pumps enable irrigation of crops during dry seasons, provide water for livestock, and other horticulture crops, by providing access to water. Additionally, they enable the expansion of cultivated land, support crop growth during the dry season, and enhance the productivity of livestock.

### 62. ARAF's Climate Resilience Measurement tracks the absorptive and adaptive capacities of smallerholder farmers to climate change, as well as their exposure to risk.

#### Post-investment, ARAF uses a Climate Readiness Toolkit survey: the development of which was funded by GCF and carried out by Decision Specific for ARAF, to understand farmers' adaptive and absorptive capacity. The toolkit is designed to be used in conjunction with the 7-question survey above. It looks at the types of farming practices implemented, their access to financial products that mitigate production risks (i.e., crop insurance and credit), and access to climate smart innovations like alternative water sources and drought-resistant seeds. This survey is conducted within a year after investment and then repeated every three years. The toolkit also provides a comparable set of farmers as regular intervals for as long as the company is part of ARAF's portfolio. This data collection helps ARAF understand changes in climate resilience over time.

#### Framework Development: For ARAF 1, we developed a comprehensive climate resilience framework in collaboration with GIZ and the University of Oxford. The framework evaluates three core aspects of resilience: the ability to absorb shocks, the ability to adapt, and access to enabling factors.

Below are the metrics considered and what the farmers are asked about:

- **Ability to Absorb Shocks**: This dimension focuses on a farmer's capacity to withstand and recover from climate-related shocks. Evaluations include such as the percentage living below the relative poverty line (i.e., reliant on farm income), access to financial resources for emergencies and crop insurance, likelihood of receiving warning and shocks, expected ability to recover, and expected coping mechanisms.

- **Ability to Adapt**: This dimension assesses farmers' knowledge and application of sustainable farming practices. The Farmers are asked about their application of intercropping, crop rotation, water harvesting methods, organic waste management conversion to manure, reduced or no tillage, soil cover top cover methods, and resilient livelihood mixes.

- **Access to Enablers**: This dimension considers access to systems and resources that enhance farmers' resilience. The farmers are asked about their access to credit, wastage and storage facilities, market access, extension services, non-rain-dependent irrigation, and improved seeds.

Surveyed farmers are categorized on a 4-point resilience spectrum—highly vulnerable, emerging, and resilient. Highly vulnerable farmers have not adopted any all-climate smart practices and have advanced tools to manage shocks; emerging farmers have greater capacities and some application of climate-smart practices but do not yet have the capacity to absorb or adapt to extreme climatic resilient practices, and have limited mitigation abilities; and highly farmers are unaware, have no access to climate resilience practices, and are unlikely to withstand weather shocks.

## B

### Climate Resilience Impact

The interventions by ARAF's portfolio companies have notably strengthened farmers' climate resilience. Our impact surveys show that the 45% of the farmers assessed were resilient, up from a baseline of 38%. This increase indicates a higher ability to cope with climate hazards due to access to quality inputs (e.g., seeds, fertilizers, and tools), improved irrigation systems (e.g., pumps and mechanization), and premium markets. These elements, provided as a bundled product for farmers, collectively enhance farmer productivity, income, savings, reinvestment into land, and income diversification.

Based on the follow-up surveys carried out in 2022, the percentage of farmers reporting an improved way of farming rose to 87%, a significant increase from 77% in 2021 (compared to 81% in 2020). Similarly, the percentage of farmers reporting increased yields rose to 63%, up from 54% in 2021 and 49% in 2020. Conversely, the percentage of farmers reporting first-time access decreased to 67%, down from 77% in 2021 (compared to 83% in 2020). The percentage of farmers reporting improved quality of life declined to 59%, down from 68% in 2021, driven by the continued drought in Kenya and higher inflation, which impacted the quality of life of farmers.

Despite these challenges, the climate resilience of farmers has improved over the current investment period. The percentage of farmers classified as resilient increased to 43%, up from 38% in 2020 and 43% in 2021. Conversely, the percentage of farmers classified as risky declined from 16% in 2019 and 21% in 2020.

### ARAF's Role

ARAIF has created a robust foundation for climate resilience among smallholder farmers in Africa. This is reflected by the increasing number of farmers' abilities to absorb shocks, adapt to changing conditions, and access critical enablers. We believe this comprehensive approach demonstrates our commitment to building resilient communities through sustainable agriculture.

#### Farmer Well-Being Outcomes

ARAIF, again, has partnered with 60 Decibels to measure farmer well-being using its "Lean Data" methodology. 60 Decibels helps ARAF and its investors understand the funds' impact on farmers' livelihoods by collecting data from a representative sample of farmers. The resilience data collected by 60 Decibels supports investors in understanding the changes the enterprise is enabling in farmers' crop/vegetable production and the significance of these changes to their livelihoods. As indicated, ARAF has completed 12 rounds of surveys with over 35,000 farmers since inception, conducted within 100 days of disbursing funds to the company. These results are compared to the 60 Decibels Agriculture Index (DAI) and other relevant indices. The data is analyzed by ARAF's team in Kenya to use the results to identify technical assistance funding opportunities for investors. The survey is repeated, when appropriate, every six to twelve months or as needed to understand the track record of performance and see impact deepening over time.

#### Gender ESG Compliance

Additionally, the fund has an ESG and Climate officer who works with ARAF companies to strengthen ESG compliance. Managing environmental, social, and corporate governance is critical in ensuring that ARAF's portfolio companies are aligned with best practices for investors, broader stakeholders, and the environment. ARAF, through pre-investment assessment and post-investment monitoring, ensures alignment of its portfolio companies' ESG performance against ESG requirements as stipulated by the IFC performance standards and other internationally accepted standards such as ILO and ISO 26000. Some of the key areas that are monitored include the implementation of ARAF's ESG action plans, the number of accidents and incidents reported by the company, number of legal and regulatory compliance and number of people displaced and/or resettled. ARAF systematically collects data on these indicators to monitor progress against its ESG targets, providing companies for their potential to promote gender-focused development pre-investment, requiring a Gender Action Plan (GAP) as part of the investment agreement. The fund also uses this information and data to pilot initiatives addressing gender disparities, with a focus on empowering women in climate resilience actions and mitigating the risk of household gender-based conflicts.

## Output 2: Companies expand and improve their offering better addressing the needs of SHFs (increased access to climate information, affordable financing, climate smart inputs, training, and formal markets)

67. ARAF's investments hope to enable portfolio companies to grow and strengthen their business models, including through increased access to climate information, affordable financing, climate smart inputs, training, and formal markets. Through formal and informal feedback loops, companies should be better able to understand new and evolving customer needs and adjust accordingly. Feedback loops from ARAF's farmer surveys provide baseline and periodic updates on the adoption of climate-smart practices in the portfolio companies' value chains. Information about whether farmers know and use certain climate smart agricultural practices, and, e.g., whether farmers have access to affordable financing, are collected through these feedback loops. The data also allows companies to assess storage and market access. Together with employee feedback, this data allows companies to pilot new products and services that meet the needs of their customers. For example, ARAF's farmer surveys in 2019 and 2020 have also informed ARAF's TA (Technical Assistance) projects. Including undertaking out-grower irrigation pilots; developing account management tools and a remote sensing platform; and performing leadership and farmer training workshops. The feedback from these pilot projects has helped companies to better serve their customers and engage farmers better. The impact of these TA projects is continually reported by the portfolio companies, which also contributes to the knowledge base building that the fund aims for.

## Output 3: Companies grow and increase their sustainability (KPIs: revenue/EBITDA; units sold; capital leveraged)

68. As companies grow, they are able to sell more products and generate more revenue. Early-stage business gradually move from start-ups with a proof of concept and market traction, to much more sophisticated businesses that can scale up their operations. This is achieved through the engagement and support of many stakeholders (i.e., government, board members, farmers, banks, local and international suppliers, etc.). In addition, companies are able to generate higher margins as they move from a cost structure dominated by the Marginal Cost of goods sold, and net income). Successfully managing this level of complexity ensures that companies are moving towards commercial viability, positioned to serve farmers in a sustainable and long-lasting manner.

## Output 4: Companies enhance female participation and inclusion in the agricultural value chain

69. ARAF conducts impact surveys to assess the wellbeing of farmers and their resilience to climate change. Through these surveys, ARAF also collects data on the gender balance within companies' management teams and female farmers. Upon identifying differences, ARAF advises its portfolio companies to collaborate with consultants and installers to develop and implement strategies for increasing female participation in the agricultural value chain. ARAF works with companies to create safe and equitable workplaces that can attract and retain talented female employees and leaders. Additionally, ARAF provides post-investment support with a gender lens, deploying the TA team to provide training on gender equality and inclusion. ARAF monitors the gender composition of employees within its portfolio companies, including managerial and non-managerial positions. This is done in order to ensure that companies are meeting their gender diversity and inclusion goals that align with current gaps and commercial priorities of the business. ARAF intends to continue and enhance this work throughout the lifespan of the subsequent fund.

# Outcomes & Co-benefits

1. ARAF II strategically works with companies that empower smallholder farmers by providing bundled solutions that address multiple challenges along the agricultural value chain. These solutions encompass access to inputs, credit, and market information, among other services.
2. ARAF-backed companies aim to sustainably enhance the quality and quantity of smallholder farmers' production, bypassing middlemen to ensure a larger percentage of product value directly benefits the farmers, fostering greater resilience in their livelihoods. This approach leads to improved food security, increased incomes, reduced poverty, sustainable land use, heightened food security, and gender-balanced growth.
3. ARAF II's portfolio includes companies that are strategically positioned at the intersection of climate change adaptation, climate resilience, ensuring a wide array of measurable outputs and outcomes for true climate-resilient growth.

# With partnership from GCF

1. ARAF I set out to demonstrate that agricultural value chains can become climate resilient by capitalizing businesses that build climate resilience for smallholder farmers, while also supporting the overall development of these enterprises.
2. Through its investments in 30 companies across Africa and Asia, ARAF has achieved its investing and impact goals; driving growth in businesses that are instilling adaptation practices in smallholder farmers. These companies have introduced new technologies, improved access to inputs, information on climate-smart agriculture, and ultimately their productivity and income.
3. As a result, these businesses have been able to attract additional private capital and have inspired similar business models across the continent.

# ARAF II aims to build upon the successes of ARAF I by identifying, investing in, and scaling opportunities that enhance climate resilience for smallholder farmers across Africa. It seeks to expand the geographic scope of impact and scale up its approach to include Asia as well.

## Outcome 1: Improved farmer access to markets and productive resources

AGAP II also seeks to provide targeted Technical Assistance trainings that specifically emphasize gender inclusivity, aiming to address the specific needs and barriers faced by female farmers and employees in agribusinesses. The program will work with investees to identify and implement solutions. Given that women constitute nearly half of total smallholder farmers**, we anticipate the TAP will support initiatives to promote workplace equity within portfolio companies, such as increasing the number of female sales representatives, providing equal access to training opportunities for men and women, improving access and utilization by women and girls, including targeting locations where women gather and creating lighter products for easier handling. The program will also seek to provide targeted Technical Assistance trainings that can serve as a potential means to adopt agricultural technologies that have the potential to boost rural economy and enhance the food security***.

### Outcome 2: Increased farmer resilience and adaptive capacity

76. ARAP's farmer-level impact evidence demonstrates Acumen’s position that bundled solutions are effective in enhancing farmers’ productivity, income, and resilience. The program has provided a foundation for ARAP II to build upon by continuing to invest in agribusinesses that support vulnerable smallholder farmers in developing markets who are threatened by climate change and other shocks.

77. The ARAP team recognizes that enhancing farmers' adoption of climate adaptive inputs and tools is insufficient; the broader goal is to ensure sustained improvement in smallholder farming practices that are environmentally and economically sustainable. To achieve this, ARAP seeks to continue using its proprietary pre-investment due diligence process to identify investees with a strong commitment to environmental stewardship, training, creating a mutually beneficial scenario for farmers, companies, and the environment. The incorporation of sustainability criteria into the investment decision-making process will ensure that investments meet production supply and standards, improve soil quality, enhance water efficiency, and promote ecosystem diversity. ARAP aims to provide investees with access to climate-smart agricultural inputs and advice, enabling farmers to manage the impacts of changing weather directly***. The alignment of incentives for agribusinesses underscores their commitment to the sustainability and productivity of their supply chains.

### Outcome 2: Enhanced farmer income, livelihoods, and productivity

78. ARAP expects that through access to an integrated package of improved inputs, finance, training, and markets, farmers will not only increase yields and decrease yield volatility but also experience elevated incomes and reduced income volatility throughout the seasons. This outcome is anticipated to enhance both the security and

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 47

B

smoothening of smallholder farmer access to finance year-round, diminishing uncertainty and empowering households to plan their spending and consumption more effectively. Additionally, ARAP seeks to explore investments in companies offering financial products and services tailored to assist farmers in safeguarding their assets against climate-related shocks, such as droughts, floods, and innovative credit models. Over time, the predictability of income is envisaged to aid farmers in planning, fostering a culture of savings and assets that can mitigate the impact of abrupt shocks such as climate-related hazards [17].

Moreover, ARAP's engagement with agricultural finance companies further demonstrates the significance of providing both liquidity and capital, highlighting the role of incremental asset accumulation as a pathway out of poverty. By facilitating access to credit for smallholder farmers, ARAP aims to unlock new markets, open new markets, and offer technical assistance. ARAP aims to reduce year-on-year variability in net farm incomes induced by climate-related shocks, reducing financial stress faced by farmers, ultimately promoting a more resilient and sustainable agricultural ecosystem.

Reducing poverty in Africa is the world's supreme development challenge, and growing the agricultural sector is key to achieving a transformational impact. A country's economic, environmental, and social well-being are increasingly interdependent. The agricultural sector has the potential to deliver high-impact development returns such as increasing rural incomes, boosting food security, making cheap energy available, and providing access to affordable health care. Innovative business models such as climate smart agriculture [18] ARAP strives to bolster climate resilience among African smallholders by investing in businesses that provide climate-smart agricultural inputs, services, and supporting other ecosystem services that build livelihoods. These vertical investments, including farmer-based intermediaries, market-integrated production companies, and value chain optimizers, link farmers to higher-value markets and increase their income. ARAP's investment strategy is designed to be replicable and scalable, enabling smallholder farmers to cultivate an ecosystem capable of sustaining and expanding more inclusive, equitable value chains.

Outcome 4: Agribusinesses demonstrate scale and viability of innovative models built towards climate resilience

1. ARAP aims to replicate or, on a larger scale and across a wider investment region, the investment strategy and lessons learned from ARAP. ARAP's portfolio companies are growing and increasing revenue following ARAP's initial investment. Notably, three ARAP I portfolio companies expanded into new countries and regions, validating the replicability of their business models [19]. The success of these companies in scaling up the impact of ARAP's investment activities underscores the proof that investing in commercially viable agribusinesses has been a successful strategy for climate resilience. This is evidenced by the rapid market penetration of ARAP investments, marking a pivotal step in fostering sustainability and growth for these enterprises. According to a McKinsey study, there is a need for investments to help demonstrate the commercial viability of the businesses across an expanding range of markets [20]. Therefore, ARAP's investment strategy should begin by prioritizing select countries and value chains where most resources, including personnel, investment capital, and partnerships, are concentrated.
