## OUTCOMES & COMPONENTS

- **Component 1 (C1)**: Improve access to markets and productive resources.
- **Component 2 (C2)**: Increase resilience of vulnerable communities.

### OUTPUTS
- Output 1: Improved farmer access to markets and productive resources.
- Output 2: Increased resilience of vulnerable communities.

### ACTIVITIES
- Activity 1.1: Improve access to markets and productive resources.
- Activity 1.2: Increase resilience of vulnerable communities.

### BARRIERS
- Barrier 1: Lack of awareness among farmers about the benefits of climate-smart practices.
- Barrier 2: Limited access to credit for smallholder farmers.
- Barrier 3: Lack of infrastructure for storage and transportation of agricultural products.
- Barrier 4: Inadequate knowledge on how to use climate-smart technologies.

### ASSUMPTIONS
- Assumption 1: Farmers will adopt climate-smart practices once they are aware of the benefits.
- Assumption 2: Access to credit will improve as more farmers become aware of its availability and benefits.
- Assumption 3: Infrastructure for storage and transportation will be improved through government investment.

## 8.3 Project/programme description (max. 2500 words, approximately 5 pages)

### 8.3.1 Acumen Resident Agriculture Fund II

Acumen Resident Agriculture Fund II is a climate adaptation fund sponsored by Acumen that focuses on improving the climate resilience of smallholder farmers in Africa and, in turn strengthening the climate resilience of the agricultural value chain while promoting food security by improving yields and efficiency across the value chain.

The fund’s goal is to improve livelihoods of these smallholder farmers and reach 20M+ beneficiaries. The fund will support a portfolio of investments that address the root causes of their yield and income and provide diversification and protection for family income. The fund’s goal is to improve the livelihoods of these smallholder farmers and reach 20M+ beneficiaries.

### 8.3.2 ARAF II

ARAF was designed using insights from Acumen’s decades-long experience in investing in smallholder agriculture, including lessons learned from its previous fund, which included reducing behavior change in smallholder farmers, encouraging sustainable adoption of improved inputs and farming techniques, and the importance of commercial finance to scale up these innovations. ARAF II builds on this experience by addressing the gaps outlined above points. Accordingly, platform businesses offering multiple services are better able to gain traction and scale. Furthermore, platform businesses that provide insurance products to smallholder farmers mitigate financial risk variability. This demonstrable thesis forms the foundation of ARAF’s guiding principles, investment strategy and the core of how we expect to achieve our investment mandate.

### 8.3.3 Key farmers needed for ARAF II

As part of ARAF’s platform-based investment thesis, ARAF has identified the following key farmer needs that support increased productivity, income, and reduced input uncertainty:

- Access to quality inputs – improve productivity
- Access to infrastructure (e.g., land preparation) and quality inputs for greater productivity and protect the investment into their farming in case of crop failure
- Access to infrastructure (irrigation and mechanization) – drive increased productivity and efficiency
- Access to extension services and training – improve yield, reduce waste, improve climate resilience; and
- Access to insurance products – mitigate financial risk variability

### 8.3.4 ARAF II’s investment strategy

ARAF II has been conceptualized and designed to extend the gains made through ARAF I by identifying, investing in, and scaling investment opportunities that bring climate resilience to smallholder farmers while expanding the geographic footprint of its investments. The goal is to identify and invest in a portfolio of platforms whose climate resilience benefits could be achieved by supporting smallholder farmers with the tools, information, resources and linkages required to increase productivity and income, protect income and diversify sources of income, leading to increased profitability for these farmers.

ARAF II’s investment strategy will focus on identifying and investing in platform businesses that provide bundled solutions and/or products to farmers as the most effective and efficient approach to engaging farmers and adopting climate-resilient practices. ARAF II is expected to invest across a range of platforms that represent some of the most compelling opportunities and innovations within various segments, including bundled production support services (e.g., inputs, training, extension), value chain solutions (e.g., aggregation, processing, logistics), solar-powered solutions and parametric insurance. ARAF’s intention to expand its geographic focus represents a desire to capture additional economically strong, climate-vulnerable markets on the continent.

90. ARAF II, similar to ARAF I, intends to invest across three strategic themes that represent the market innovation opportunities aimed at providing bundled solutions to smallholder farmers, namely:

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 52

## Aggregator Platforms: Agri-processors bypass intermediaries to procure directly from farmers, addressing two or more key farmer access needs such as improved inputs or affordable credit bundled with services such as extension and training. These platforms are often owned by the same companies that will be aggregators.

### Digital Solutions: Information, communication, and technology (ICT) agri-businesses, leverage digital platforms to link farmers directly with input suppliers and off-takers to bypass exploitative intermediaries. They also push information like farming knowledge, weather, and market prices to farmers, and connect them with financial services providers.

#### Innovative Financial Solution: These companies provide access to formal financial services like affordable credit, insurance, or savings accounts. This allows smallholder farmers to purchase inputs such as seeds or other productive assets to diversify their incomes, increasing their livelihoods and their resilience against negative impacts of climate change.

**91. AARF has a strong well-rounded team with input investing, business strategy and operations, ESG and climate expertise. The on-ground presence reinforces the Fund’s ability to source deals, deploy capital, effectively manage portfolio companies with an emphasis on value creation and underlying impact, and efficiently monitor and report financial performance. This is particularly important in the context of AARF’s focus on agribusinesses in the region, which provides the requisite local knowledge and network for fund management and has greatly contributed to AARF’s success over time as the Fund continues to build a strong track record with entities involved in strengthening the AARF brand by building and maintaining relationships with LPs, co-investment partners, government agencies contributing to the wider ecosystem development by attending conferences for knowledge sharing and pipeline building.**

## Output 1.1 - ARAF II FUNDRAISING: BUILD ON EXISTING PIPELINE, SCREEN NEW TARGET MARKETS

### Activity 1.1.1 – ARAF II FUNDRAISING: BUILD ON EXISTING PIPELINE, SCREEN NEW TARGET MARKETS

92. **ARAF II is the US$120MM impact investment fund that intends to invest in fast growing agribusinesses with climate adaptation offerings (products and/or services) that support smallholder farmers to become more resilient to climate change.** The fund will be structured as a limited partnership, with the Green Climate Fund as the lead investor used to fund gender-focused initiatives, farmer training, business development and impact assessment and monitoring for projects in Kenya, Tanzania, Ethiopia and Senegal. ARAF II will also focus on digital platforms and innovative financial solutions, which are aligned with the following GCF result areas as set out in the theory of change.

93. ARAF II intends to fundraise based on commitments from private institutional investors, development finance institutions, and it is hoped that the Green Climate Fund will anchor the fund, with US$60MM in first loss equity capital, as well as provide US$8MM in grants in support of the TAF. The first loss nature of the GCF’s investment can act as a downside protection for more return-focused private capital investors and catalytic participation in ARAF II.

94. ARAF II intends to make equity and quasi-equity investments between US$50K to US$MM into 18-20 companies across Africa, with a focus on the following countries: Kenya (East Africa), Tanzania (East Africa) and North Africa (Egypt and Morocco). **ARAF II continues to engage with Kenya, Tanzania, Ethiopia, and Senegal in hopes of their potential inclusion amongst the future Fund’s target countries.** With a diversified portfolio, constructed such that no single investment accounts for more than 10% of the fund size, and no one company accounts for more than the lesser of 10% of the fund size and US$2MM.

95. As a result of established relationships and network from Fund I, as well as an on the ground presence, ARAF is continuing to explore new investment opportunities. Accordingly, ARAF II already has a pipeline of 30 priority investments across the 3 investment rounds with preliminary discussions and engagements ongoing.

## ACTIVITY 1.2: INVEST IN LOCAL AGROBUSINESSES ACROSS THREE STRATEGIC THEMES: AGGREGATOR PLATFORMS, DIGITAL PLATFORMS AND INNOVATIVE FINANCIAL SOLUTIONS

### AAFT II
Intends to focus on investing in, and scaling, early-to early growth stage agribusinesses with initial ticket sizes ranging from US$50K to US$8MM with the ability to follow-on capped at a total of US$12MM. The moderately larger size allows for more flexibility in terms of investment timing and structure.

### 100. AGGREGATOR PLATFORMS
These companies typically have commercial imperatives to provide end-to-end solutions across the production cycle, from inputs to offtake, for example, the company may be utilizing produce from smaller farmers for input in its processing activities or they may be exporting to international buyers. The size of these platforms and their ability to manage a large volume of working with farmers, these companies have demonstrable impact on farmers' productivity and wellbeing.

#### Pipeline:
- **Fertilizer agro-processing:** Primarily processes that incorporate inputs directly from smallholder farmers to their businesses, which can manage either the quality of the raw materials utilized for manufacturing or inventory sold into either the domestic or international markets.
- **Soil testing and soil management services:** These are innovative approaches including pay-as-you-go shared service models, precision agriculture, incorporating as example soil diagnostics to provide exactly what the soil requires in the short and long term towards sustainably increasing yields/acre.

Example of the resilience benefits from companies in this category include:
- Increased productivity by facilitating transportation and storage infrastructure, which provides farmers with access to quality inputs and direct market linkages.
- Protection of natural resources, including land and water resources, and encourage agroforestry to address soil erosion.
- Access to financing and credit solutions, which boost productivity by improving soil quality and making farmers less dependent on the timing and quantity of rain.
- Investments in processing infrastructure for value addition, resulting in guaranteed outputs, reduction of post-harvest losses, and increased income.

#### Storage infrastructure and cold chains preserve and/or extend the shelf life of produce, limiting post-harvest waste. This allows farmers to store their produce longer before selling it on the market or to sell all of their produce during harvest season and can manage when and how much produce they sell, enabling them to better plan for future production.

- For a large proportion of smallholder farmers in Africa, post-harvest losses have been attributed to approximately 30–40% of produce lost. Access to cold storage facilities among smallholders is critical to reducing post-harvest losses and achieving higher yields, as well as improving the quality of produce, enhancing rural livelihoods and increasing farmer incomes.

powered cold storage services to smallholder farmers in Kenya. A study of smallholder farmers using Sokofresh cold storage services indicated that approximately 77% of the smallholder farmers experienced increases in income due to access to Sokofresh services.

### Small-scale producers face challenges in accessing lucrative market opportunities serving domestic or export value chains. The difficulty in accessing market opportunities is due to factors such as high transportation costs, lack of infrastructure, and limited access to finance. These barriers make it difficult for smallholder farmers to meet quality requirements. Aggregator business models can enable smallholder farmers to navigate these barriers.

### Kentaste is one of the top manufacturers of coconut products in the East African region. The company has established a network of cold storage facilities across Kenya, Uganda, and Tanzania, with plans to expand its reach across the East-African region. Kentaste sources coconuts from more than 2,000 smallholder farmers through its supply chain. The company provides various services to smallholder farmers that includes credit, training, and access to seedlings.

### Kentaste usually buys coconuts at a premium price compared to the market price of coconut. This is because Kentaste has control over production, prevailing market prices, and a premium for organic certified coconuts. Due to Kentaste's support to farmers and its aggregator sourcing strategy, some smallholder farmers supplying coconuts to Kentaste have been able to increase their income by up to 100%.

### Digital platforms: These companies are working to digitize the value chain to provide farmers (including farmers at the farm level) with just-in-time access to inputs and vaccines, information on demand patterns, which then can be used for better decision-making. They also provide market intelligence through an agent network and access to premium markets of various types of buyers including local processors and international traders.

### Flexibility: Companies that are utilizing technology to serve and connect value chain participants more efficiently. Enabling data and intelligence companies, applying new technologies and data to provide precision and predictive analysis and business intelligence solutions.

The resilience benefits brought on by these companies include:

# Impact

* Increased price transparency and access to demand information, which helps farmers price their crops better and ensures that they do not leave money on the table in transactions.
* Improved market access and efficiencies, which eliminates the long chain of intermediaries, allowing farmers to capture more value from their produce. This has led to increased income for smallholder framers through providing them additional capital to reinvest in their productivity and/or to protect themselves and their families from future emergencies.

## AgriCentra

* AgriCentra, a Tunisian AgTech company, has developed two digital platforms that enable smallholder framers to access markets and financial products. 108 AgriCentra developed AgroTrade, a digital platform that connects smallholder framers with buyers and traders in the agricultural sector. This platform enables farmers to sell their produce at market prices and receive payment within three days of selling their produce. AgriCentra also developed AgriPay, a digital platform that enables smallholder framers to access financial products such as loans, insurance, and savings accounts.
* AgriCentra has been able to increase the income of the smallholder framers on its platform by 30 per cent on average since it launched in 2018. The company has also helped farmers to build their credit scores and access financial products.

## Moome

* Moome, an AgTech company based in Tunisia, is positively transforming the Tunisian dairy market through digitization of farm operations. Moome provides smallholder framers with digital tools that enable them to monitor various aspects of their farm operations such as animal reproduction, milk production, feed consumption etc.202 The data collected by these tools can be used for decision-making and to access financial products.
* A survey of 278 farmers using the Moome digital platform indicated that at least 72% of the farmers reported an increase in productivity. The survey also indicated that at least 60% of the farmers realized a reduction in their costs.

## Innovative financial solutions: Companies providing access to financial products to farmers, including digital transactions, savings, and access to affordable credit that enables SHFs purchase required products and services. This service offering may be stand-alone services or part of wider services offered by companies. Some digital financial services include:

### Digital Platforms
- Increased ability and willingness of farmers to invest in their farmlands, particularly upfront investments in land preparation and purchasing quality seeds, thereby increasing farmer productivity and income;
- Improved access to inputs such as fertilizers, seeds, and tools;
- Protection of farmer investments, securing ability to participate in future farming cycles.

Digital platforms are transforming the agriculture sector by making it more productive, efficient, sustainable and climate resilient. Digital platforms provide farmers with access to credit, advisory services, market linkages, inputs and crop insurance. They also provide farmers with access to agricultural weather and climate information. For instance, DigFarm, a digital platform, uses mobile phones to enable smaller farmers to access credit, advisory services, market linkages, inputs and crop insurance. A survey of 100 farmers in Kenya conducted by Mercy Corps found that 75% of the farmers who used DigFarm services indicated that approximately 90% of users agreed that DigFarm had improved their productivity by enabling them to make better decisions about when to plant or harvest crops, while 75% of the farmers acknowledged that they had become more climate resilient due to using DigFarm services and products. The survey also found that 83% of respondents agreed that DigFarm provided a good source for basic needs and increased knowledge in using modern farming techniques due to DigFarm services and products.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 58 B

## Sub-activity 1.2.1 - Initial Diligence

104. The Fund Manager intends to obtain information from prospective companies towards evaluating the fit and potential of the opportunity. The Investment Team would typically screen the opportunity against the Fund's investment criteria, seek to understand the company's historical performance, business model and future growth prospects, assess the climate resilience of its operations, identify any significant climate impacts and potential ESG risks. The Fund Manager expects to assess the climate resilience of potential investments using the Agricultural Resilience Investment Screening Tool (ARIS) tool, which was developed by Acumen and commissioned by Winrock International. The tool provides a simple, rapid and robust methodology for investment managers to assess the climate resilience potential of agri-business opportunities.

The Fund Manager aims to utilize ARIS during the transaction screening phase to support the Investment Team's decision to recommend the investment to the Investment Committee. The Fund Manager intends to provide the results of the ARIS tool to the Investment Committee to support its decision-making process.

## Sub-activity 1.2.2 - Preliminary Investment Memo

105. The Fund Manager would prepare an Investment Committee Memo highlighting the findings from the initial due diligence, including historical performance, current business model in the context of the wider industry and macro situation, opportunity set, financial projections, investment structure, agreed conditions, management team, identified risks and mitigation.

## Sub-activity 1.2.3 - Invite IC to Due Diligence

106. The Fund Manager would present the opportunity to the Investment Committee at a meeting providing the IC with the opportunity to delve deeper into the opportunity and ask questions. The IC is expected to be composed of individuals with deep operating expertise, varied and complementary skillset, and well-versed in either the sector or geography of the investment opportunity. If the IC deems that it would like to move forward on this opportunity, it will provide its recommendation to the Fund Manager. If the IC may either approve or reject the investment moving to the next phase. It may also request additional information to enable a more informed decision-making process. In such cases, the Fund Manager and/or the IC will often recommend additional risks for further investigation during the due diligence period.

## Sub-activity 1.2.4 - Full Commercial, Legal, Financial Tax, ESG Due Diligence

B

107. The IC approval is intended to provide the Manager with the ability to proceed to formal due diligence, during which external advisors would typically be engaged to perform due diligence exercises (respect for ESG / Climate and Commercial due diligence, which are intended to be performed in-house). The due diligence process will be conducted by the Fund Manager and its advisors. This document sets out the areas that need to be addressed either in the definitive transaction documents, or prior to signing an agreement with the target company. It is important to note that this document does not set out how to have the climate resilience assessment that was conducted during the initial DD phase validated by Winrock International (or similar third-party provider).

Sub-activity 1.2.5 – Agree terms and complete diligence

108. The Fund Manager aims to discuss the due diligence findings with the target company and to provide the target company with a draft agreement for signature, which will resolve the various risk situations identified by due diligence advisors. Following these discussions, the Fund Manager and its advisors will negotiate terms with the target company based on standard market practices and incorporating the results of the due diligence exercise.

Sub-activity 1.2.6 – Investment documentation and closing

109. Concurrent with the due diligence, the Fund Manager and transaction legal advisor will typically negotiate the terms of the investment agreement(s) and any side letter(s) to adequately reflect previously agreed on investment principles and structure, provide relevant guidelines and standards for consideration in relation to the relationship between the Fund Manager and the Fund Investee company, as well as incorporate the findings of the due diligence exercise. Following the completion of any Conditions Precedent, the Fund Manager will deploy the capital from Limited Partners and subsequently fund the investment to arrive at transaction close.

ACTIVITY 1.3: POST INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT, LEVERAGE CAPITAL AND MANAGE EXITS

### Activity 1.3.1: Post Investment Management

110. ARAF’s investment philosophy is rooted in partnering with growing businesses on their journey to scale. As a result, ARAF seeks to provide companies with continued support over the different phases of their growth and across the breadth of their business operations. Due to the investment stage, ARAF is often the first institutional investor that provides capital to a company. This allows ARAF to work closely with portfolio companies to enhance their governance structure, build the accountability of the management team, as well as provide strategic and operational guidance to fuel growth and expansion. This support strengthens the value proposition for ARAF’s investment in the broader private sector and/or providing and creating opportunities for partnership and engagement with the public sector.

111. ARAF always aims to obtain the right to a board seat or observer board seat, depending on the stage of the company. Board representation is critical as it will be able to add value by providing strategic guidance to portfolio companies towards helping them scale their business and impact. ARAF’s board representative may be a senior executive from the private sector with relevant experience or a dedicated professional with specific expertise or relevant experience. ARAF typically selects its board nominee based on the combination of having worked in the current context in the board’s business direction and plan.

112. ARAF also assigns a Relationship Manager on each portfolio company to complement the ARAF team actively engaging with the portfolio companies, with the standard of monitoring touching point at least once a month, and sometimes more frequently, depending on the stage of the business. The goal is to stay up to date with developments within the company to understand their impact on financial performance, provide

## Activity 1.3.2: LEVERAGE CAPITAL AND MANAGE EXITS

### 118. Exit consideration begins during the investment phase with analyses of the most feasible path-to-exit incorporated into the assessment of investment opportunities and choice of investment instruments. However, exit planning starts in earnest once an investment is made. The Fund Manager’s approach to exits is designed to lower a successful exit by partnering with management teams to build attractive businesses that are taping into strong market trends and have the potential for significant growth. Exit strategies include: having a business that can operate additional value; have proper processes and controls in place; are appropriately staffed and developing employees; have a good balance of revenue streams (e.g., recurring revenue, one-time revenue); and managing a broad spectrum of risks ranging from political, macro-economic, regulatory, environmental, market and operational, etc. From the onset of the investment, ARAM also tries to introduce portfolio companies to as many potential exit opportunities as possible so that management teams are aware of what these investors are looking for, and/or can begin to build relationships that might crystallize into future partnership opportunities.

### Sub-activity 1.3.2.1 - Manage exit process by identifying exit potential in the portfolio, narrowing down on options, and executing closure of transaction:

#### Activity 1.4: Monitor Portfolio on Robust Impact Metrics (Including ESG, Gender, Climate)

**120.** At the beginning of the portfolio management phase of ARAF's engagement with a company, just shortly after the transaction close, ARAF typically discusses the key elements of its portfolio management practices to ensure alignment between ARAF and the company on the following: 1) the scope of the monitoring and reporting obligations; Environmental and Social Action Plan (ESG Action Plan); Gender Action Plan ("GAP"); 100-day plan (all of which are rights and obligations that have previously been agreed upon and which are reflected in the legal documentation between ARAF and the company).

**121.** ARAF actively monitors ESG matters in portfolio companies from the point of investment onwards. This monitoring typically includes: 1) monitoring risk areas; 2) assessing progress made on the ESG Action Plan; and, 3) evaluating performance against the targets for the year. ARAF also monitors gender statistics and action plan progress in portfolio companies from the point of investment onward. Such monitoring typically includes assessing the progress made on implementing the GAP prepared by ARAF and agreed upon with each portfolio company at the time of initial investment. This monitoring is conducted during the DD phase. This GAP could be supported by an allocation from the Technical Assistance Facility.

**122.** The ARAF team intends to track standard financial metrics, social performance, ESG metrics and ongoing market trends in the industries and geographies in which ARAF's portfolio companies operate. In each case as reported and audited by portfolio companies, ARAF will review investment-complex financial and impact performance against the target for the year that ARAF would have agreed upon with the company on its objectives and targets for the following year and towards agreeing on a formal plan, which is subsequently approved by the Board.

**123.** With respect to financial reporting, ARAF plans to pay particular attention to top and bottom-line trends, margins, cash balance, cash flow requirements, as well as any other significant changes in financial performance and health of portfolio companies. In addition, ARAF will monitor the progress made by each portfolio company with respect to social performance tracking. ARAF intends to monitor metrics which are agreed upon with each portfolio company's management at the time of initial investment. These typically include units sold as a proxy for lives impacted, number of customers served, and other relevant KPIs.

**124.** ARAF aims to prepare and share quarterly fund reports with limited partners covering portfolio and fund updates which cover financial performance, macro-economic updates, business expansion strategies, company changes, and any other material developments in the portfolio companies' businesses. These reports will highlight the Lean Data summaries discussed above with its limited partners. Additionally, ARAF intends to report on the Fund and investee companies ESG progress and compliance. The Fund also intends to prepare annual reports for its limited partners which will include a summary of the Fund's performance as well as plans to organize an annual LPAC meeting to update its investors on the Fund’s activities and performance.

**125.** To monitor ongoing market trends, ARAF typically enquires about key hires and development priorities and any other material developments in portfolio companies' businesses. ARAF will look through its investment-specific requirements for additional disbursements. In these scenarios, ARAF is likely to aim to monitor progress against established milestones to anticipate future funding needs.

## COMPONENT D: TECHNICAL ASSISTANCE FACILITY (TAF)

To maximize the climate resilience impact of the Equity investments and augment the activities of the ARMF II, Acumen is raising a $12MM Technical Assistance Facility ("TAF") alongside the Fund. The purpose of this Grant will be to provide technical assistance to portfolio companies in order to help them better understand how they can improve their business and operational capabilities. ARAF also intends to use the TAF to ensure that companies can maximize their climate impact while having strong management systems to address environmental, social and governance (ESG) issues.

### Design of the TAF

130. The design of the TAF builds on the experience of the ARMF Fund TAF, which as of December 31, 2023 had deployed more than $2.6MM in grants into projects focused on climate adaptation, conducting impact surveys, and helping companies to better understand their ESG performance. The TAF will be structured around four pillars: 1) Regular fund company priorities based on our close engagement with ARAP I portfolio companies and the broader climate resilience ecosystem; 2) The TAF builds on this experience with a focus on targeted interventions in four key areas: (i) Climate Resilience, (ii) Business Development, (iii) Environmental services & Diversity; (iv) Lean data support and (4) Other (TA Audit Fees Legal Fees/Environmental, social governance (ESG)).

### The TAF Entity

132. The executing entity for the TAF is the Manager: Acumen Capital Partners, LLC. As provided in Section B.4, TAF decisions are made by the Technical Assistance Committee. The Manager is responsible for reviewing, approving, and implementing all TA projects. TA projects are, however, fully executed by the portfolio companies who will have the opportunity to hire third-party technical partners as part of their approved TA applications. Contractors enter short-term (i.e. less than 12 months) contracts with ARAP II portfolio companies with tailored support in areas such as climate resilience, business development, environmental services and consultation, mitigation consultations, smallholder pilots, in person and digital farmer training development, and senior management coaching, etc.

## TAF mandate and scaling

136. Acumen's experience with ARUF Fund I has shaped the scope and scale of the proposed Technical Assistance Facility (TAF). The design reflects key insights gained from close collaboration with portfolio companies of ARUF Fund I, including the following: 1) The TAF is designed to be flexible in terms of funding size and scope. The proposed $120MM TAF represents 10% of the target fund size of $1200MM. This allocation is largely aligned with industry benchmarks for technical assistance facilities. For example, ARUF Fund I's Technical Assistance Facilities constituted approximately 12% of the investment fund amount[16]. Further, a 2014 World Bank policy research report on climate change and financial markets found that technical assistance facilities typically range between 7% and 15% of the investment’s value, while technical assistance facilities themselves vary between 6% and 20% of the total fund size, with an average of approximately 10%-15%. In addition, a proprietary analysis by Acumen of ten funds in Africa found that the average funding-to-fund size ratio of around 11% for Technical Assistance Facilities based on an analysis of ten funds in Africa[17].

#### Technical Assistance experience and learnings from ARAF Fund 1

**137. After implementing ARAF 1’s TAF for the past four years, ARAF has identified learnings that have informed the design of the ARAF II TAF:**
- **Human Capital and Organisational Management:** Early stage companies often lack seasoned senior management and are heavily reliant on good talent. Although provisions are included in the project, the effort required to recruit and retain top talent is often underestimated.
  - ARAF provides additional recruitment support through leveraging TA for consultancy and mentoring services and leverage relationships with existing third parties (e.g., externs, headhunters).
- **Systems and Analyses - Start-ups often need robust systems relating to critical functions such as finance and accounting. ESG, legal, and compliance are also important considerations. The lack of a system often results in a lack of visibility into the company’s operations and the inability to track system overviews. ARAF supports implementing processes with recruitment of the right staff and consultants required to develop the appropriate systems and procedures.
- **Change Management:** Start-ups need to be able to adapt quickly, and this requires a culture that embraces change management practices emphasizing the need for continuous training and delivering critical information to farmers. The TAF aims to support farming training initiatives that lay the foundation for in-house / in-company farmer training business models. ARAF will also provide technical assistance to help companies develop their own training programs, encourage companies to incorporate farmer training at every touch point, develop training manuals, and pilot both virtual and in-person training models.

#### COMPONENT 3: INSIGHTS AND KNOWLEDGE SHARING

**ACTIVITY 3.1: PARTNER WITH CO-INVESTORS AND STAKEHOLDERS TO IMPACT AMPLIFY**

**138. ARAF aims to continue to solidify its position as an ecosystem builder and thought leader by collaborating with co-investors and other stakeholders to share insights that progress the conversations and influence action and deliver impact. The Manager is the Executive Chair for Component 3.**

**Sub-activity 3.1.1: ARAF works with co-investors to increase capital access to target pipeline companies.**

**139. ARAF maintains a unique position within the African agriculture investing sector, given its broad sectoral focus on providing investment opportunities in agribusiness and related sectors. The Manager will adhere to all the relevant policies of Acumen that GCF reviewed in the re-accreditation of Acumen in 2022. # B

Acutem became a GCF Accredited Entity in 2018. Acutem is an AE that has three funding lines: 

* The first line is the program KivaQ Ventures, a venture capital fund focused on creating clean energy access in East Africa and at the end of its investment period, impacted over 150 million low-income individuals and other vulnerable groups.
* The second line is Acutem for Access to Agriculture Fund, a venture capital fund focused on building climate resilience for smallholder farmers in West and Central Africa. At the end of its investment period, it has provided concessional capital to support businesses in the solar home system sector survive the impacts of COVID-19.
* The third line is Acutem for Access to Agriculture Fund, a venture capital fund focused on building climate resilience for smallholder farmers in West and Central Africa. At the end of its investment period, it has provided concessional capital to support businesses in the solar home system sector survive the impacts of COVID-19.

In addition, for over 20 years, Acutem has been directly investing in small and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs) in East Africa, West Africa, Central Asia, South Asia, Sub-Saharan Africa, and Latin America, with the goal of changing the way the world tackles poverty. As of the end of 2023, Acutem had deployed approximately $168 million into 167 companies, impacting over 500 million lives.

## B.S. Justification for GCF funding request (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

### 147. GCF participation in ARIF will be instrumental in catalyzing additional capital into the Fund, particularly private capital.
ARIF was able to leverage GCF investment by 1:5x and the intention is to double this multiple to 3:ix in 2023. The commitment covers up to $48 million of the US$87.88 required by the NDCs. Additionally, private capital providers are committed to providing an additional $69 million of funding for ARIF, with much of the capital coming from outside the region. Moreover, agriculture does not attract a considerable proportion of investment flows, as most of the venture funding in Africa is focused on FinTech rather than AgTech, which can be attributed to the lack of market readiness and limited access to financial services in many countries. The estimated US$748 million gap for agribusinesses. Moreover, despite the crucial need for equity capital to support smallholder farmers, only 0.1% of total climate finance is directed towards this sector annually. The US$58 from private equity and venture capital funds represents approximately 0.7% of the needed $8 billion in annual investment required by 2030.

### 148. In 2020, global climate funding amounted to approximately US$560 billion, out of which only 1.7% (US$10 billion) targeted smallholder farmers.
The 1.7% covers only a small fraction of the climate mitigation and adaptation needs of smallholder farmers in Africa. The GCF has been working with ARIF to attract additional private sector finance towards investing on the continent, particularly in underfunded and climate vulnerable sectors, like agriculture.

The participation of private climate finance as a percentage of total climate finance in Africa lags the global average.

The GCF’s ability to offer first loss capital provides significant diversification for risk-savvy investors as it is essential given the high risk associated with investing in agriculture and the relatively early nature of investing in climate adaptation. ARAF experience and subsequent discussions with impact investors reveal that the fund has a very strong track record, with an average annualized return of 13% and a compound return profile and the high impact potential, enabling ARAF to take on the perceived greater risk at a lower threshold. In discussions with existing investors, many have revealed that they would have been unable to participate in the fund had it not been for the GCF’s first loss capital. The results of these discussions, prospective investors in ARAF are particularly interested in the risk mitigation that the GCF’s first loss capital provides. This is especially important given the fact that the fund has a target return profile on I or 1.3 basis, by proposing that the GFC provides Catalytic First Loss Capital of US$20M as junior equity, to create a more attractive investment proposition.

• Limited Partners require a structure that includes first loss capital for the following reasons:

* Historically, the agricultural sector in Africa has been perceived as high risk. Although ARAF’s progress and performance has largely been positive, the fund is still very early in its lifecycle and would need to record several more years of strong performance before it can be considered a mature investment vehicle and more consistent performance across multiple fund managers and funds is necessary to shift widely held and negative perceptions.
* Additionally, ARAF is a single-sector focused fund, focused on agriculture, competing for DFI investment allocation with sector agnostic funds.

These two factors put ARAF at a disadvantage in attracting investment fund, one that is addressed by the application of first loss capital types, including DFIs.

B.6 Exit strategy (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

## Extent potential is a fundamental component of ARAF's investment strategy and fund structure

### Factors affecting exit strategy

- Analysis and discussions from the onset of evaluating investment opportunities
- Investment Committee discussion
- Monitoring of market conditions and holding period for opportunistic engagements

### The ability to exit is a function of the underlying fundamentals of the business and the attractiveness of the sub-sector into the long run; the company's ability to scale and attract additional investors, whether strategic or financial, who are compelled to buy out existing investors; and the investment instrument utilized. ARAF and Acumen have historically focused on companies that have a strong track record of profitability and growth, which is expected to continue for ARAF’s ability to understand and manage exit considerations well.

### The need to exit guides ARAF's investment activity

- This is focused on supporting high-growth, scalable agricultural enterprises with the potential to provide services to smallholder farmers.
- ARAF's investment team assesses the strategic and commercial merits of investment opportunities to understand their value proposition within the context of opportunities for scaling during the investment holding period. The team considers the company’s revenue potential five years into the future, the opportunity for profitability, the ability to scale operations, the number of farmers served, the number of years to profitability, and CAPEX outlay, amongst other assessments.
- The expectation is that after an initial period of growth, companies will be able to demonstrate a track record of consistent and sustainable financial progress and/or improve their financial profile, which enables them to attract additional external capital from multiple sources. ARAF has historically focused on companies with strong balance sheets raising follow-on rounds; and an additional five portfolio companies currently advancing fundraising rounds.

### Smallerholder farmer affordability of services, solutions, and products is a key determinant of the commercial viability and scalability of ARAF portfolio companies

- Accordingly, during the initial assessment of investment opportunities, ARAF’s investment team assesses whether the company has a viable business model with commercially important to provide the services to farmers for free or on credit with reasonable repayment terms.
- ARAF continuously gathers intelligence from its portfolio companies and their farmers. Farmers are able to pay by learning directly from the farmers about their previous practices and available substitutes to verify the value proposition of the service or product being offered, as well as through ARAF’s annual survey of farmers’ ability to pay to assess the sustainability of the businesses.
- ARAF continuously gathers intelligence from our annual surveys, as well as from our positions on the board (where price increases and/or the customers' ability to pay are discussed). This is done in order to ensure that we have a good understanding of farmer liquidity and willingness to pay towards protecting the financial viability and sustainability of the companies and their business models. ARAF has historically focused on companies with strong balance sheets utilized for ARAF portfolio companies (some of which were piloted using ARAF TAF funding) including:
- **Subsidy models:** Through irrigation as a service where farmers do not have to make upfront CAPEX payments for irrigation, but rather are able to pay as they use it.

**157. ARAF supports investor companies with strategic and operational guidance, as well as access to experts and consultants, all towards ensuring that our investee companies grow to be financially sustainable enterprises.**

**ARAF II is structured in such a way that it can provide capital at any stage of the investment process, even as early as during the investing process. The aim is to expose investee companies to as many capital providers as possible, not only to attract capital from part of the market provider, but also to obtain information about what may be attractive to potential capital and/or underwriting partners that particular investor may become interested in a company.**

**158. ARAF II aims to be structured as a limited partnership with a 10-year term, with the possibility to extend for two consecutive periods of five years each. It expects to invest in fast-growing SMEs with attractive, viable and sustainable business models that are poised to acquire substantial scale in the short to medium term. Exits in portfolio companies will be structured as a combination of exits to strategic investors or to private equity or financial investors. In some cases, ARAF II may structure the investment with a self-liquidating instrument, such that the investee company repays ARAF II after an agreed holding period via a revenue or income stream. Those exit structures are designed for substantial returns during the holding period and are part of due diligence process and are an integral part of due diligence. Given that ARAF II intends to invest in early-stage companies, for equity deals, there is no guarantee that the company will be profitable at the end of its investment term. However, quasi-equity deals oftentimes have a distribution component. Most of the returns are expected at exit or at the end of the investment term.**

D. EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

The section reviews the performance of the project/programme against the investment criteria as set out in the GCF's Initial Framework.

### D.1 Impact potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

As a group, smallholders are among the most disadvantaged and vulnerable in the world. Half of the world’s poorest people live on less than $2 per day, many of them smallholder farmers; and the majority of people living in absolute poverty globally are found on small farms. Smallholders play a key role not only in food security, but also in maintaining biodiversity, providing ecosystem services, and protecting global systems from some of the world’s climate impacts. For example, Costa Rica has a total population of 4.5 million people, yet its agricultural sector employs over one-third of all workers, and its coffee sector's importance to the labor market is further amplified when considering the rural population exclusively. In rural areas where more than half of the nation’s lives, over 70% of people are engaged in agriculture.

Much of the global sector’s potential for increasing productivity and improving livelihoods lies in enhancing income for these households and most families consume what they grow, trade goods for other necessities, and sell their produce to local markets. Smallholder farmers typically earn less than $2 per day (or less than $50 per month) on average.

ARIA was built from learning from Acumen’s experience investing in early-stage businesses that enable smallholders across developing countries to improve their productivity and incomes. ARIA's strategy is based on the underlying belief that catalytic impact can be generated by supporting smallholder farmers with bundling of financial services, access to markets, and other inputs to help them achieve a sustainable income exceeding this investment strategy and proving out its aforementioned investment thesis. As demonstrated in Acumen’s experience, these bundled services are critical for increasing productivity and access to credit. ARIA's portfolio companies have innovated financing models, improving productivity, and impacting the lives of smallholder farmers. ARIA has also been successful in scaling up its model into climate-resilient agriculture, attracting diverse investors and fostering sustainability. ARIA has shown initial success in scaling up its model by investing in 10 companies across five countries. To continue to attract additional private sector capital, ARIA seeks to continue to advance ARIA's track record in investing in agribusiness whose strategy, performance and success require them to go beyond either solely sourcing from or supplying inputs to large-scale commercial farms. ARIA aims to invest in a diverse portfolio of smallholder farmers towards improving their productivity, farming methods, market access or providing greater risk reduction.

ARIA’s investment and impact strategy to enhance the climate adaptation of vulnerable farmers is guided by AGRIFUND’s 2015-2020 Strategic Plan. ARIA aims to invest in companies that are aligned with AGRIFUND's strategy. ARIA targets to lend companies who work with farmers to improve their yields, income and diversity their livelihoods. The portfolio companies will be expected to provide smallholder farmers with access to credit, access to inputs, access to financial products (credit and insurance); access to infrastructure (mechanization and irrigation); access to markets; and access to information technology. ARIA aims to invest in companies that ensure supply chain transparency and access to premium markets. These companies are driven by market forces to ensure that smallholder farmers improve the quality and quantity of their production in a sustainable manner.

**162. The funds intend to impact 4 million people directly and 16 million indirectly. ARIIF aims to focus its investments on rapidly expanding agri-businesses already showing strong potential for adapting to climate change. This investment will provide a comprehensive package of solutions, encompassing farm inputs, training, credit facilities, and market access, a strategy learned from its initial fund.**

**ARAF has seen that the broader the service offering, the higher the farmer resilience score. The integration of these services into one package can increase farmers' income levels, enhance the quality of life for farmers, and ultimately bolster their resilience to climate challenges.**

## STAKEHOLDER NEED FOR IMPACT

### Farmers
For most small-scale farmers, achieving resilience is a major challenge. Many are already vulnerable as these farmers depend on rainfall that has become more erratic in recent years. They also face increasing competition from larger farms that have better access to inputs and markets. Adoption of drought-resistant varieties can help increase yields while reducing water use. Access to credit for purchasing climate-resilient inputs is critical.

### Agribusinesses
In Sub-Saharan African countries, agribusinesses are the largest employers in agriculture, employing around 20 percent of the gross domestic product (GDP). They employ approximately 24 percent in income-generating activities and 35 percent in non-income-generating activities. There is a pressing need to improve both production efficiency and productivity by advancing sustainable agricultural practices.

### Ecosystem
At the ecosystem level, ARIIF aims to catalyze investments that will help farmers adapt to climate change while increasing their resilience in agriculture.

**ARAF IMPACT CREATION**

* Investing in agribusinesses. Full support for agribusinesses to increase their resilience to climate change.
* Providing end-to-end financing, from input supply to storage facilities, transportation and market access to qualify inputs and direct market linkages.
* Investing in infrastructure that will provide resources and agronomy to address soil degradation.
* Accessing public funds to improve mechanization and irrigation systems.
* Investing in processing infrastructure for added value and reduction of post-harvest losses.

**ARAF aims to support approximately 20 early adopters of climate-resilient technologies through investment and technical assistance. These investments will help farmers increase their income, strengthen their business models, enabling them to provide services to other smallholders.
* Investing in agribusinesses that directly engage with producers to improve products, yields, and incomes.
* Investing in agricultural finance and private sector initiatives.

**ARAF is committed to promoting the widespread deployment of climate-smart agriculture technology throughout Africa.**

## D

### Potential for scaling up and replication

164. ARAP II intends to replicate on a larger scale and across a wider investment region, the investment strategy and success of ARAP I, the world’s first climate equity fund focused on climate adaptation.
2. With partnership from GCF, ARAP II will invest in smallholder farmers’ businesses that build climate resilience for smallholder farmers, while also supporting the overall growth of the agricultural sector. 171\. Capital Investment: Acumen is requesting that GCF invest equity capital into a 10 year Fund (plus up to two 1-year extensions, i.e., 12 years maximum). This vehicle and its GCF's anchoring investment aim to enable Acumen to scale up its impact in the climate finance space by addressing the lack of long-term institutional investor risk appetite. The proposed fund structure shall be tiered and includes a Junior and Senior equity layer. The participation of GCF and other catalytic capital providers in the Junior layer is expected to further de-risk Acumen, and attract additional financial resources from institutional investors. This Fund will aim to provide climate protection and enhancement of returns. Despite agriculture's significant contribution to employment and GDP across Sub-Saharan Africa (SSA), it remains underfunded relative to its potential for climate change mitigation and adaptation[10]. According to the Global Impact Investing Network (GIIN)[11], while 61% of impact investors allocate their capital towards environmental causes, only 25% focus on agriculture. Furthermore, despite the fact that climate finance for agroforestry systems is alarmingly low, with only 4% of total global climate finance being allocated to this sector[12], the potential contribution of agroforestry systems to climate change mitigation and adaptation is immense. These systems can sequester a mere 3% of total global climate finance, amounting to an annual average of USD 28.5 billion[13]. Furthermore, only 0.4% of global climate finance is allocated towards the development of agroforestry systems as part of climate change solutions, with an annual average VC investment of USD 4.8 billion[14]. To address the pressing challenge of scaling up climate action in agriculture and agroforestry, it is critical to unlock the potential of these systems by increasing their share of global climate finance.

Climate finance for agricultural systems must escalate significantly, at least sevenfold from current levels, to meet the commitments made under the Paris Agreement. The Global Land Use Coalition (GLU)[15] estimates that USD 20 billion annually according to the Food and Land Use Coalition (FOLU) [16]. Boosting agricultural investment represents a key opportunity for climate finance to make significant progress in meeting the livelihoods of millions of people. By directing resources towards this critical sector, ARAF is able to play a pivotal role in unlocking its potential for sustainable development and positive impact. The first loss capital provided by the Green Climate Fund (GCF) to ARAF has already attracted USD 10 million from institutional investors, making it attractive to other like-minded impact investors who are struck with the risk profile of investing in African agriculture. This is a critical step towards scaling up climate action and unlocking the potential of agroforestry systems in leveraging GCF financing for this purpose. It is anticipated that ARAF's proven track record will further enhance ARAF's ability to attract institutional investors.

## Environmental co-benefits
ARAF aims to prioritize portfolio companies dedicated to promoting climate-smart agricultural practices aimed at conserving natural resources and preventing environmental degradation. Through collaboration with the European Union’s Emission Reductions Scheme for Agriculture and Forestry (ERSAF) that prioritizes environment conservation, Commitment to these ESARS is anticipated to involve adhering to requirements such as those set by the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) and the European Union’s Emissions Trading System (ETS). Furthermore, ARAF shall mandate all portfolio companies to implement water management practices that are environmentally sustainable. These include reducing water use through the adoption of drip irrigation systems, materials, land conversion strategies, reduced or no tillage approaches, and soil testing protocols. This concepted approach is expected to significantly reduce greenhouse gas emissions while simultaneously enhancing carbon sequestration. Regenerative agriculture, with its focus on soil health, resource efficiency, biodiversity, carbon sequestration, and water conservation, is a key component of ARAF’s strategy. It provides benefits such as improved environment and farmer yields. It enhances long-term farmer livelihoods by reducing costs, improving crop yield and quality, and providing resilience against market volatility and extreme weather events[1].

## Economic co-benefits
ARAF it intends to invest in companies aimed at enhancing farmers’ economic well-being through various enhancements expected to significantly improve the well-being of farmer households. In the short term, regular income increases are anticipated as a result of improved productivity and increased farm output over time. Over the medium term, the additional income is anticipated to accumulate, potentially compelling affluence. This affluence is expected to be more pronounced in cases where investments are made by women farmers. In the long term, it is expected that these bolstered livelihoods will become more solidified, resulting in sustained economic growth and improved social well-being for farmers and their families. Currently, ARAF portfolio companies has promised investments in initiatives aimed at enhancing the livelihoods and well-being of farmers. Currently, ARAF portfolio companies have demonstrated a clear connection to income increases. Working with companies within the ARAF portfolio has enabled them to improve productivity by 20%, which has translated into tangible economic benefits, with over 80% of surveyed farmers reporting that their improved productivity has led to increased farm output and income. This increase in productivity is anticipated to have a positive impact on the well-being of farmers, as reported by 85% of respondents. This increase in productivity and income is expected to be sustained through enhanced productivity and income derived from collaborations with companies supported by ARAF[2].

Gender-sensitive development benefit: ARAP's portfolio companies are expected to undergo a thorough evaluation process that includes an assessment of their gender equality policies and strategies. Following this investment, they are expected to be required to formulate a Gender Action Plan. Subsequently, they must report on progress towards achieving the goals outlined in such plans. The ARAP team acknowledges the importance of understanding and addressing differences in farming experiences between male and female farmers. This is particularly relevant given that women often have limited access to resources and are responsible for household water supply, energy provision, and food security, further accentuating their vulnerability. Marginalization from decision-making and access to environmental resources, women's lack of education, and the gender pay gap all contribute to this marginalization. Existing research indicates that women require them to intensify efforts to sustain their households, leaving little room for skill development or education. Women are also more likely than men to be displaced by climate change, seeking refuge during disasters, exacerbating their hardship[1]. In conflict ridden contexts, women endure exacerbated gender inequalities and face additional barriers in accessing resources and services. The ARAP team acknowledges an additional stressor that amplifies their vulnerability[2]. FAO research underscores the pivotal role of gender equality in agriculture and agri-food systems. Closing gender gaps in farm productivity and wage disparities in agri-food system employment could yield significant economic benefits, with estimates suggesting that closing these gaps would increase global food security by 14% and lift 50 million people out of food insecurity[3]. To achieve this, ARAP aims to conduct farmer surveys to analyze

Africa’s population was estimated at 1.4 billion people in 2022 and is projected to reach 2.5 billion by 2050, with an expected Compound Annual Growth Rate (CAGR) of 2.1% annually[8]. Agriculture currently stands as Africa's largest economic sector, employing approximately 60% of the continent’s workforce[9], while rainfed agriculture provides 95% of the continent's food, making anticipated changes to rainfall patterns and temperatures a matter of critical concern for both urban and rural livelihoods. In Africa's climate finance landscape, 49% ($US 14.8 billion) is allocated to mitigation efforts, with 30% ($US 9.2 billion) dedicated to adaptation measures[10]. This distribution contrasts with other global regions, where adaptation typically receives only 75% of the funding compared to mitigation.

Africa's vulnerability to climate change, substantial increases (see -13 and 16%), respectively—are still necessary[11]. Persistent funding gaps undermine Africa’s ability to adapt to climate change. # Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal V3.0 | Page 87

## North Africa (Morocco)

### Needs assessment

Morocco’s National Strategic Adaptation Plan for climate change by 2030 emphasizes key measures for agricultural adaptation, including:

- Supporting the adoption of sustainable agricultural practices to maintain soil quality and quantity.
- Promoting practices that facilitate water infiltration, reduce flooding, and rehabilitate degraded land.
- Protecting water reserves through strict control over irrigation and communication programs.
- Leveraging information and communication technologies for more sustainable agriculture.
- Digging smart irrigation systems to sustain crop yields.
- Facilitating access to financing for small farmers and cooperatives to reduce food losses.
- Improving storage techniques and providing training on post-harvest management.
- Strengthening agricultural insurance programs.
- Preserving agricultural land and urban areas through the creation of green belts.
- Encouraging urban agriculture and developing agri-food chains to enhance resilience.

### Alignment

AGAP’s activities are aligned with the country's climate strategies as outlined above. With the support of the Green Climate Fund tech platform, the Fund is expected to enable the following leverage offerings to smallholder farmers:

- Access to markets, storage facilities, and information, thereby reducing waste and increasing income.
- Align farm operations with the changing seasons.
- Access to credit, affordable credit, and microfinance for small-scale farmers in land and farming activities and diversify their livelihoods.
- Access to assets such as irrigation systems, including water conservation technologies.
- Opportunities to improve yields through agrotechnology and reduce yield losses caused by climate change.
- Extension services: awareness raising, and capacity building on the use of appropriate land uses and income-generating activities.
- Access to insurance products for small-scale farmers in response to climate change.

### East Africa (Kenya)

During the NCSAP process, stakeholders emphasized prioritizing adaptation actions that would have a direct impact. Actions focused on prioritizing actions within the agriculture, livestock, water, environment, infrastructure, and tourism sectors. Actions in agriculture include the following of the agricultural value chain include:

- Promote indigenous knowledge on crops.
- Increase the use of agroforestry to mitigate impacts on the agriculture value chain.

### D.6 Efficiency and effectiveness (max.: 500 words, approximately 1 page)

192. ARAP is conceptualized as a blended finance fund that pools capital from various types of investors, who will be attracted by its risk-adjusted returns. The fund aims to support the development of climate-smart agriculture and resilience of smallholder farmers by investing in ecosystem agribusinesses.
   - As provided by GCF*, development organizations are advised to utilize blended financing structures as a way of maximizing their impact. ARAP is designed with a dual equity structure, which will allow for a wide range of market participants. ARAP expects that this Fund will be supported by a range of investors including DFIs, private sector investors and other public sector entities.

193. ARAP has therefore been structured to incorporate two tiers of equity – a junior equity tier and senior equity tier. A premium is assumed that the GCF invests US$20M in equity into ARAP in the junior equity tier as catalyst for additional investment from private sector investors, including DFIs. The expectation is based on ARAP’s experience where GCF’s investment was leveraged by 5:3x to attract additional private sector capital into the sector. This leverage ratio is expected to be similar and will be used to induce equity exposure to the often volatile and risky agri-sector. Based on ARAP’s performance and track record, it is anticipated that the junior equity tier will be fully subscribed within a year of launch.
   - The fund also proposes that GCF provide grant capital of US$4M for ARAP’s TAF, which ARAP will seek to leverage by 2x towards raising at US$8M Technical Assistance Facility.

194. ARAP I portfolio companies have raised 5:3x ARAP’s investment amount, raising an additional US$60M in equity from private sector investors and DFIs. This demonstrates the potential for ARAP to be a catalyst for other USPs in the fund level, as well as attract additional private sector capital into the sector at the scale of ARAP, thereby increasing the scope and impact of climate-resilient agribusinesses operating locally within countries.

195. ARAP II expects to design and deploy financing instruments into viable business opportunities in a risk-adjusted manner. The fund will provide grants and/or equity investments to smallholder farmers to identify, invest in, and manage in innovative business models driving impact and livelihood improvement for smallholder farmers. ARAP II will establish appropriate capacity processes and internal controls to monitor its investments and report to investors.

196. ARAP’s investment approach is focused on companies that have a wide network of farmers, whether through out-grower schemes or other arrangements. This allows the fund to leverage ARAP’s position in amplifying the fund’s ability to reach farmers. ARAP II seeks directly impact 4MM smallholder farmers and indirectly impact 20MM lives by helping farmers to prepare for, and recover from, climate shocks through practical adaptation measures such as improved water management practices, better soil fertility, consistent access to water, and access to insurance, amongst others. As well as other co-benefits including increased income generation, ARAP II will also seek to build the livelihoods of smallholder farmers by investing in their knowledge and skills. We believe that ARAP II represents a cost-effective opportunity to improve the livelihoods of millions of smallholder farmers and their families, who represent some of the most vulnerable demographics in our population.

## E. LOGICAL FRAMEWORK

This section refers to the project/programme's logical framework in accordance with the GCF's Integrated Results Management Framework to which the programme contributes as a whole, including in respect of any co-financing.

### E.1 Project/Programme Focus

#### A. Mitigation or Adaptation

- ☐ Reduced emissions (mitigation)
- ☒ Increased resilience (adaptation)

#### B. GCF Impact level: Paradigm shift potential (max 600 words, approximately 1–2 pages)

The assessment of impact is meant to help a project/programme monitor and assess how it contributes to the paradigm shift described in section D.2 above by applying three assessment dimensions - scale, replicability, and sustainability.

Accordingly, for each assessment dimension (see the definition per assessment in the accompanying guidance note), describe the current state (baseline) and the potential scenario (target) and the rate of change from the current state (baseline) by using the three-point scale rating (low, medium, high) provided in the guidance note. Also describe how the project/programme will contribute to achieving this scenario (see section D.2 above for details on the assessment dimensions). For each dimension, provide a description of the target state and rate of change. Please refer to Section B.2.a (theory of change).

| Scale | Agriculture currently stands as Africa's largest economic sector, contributing 17.5% to Sub-Saharan Africa's GDP and employing approximately 40% of the workforce. However, the agricultural sector in Sub-Saharan Africa is still in its early stages compared with other sectors, with increased investments needed to mature the sector. The sector also faces a lack of capital and patient capital available to bridge | | | The successful implementation of ARAP II aims to scale up existing business models and increase the number of agriculture cooperatives and agribusinesses that can provide sufficient scale for providing climate-resilient agricultural products. | ARAP II aims to mobilize US$120MM and $125MM for the Technical Assistance Facility |
| | | | | With partnership with the GCF, ARAP II plans to demonstrate that agricultural cooperatives and agribusinesses can provide services for smallholder farmers in East, West and North Africa by investing in smallholder farming. | |

catalyzing millions of dollars in private capital in climate sensitive agriculture. AFRI aims to additionally develop and share key insights and learnings with stakeholders in the industry in order to amplify impact.

Many African companies struggle to access the necessary capital and support for effective scaling and demonstrating replicable, scalable, replicable models, compounded by a lack of awareness. AFRI is dedicated to promoting the development and deployment of relevant technology throughout the continent.

ARAF II aims to demonstrate the effectiveness of targeted TA in building companies’ capacity to absorb that learning, creating a blueprint for other financiers.
ARAF II plans to demonstrate and create financially sustainable enterprises that can be scaled up such that they are sustainable post intervention. The fund expects to implement best in class governance, training and support.

The establishment of sustainable and profitable business models across the agriculture and climate adaptation

ARAF II aims to be established and provide financing ($120M over the investment period of 5 years) for companies within the African sector. Successful implementation of these investments will provide replication both within and outside Africa. Approximately 30% of ARAF’s African businesses are intended to benefit through financial investment and technical assistance to build their capacity to absorb that learning, provide climate adaptation products, services, and training.

Most of ARAF’s investments aim to have a positive impact on the environment in terms of focus creating opportunity employment opportunities which will further develop capital in the sector.

ARAF II plans to create an enabling environment to support the growth of the business sector by promoting inclusive and sustainable business models in Africa specifically, in ARAF's target countries.

institutional, and fiduciary frameworks to provide financing solutions to the sector.

Preparing for successful exits is a core element of ARAF II's strategy. The fund aims to dedicate substantial effort to comprehensively understand each portfolio company's strategies and financial performance in order to establish financially sustainable enterprises attractive to other investors.

ARAF II aims to structure investments to facilitate clear exit paths, medium-term liquidity, and risk mitigation for early-stage agribusinesses. ARAF aims to foster economies of scale, reducing costs and enhancing viability in the long term through these exits. These successful ventures are expected to serve as proof points that demonstrate assumptions that inclusive business models can be economically viable and profitable.

Anticipating similar impact, ARAF II is poised to become a leading knowledge provider in agribusiness. ARAF II aims to strategically convene key stakeholders from the sector where knowledge and insights from its works. This will help to identify common standards and synchronization with country level national strategies. ARAF II also aims to be an attractive option for other funds looking to invest in emerging markets, particularly those that are under building similar models in other markets.

reporting on project indicators, implementation challenges and financial results. Please note that interim and final evaluations are expected to embed an assessment of performance against Key Performance Indicators (KPIs) to determine a rating in a five-point scale rating. Refer to the guidance note for the summary requirements and factor in additional evaluation/assessment activities under this section accordingly.


The following outlines the reporting schedule that ARAF aims to follow for GCF:

* **Fund-to-annual and annual reporting**
    * Interim evaluations
    * Final evaluation (after completion)


**Annual and quarterly reports:**

The ARAF II team plans to report on the ARAF II Program, adhering to the project Funded Activity Agreement and the Accreditation Master Agreement. Our Team has created a framework for Key Performance Indicators (KPIs) to oversee throughout the Project's duration, ensuring alignment with the outcomes outlined in the Funding Proposal.

ARAF typically secures a board or owner assent in the investee companies, a practice that is expected to continue with ARAF II. This area reflects our representatives' expertise on governance issues and facilitates contribution to strategic decision-making, ensuring alignment with the fund's objectives, which, in the case of ARAF II, aims to emphasize climate adaptation. Moreover, The Project Team plans to assign a dedicated relationship manager to each investee, tasked with ensuring thorough understanding of their business model and strategy. This will enable the team to better understand how the investees are engaging with senior management and the board of investors, guaranteeing that their business strategy remains aligned with our climate adaptation objectives. An Annual Business Plan (ABP) is expected to be developed for each investee company in line with ARAF II's KPIs. The ABPs will outline the key performance indicators expected to be held regarding the following year’s objectives and targets, culminating in a formal plan agreed upon with the company's management for adoption.

ARAF II aims to continue to conduct an annual review, which is expected to culminate from the monthly and quarterly reviews scheduled throughout the year. This comprehensive assessment is expected to evaluate the performance of ARAF's portfolio companies on financial, operational, and social, ESG, and impact metrics. It is anticipated that this process will be conducted in collaboration with the Fund’s Investment Team members and the ID to strategize how best to support the portfolio companies going forward. Ultimately, this process may inform strategic decisions regarding the continuation of investments or divestment from certain investees. The review will also provide an opportunity for ARAF II to engage with the industries and geographies where ARAF's portfolio companies operate, as reported and audited by portfolio companies. The Project plans to report financial results, including revenue, expenses, profit/loss, cash flow, and balance sheet information on a quarterly basis. This will be in line with the Fund’s required financial statements, ESG, and impact data. Reporting to GCF is planned to also occur via the API, with reporting requirements established in legal agreements between donors, investors, and investees. The Project Team plans to report on the performance of ARAF II's underlying portfolio investments and make any adjustments deemed necessary to the valuation of each of the investments, including write-downs of any impaired investments. ARAF II plans to also host an annual investor meeting for the investors.
