## Impact reporting:
ARAF also anticipates engaging National Designated Authority (NDA) quarterly and annually. The team plans to share progress reports with NDAs and seeks regular feedback from NDAs on project activities and gather feedback. NDA feedback is expected to help support and inform project activities and potentially influence strategy.

### To track ARAF's progress in achieving its climate adaptation objectives, the ARAF team intends to collaborate with each investee company and the UN Decisive Lean Data (DLD) team to develop a range of proprietary indicators measuring progress at both ARAF and Project/Programme levels. We plan to initiate various Lean Data projects over the fund's lifespan to evaluate improvements in climate resilience among farmers, including enhanced income security, improved access to markets, increased productivity, and reduced vulnerability to extreme weather events. The evaluation process is expected to enable us to form insights at the ARAF level, tracking progress against objectives and providing timely feedback to investee companies where appropriate. This will help ensure that the fund's investment decisions are informed by independent impact experts to verify impact performance.

### ESG Reporting:
Investors in ARAF expect to receive an annual report on the ESG performance of its investee companies. ARAF is also proactively monitoring ESG matters in portfolios, including at the point of investment creation. This monitoring is expected to include: (i) tracking the development of ESG risk areas; (ii) assessing progress made in the ESG Action Plan; and (iii) assessing any changes in the business that could introduce additional ESG risks. The objective of this review is to identify emerging ESG risks, assess their impact on ARAF's investee companies, and develop mitigation strategies where appropriate. This will help ensure that the fund anticipates and can respond to independent expert impact experts to verify impact performance.

## Mid-term evaluation:
- During the mid-term evaluation, significant development results are not expected to have been achieved, given that the portfolio will not have reached full maturity. Consequently, the evaluation is expected to primarily focus on assessing the implementation of the investment strategy. This assessment is intended to include an analysis of the fund's performance against its objectives (e.g., the creation and catalysis of private sector capital, climate resilience scores, farmer well-being metrics, and the total number of people reached, and lives impacted).

## Final evaluation:
For the final evaluations, it is anticipated that the fund will have reached maturity. The end-of-program evaluation of the Fund is expected to adhere to the GCF evaluation policy. An independent evaluation is anticipated to monitor key performance indicators, results, as well as gender and other inclusion targets.

F. RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

### F.1 Risk factors and mitigations measures (max. 3 pages)

#### Selected Risk Factor 1

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | Medium |

**Description**

Please describe the risk to the best of your knowledge at this point in time.

### Execution Challenges & Risks

- **ARAF** expects to invest in a mix of early growth-stage, innovative companies. Given their level of maturity, these companies may face a series of challenges regarding their business model, company operations, and market dynamics. As a result, these companies may fail or may take longer than anticipated to identify financially viable businesses.

- **ARAF** will generally seek minority stakes with strong contractual rights and minority investor protections in consortium with other co-investors. As such, ARAF does not expect to have sole control over the operational activities of its portfolio companies.

### Mitigation Measures

- The risk that the identified risk will be mitigated or managed: Do the mitigation measures lower the probability of risk occurring? If so, to what level?

- **ARAF** expects to conduct due diligence activities prior to investment, consisting of an in-depth analysis of financials, management team, legal and ESG performance. ARAF intends to require mandatory on-site visits for all investments to ensure that the portfolio team can accurately assess high quality investment opportunities and select companies with strong business models, robust governance structures, and compliance with local legislation, as well as those complying with all legal and statutory requirements in the regions in which they operate.

- To further reduce the risk of failure, the Fund will not only provide investment capital and targeted Technical Assistance (TA) to its portfolio companies but also seek to have a Director or observer on each of the Future portfolio companies' Board of Directors, identifying and working with co-investors to connect to local networks of experts and value add partners, and offering specific technical assistance programs.

- In addition to hiring a team of investment and sector experts, the Funds intends to set up a robust post-investment monitoring system. This will be conducted in accordance with the Future Fund's protocols, focusing on high transparency, annual reports, and direct investment oversight by an Investment Committee following international best practices.


#### Selected Risk Factor 2

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | Medium |

**Description**


---
**Page 129**
---

# Regulatory Uncertainty

* **ARAF II aims to invest in innovative companies in the Agricultural / Agribusiness sectors in its target geographies.** Certain value chains and industries tend to attract political attention, leading to unanticipated policy changes, price controls, and new or increased tax rates, all of which can negatively impact the Fund's profitability by creating market distortions and reducing the attractiveness of investments.

* **ARAF II is also exposed to operational and compliance risks stemming from the processes, people, and systems of the Fund’s external service providers or third-party contractors.**


## Mitigation Measure(s)

Please describe how the identified risk will be mitigated or managed. Do the mitigation measures lower the probability of adverse impacts on the Fund?

* **ARAF II seeks to assess regulatory risk in the markets where it operates and will consult with local regulatory agencies to understand their planning processes and potential for changes.** ARAF II intends to also carry out regular regulatory and legislative reviews across its vehicles and markets to ensure potential adverse impacts are mitigated or managed, thereby reducing the probability of adverse impacts on the Fund.

* **When selecting early contractors, the ARAF II team expects to screen the identification information of each individual and/or entity associated with the agreement.** Each search will be reviewed against international sanctions lists, as well as other relevant lists (e.g., terrorist watchlists). The search will also review any adverse financial history (i.e., bankruptcies, borrowings, etc.). Furthermore, to avoid any instances or appearances of impropriety, the ARAF II team aims to ensure that each of its procurement decisions will be based on the following principles:

  * a) Reasonable price: The ARAF II team will not accept bids from individuals and/or entities with adverse financial history (i.e., bankruptcies, borrowings, etc.).

  * b) Transparency and integrity: clearly defined procedures for fairness in internal and external parties with consistent application.

* **Competitive procurement selection: where applicable, standardized evaluation criteria and procedures for impartiality and opportunity for all potential contractors and vendors.**


## Selected Risk Factor 3

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Other | Medium | Medium |

### Description

Please describe the risk to the best of your knowledge at this point in time.


# Exit and Valuation Risks

* **ARAF II intends to invest in companies in markets which have limited options for liquidity events / exits due to lack of developed financial markets, including lack of well-capitalized private equity and venture capital firms.** Many of the countries in which ARAF II seeks to invest exist a level of control on the repatriation of profits from foreign investments.

* **Accounting standards in the countries in which ARAF II seeks to invest do not generally correspond to international accounting standards, and therefore may be subject to significant differences.** These differences are not yet in place in many target countries. Many portfolio investments will generate revenue that is denominated in foreign currency, and changes to exchange rates may affect the value of the Fund’s investments in portfolio companies.

## Mitigation Measure(s)
Please describe how the identified risk will be mitigated or managed. Do the mitigation measures lower the probability of risk occurring? If so, to what level?

- **The ARAF is Investing Teams** expects to maintain ongoing relationships with potential strategic acquirers of portfolios and downstream stage investment firms to help improve exit opportunities for Fund investments.
ARAF intends to carefully monitor investments and utilize regional teams to stay abreast of changing markets and conditions. ARAF will also remain open to utilizing alternative investment structures, such as pre-funding or pre-liquidating instruments with a pre-determined liquidation date to help improve the likelihood of financial exits and offset the unpredictability of markets.

## Selected Risk Factor 4
### Category: Transactional and operational
### Probability: Medium
### Impact: Low

**Description**
Please describe the risk to the best of your knowledge at this point in time.

# Human Rights Risks

- **In Sub-Saharan Africa**, there are an estimated 117 million children working long hours in hazardous conditions pre-pandemic. The situation was exacerbated by the COVID-19 pandemic and humanitarian events, as the majority were on family farms to compensate household loss of income or mitigate movement restrictions. There is therefore a risk that the companies ARAF invests in could be working with smallholder farmers utilizing child labor in their agricultural production activities.

## Mitigation Measure(s)
Please describe how the identified risk will be mitigated or managed. Do the mitigation measures lower the probability of risk occurring? If so, to what level?

- **It is important to note that due to the expected size and nature of ARAF’s investment portfolio**, ARAF and its investee companies may have limitations in the information they can collect on human rights risks or the influence they can exert over their supply chains. ARAF will therefore take a risk-based approach with respect to human rights, to the extent possible.

- **ARAF’s Investing Teams** take a risk-based approach with ARAF. The team intends to engage its portfolio companies that are either operating in, or sourcing from certain geographies under scrutiny (or other countries associated with human rights abuses) to enhance disclosure and transparency of labor conditions within their supply chains.

- **ARAF’s investee companies** shall undergo ESG due diligence prior to investment as well as annual ESG monitoring. The analysis includes desk-based review, direct engagement with key individuals within the company, and a review of publicly available information. This will provide an understanding of the social context and geographic footprint to gain a high level sense of the extent to which the company is aware affirming that they are not operating in or sourcing from countries under scrutiny (or other countries associated with ILO standards on labor). 3) addressing the protection of Human Rights, responsible use of security forces.

## Selected Risk Factor 5

| Category | Probability | Impact |

| Technical and operational | Low | Select |

### Description

Please describe the risk to the best of your knowledge at this point in time.

**Risk of Conflicts in Fragile States**

- Two of the countries of focus for ARIFM II – Nigeria and Uganda have received a score of “Alert” or worse on the fragile states index. This reflects the increased risk of conflict in these countries and their governments’ lack of preparation to deal with the potential conflicts. The index reviews twelve conflict risk indicators including security apparatus, political stability, economic development, education, health, infrastructure, environment, population size and brain drain, state legitimacy, public service, human rights and rule of law, demographic pressures, refugees and IDPs, and natural resources.

- Other countries of focus score between “Warning” and “High Warning” reflecting different levels of preparedness by country.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

Please describe how the identified risk will be mitigated or managed. Do the mitigation measures lower the probability of this risk? If so, to what extent?

At the global level, the ARIFM II team will seek to ensure diversity country-focused to limit significant exposure to any one fragile high countries. Given the contextual nature of each territory, the team expects to carry out country-based risk assessments which will include both formal and informal political economy factors driving security risks in these countries. The ARIFM II team will also seek to engage with investors who have experience investing in newer markets. ARIFM II will seek to partner with co-investors with experience navigating these risks in order to mitigate the exposure of the fund. The ARIFM II team will also work closely with the Asset Allocation Team (AAT) for on-going conduct-on analysis of the macro-risks impacting each of the newer markets. The AAT has access to proprietary data and models that can be used to assess the impact of these risks in a given market.

The teams intends to summarize macro risks for each investment in investment memos and will be shared with the Investment Committee along with strategies to address the risk aligned to the context of the specific investment.

## Currency Depreciation

- A number of the target countries for ARIFM II have been facing runaway depreciation of local currency in recent years, which can impact the dollar value of fund investments and compromise returns. The devaluation of local currencies could also lead to increased costs for ARIFM II as it invests in companies that import parts in their supply chains or capes requirements.
- In 2018, 35% of ARIFM II’s target countries were affected, and 23% is anticipated in Egypt, Ethiopia, and Nigeria respectively. Three of ARIFM II's target geographies, while high single digit depreciation of 6% and 8% is anticipated in Kenya and Uganda.
- If ARIFM II returns are denominated in USD, the fund will attempt to mitigate this risk (to the extent possible).

### Mitigation Measure(s)
Please describe how the identified risk can be mitigated or managed. Do the mitigation measures lower the probability of a loss? If so, to what extent?

- ARIFM II will seek to invest its Force IIa by investing in additional countries beyond those anticipated to face high depreciation of their local currencies. These countries will either face less depreciation or appreciation of their local currencie, namely: Morocco (+1%), Cote d’Ivoire (+2%), Senegal (2%), Uganda (-1%) and Tanzania (-2%).
- Furthermore, ARIFM II will attempt to help its portfolio companies, particularly those heavily dependent on imports for raw materials, by investing in companies that have a significant local component, local currency denominated debt, mitigating the effects of depreciation on the dollar value of the fund.
- Finally, where possible, ARIFM II will endeavor to invest in companies with a significant export component, exporting or limiting the mismatch between local currency revenue and dollar funding.

---

### Category | Probability | Impact

## Inflation

- Some of the African countries that ARIFM II is targeting are experiencing high inflation, mainly on the back of depreciating local currencies and high oil prices. A trend is expected to continue into 2024: Egypt, Ethiopia, Ghana and Nigeria are expected to register inflation rates higher than 20% in 2024.
- High-inflation can substantially reduce buying power, affecting demand and profitability of portfolio companies. High levels of inflation will also likely trigger an increase in interest rates by the Governments of ARIFM II's target countries.

## Mitigation Measures

### Risk of reduced portfolio risk will be mitigated or managed. Do the mitigation measures lower the probability of risk occurring? If so, to what level?

* **ARAF** will attempt to mitigate against the effects of inflation by investing in companies that have limited import exposure and/or access to export opportunities where possible, e.g., sourcing or manufacturing inputs locally, leading to lower cost products.
* Furthermore, even under a high inflation scenario, the average price elasticity of food remains lower in absolute terms than other sectors, thereby mitigating the effects of income loss for ARAF’s portfolio companies relative to other sectors of consumption.

## Selected Risk Factor 8

### Category: Technical and operational
#### Probability: High
#### Impact: Low

**Description:** Describe the risk at the level of your knowledge at this point in time.

---

### Single Sector Exposure

* **ARAF** will invest in smallholder farmer climate resilience solely through the agriculture sector, leading to exposure to several sector level risks that have the potential to impact the entire investment portfolio.
    * Agriculture remains highly vulnerable to weather conditions and water availability. Extreme climatic events (such as droughts or floods) can lead to large-scale crop failures, causing significant losses for both farmers and investors. The African countries targeted by ARAF will also face serious challenges in terms of access to irrigation infrastructure due to the unpredictability of water access (for both irrigated and rain-fed farms), combined with overall water scarcity being a significant challenge.
    * In addition, agricultural commodity prices can be highly unpredictable, as they are influenced by global markets and local factors. Fluctuations in prices can in turn affect revenues and profitability of portfolio companies.

---

### Mitigation Measures

* **ARAF** will attempt to mitigate against its single sector risk by diversifying investments across different agricultural value chains within the Fund’s different target countries (6 in total). ARAF continues to engage with Kenya, Tanzania, Ethiopia, and Senegal in hopes of their potential inclusion amongst the future Fund's target countries.
* **ARAF** will also attempt to restrict its investment mandate to limit exposure to politically dependent value chains (like milk in Kenya, sugar in Tanzania and maize in Zambia).

---

#### Concentration Risk

- **ARAF II will be investing in a limited number of companies, leading to a concentration of risk. A decline in the value of a significant portion of these investments can have a substantial impact on the overall performance of the LP. Additionally, political instability and changes in government can impact business operations, sector growth and profitability.**

#### Mitigation Measures

Please describe how the identified risk will not be mitigated or managed. Do the mitigation measures lower the probability of loss?

- **In addition to a rigorous post-investment deal management process, ARAF II may display its investments in branches based on specific conditions precedent. This phased approach will allow for managing certain company risks to reduce the impact of concentration risk. The LP has also established an investment committee where positive prospects for performance are high and fund manager has a clear line of sight on exit options.**

## Selected Risk Factor 10

### Description

Please describe the risk to the best of your knowledge at this point in time.

#### Fiduciary Compliance (AML / CFT)

- **The geographic areas of focus for ARAF II have an elevated risk for funds being misused for money laundering. This includes jurisdictions such as TIP, ML/TF or any other financial misconduct or crime.**

#### Mitigation Measures

Please describe how the identified risk will not be mitigated or managed. Do the mitigation measures lower the probability of loss?

- **Both ARAF II and the AE have AM/LCFT policies that aim to ensure that ML/TF risks in the processes of fundraising for the LP and deploying future investments are properly identified, monitored, mitigated and reported in order to protect against regulatory bodies and service providers from being misused for ML/TF or any other financial misconduct or crime.**

- **The use of any materials and technology provided by the LP is subject to AEC’s Code of Ethics which is applicable to employees of the LP and its affiliates, as well as any third parties acting on behalf of the LP or our Board or committees. The LP will also have a Grievance Policy which can be found in Annex 1 (and which includes provisions for reporting ARAF II’s Grievances). Further information is available on the website.**

The LP utilizes the LeadersRisk Brights Insights KID Compliance Package software to conduct these searches, reviewing +100 websites for each company in ARAF II’s portfolio. This is a standard practice within ARAF II and ensures that the result is accurate, involving the AE’s Risk and Compliance Manager and General Counsel as needed.**

199. In the course of conducting an environmental and social impact assessment (ESIA), applying AARIF investing principles, and implementing the AARIF investment policy, Acumen will consider relevant environmental policies and procedures including Acumen’s and GCF’s environmental and social policies, and developing the AARIF II Environmental and Social Management System (ESMS) to ensure alignment with the GCF’s environmental and social Category I-2 with mostly environmental and social Category C investments and activities. The executing unit, the AARIF team, stands on leading the project's environmental and social management system.

200. Since ARIF was I rated Category C13, Acumen has been in accredited with GCF and is now able to lead Category B1-2 projects. The AARIF team believes that categorizing this project as 1C can lead to a larger portfolio of investments in the region, which will be more impactful for the most vulnerable communities and the vulnerable agri-communities. Similar large pan-African agricultural funds have rated themselves as Category I- 2B or I-3A. The AARIF team believes that this project is aligned with the GCF’s environmental and social policies of the pipeline, particularly the financial social solutions and digital platform companies, are likely to be key in supporting the transition towards a more sustainable future.

201. Much like AARIF II ARIF II investments will likely be ESG Category C. AARIF II expects to make several investments that fit ESG Category B. Business models may have typical characteristics of Category B projects:

• Land and resettlement issues
• Agroforestry and deforestation issues
• Market access for agricultural products and services
• Informal labor practices for suppliers
• ESG readiness

AARIF incorporate ESG consideration across the various stages of the investment and portfolio management process in the following ways:

Screening: AARIF II intends to screen and conduct regular ESG due diligence on all ARIF II investment opportunities. Initial screening includes ensuring a company complies with the AARIF II exclusion list and desktop research for any known ESG issues.

Initial Due Diligence

203. In Initial Due Diligence, the Deal Team Lead and the ESG Manager will analyze potential ESG risks and financial reporting governance risks based in part on the company’s sector, sub-sector and geography as well as on an initial understanding of the company's operations. Based on these factors, the deal team will rate potential investments using a preliminary rating system known as the Preliminary Private Market Investment Memo (“PMM”), and it will ultimately serve to guide the team as it prepares its formal diligence plan.

Formal Due Diligence

204. Once the initial due diligence, the ESG Manager conducts extensive ESG diligence, focusing on areas highlighted from the initial ESG risk evaluation. The ESG diligence is meant to uncover ESG risks, identify potential risk mitigation strategies and assess the overall impact of these risks on the investment. Based on a combination of formal ESG diligences, the Portfolio Manager rates each of the ESG risks as Low, Medium or High and come to a conclusion on whether the investment remains appropriate given this assessment. The Portfolio Manager also identifies any ESG issues that are material to the investment and assesses potential risk mitigation strategies to mitigate any ESG issues or risks deemed unacceptable. These elements are intended to be presented to the IC in the Investment Memo (“IM”).


---
**Page 136**
---

# Environmental and Social Impact Assessments

If projects have Category B characteristics, a third-party environmental and social expert with local knowledge is engaged to conduct an Environmental and Social Management (ESM) on the company/project with a public version of the ESM shared with GCF and on the ARAIF website prior to investment. ESAs are expected to include:

- In-depth identification of environmental and social risks using the IFC environmental and social performance standards
- Analysis of community risks and potential grievances
- Analysis of land resettlement or displacement risks
- Analysis of indigenous peoples' SEAM concerns
- Analysis of environmental and social management of investment
- Gap analysis of E&S risks and E&S capacity
- Mitigant strategy
- Translation into local language

## Post Investment

In the post-investment stage (post investment) we will work with the investee company to gauge their capacity to monitor and report on the environment and social safeguards. Together with this, the ARAIF portfolio manager will review the ESM annually or as required by the IFC environmental and social performance standards implementation, changes in the environmental and social risks, updates on the status of grievances redressed/outstanding (if any) and ESG action plan update.

## 2017

ARAF intends to focus its investing in early stage small & medium enterprises that benefit smallholder farmers and/or women. The ARAIF portfolio will be limited to projects in emerging markets in projects funded by ARAIF will include software for digital platforms and basic food processing technologies. We will ensure as part of our ESG diligence that the investee company has a robust environmental and social management system in place. The scope of these projects will initially be local (limited to a sub-region within a country) and we would like to support scale-up of these projects into other regions.

## 2018

ARAF will not invest in companies that use or permit the use of hazardous agrochemicals and/or participate in activities that lead to deforestation or negatively impact the environment. ARAIF will invest only in companies that comply with national and local regulations and standards regarding environmental best practices as well as their working conditions.

## The ARAF and Acumen team has developed an Environmental and Social Management to identify, manage, monitor, report on, and respond to environmental and social risks and impacts. The ESMS aligns with both Acumen's ESG Policy for its investments and the IFC ESM standards.

### Scope of E&S assessment

The ARAF and Acumen team seeks to mitigate harm with ARIF II investing activities. Acumen and ARAIF combined their due diligence research, engaged in stakeholder outreach, and utilized industry best practices, tools, metrics, and methodologies to develop a comprehensive assessment of ARAIF's environmental and social risks and impacts.

## Stakeholder engagement

Stakeholder engagement included input into project design, market research, project management, climate, gender, and poverty strategies, assessing ARIF II viability, and ensuring ongoing partnership when the project transitions from Acumen to ARAIF. The team engaged with a wide range of stakeholders including potential companies, potential partners, industry leaders and experts, industry associations, government entities, regulators, investors, and other key stakeholders. Data gathering and analysis was conducted through meetings, group discussions, webinars, and utilized prior 60 decibels customer surveys.


---
**Page 137**
---

212. ARAP values its important outreach initiative and intends to continue this engagement with stakeholders through project development and implementation. The ARAP project team also utilized thorough desk research to understand specific risks for the project, investors, the pan-African context, and relevant mitigants.

Key Project Level Environmental and Social Risks

### 213. The following are the principal environmental concerns

- **Land use and displacement:** Scaling commercial enterprises are displacing small landowners. Displaced households, particularly those who are forced to leave without compensation or a land acquisition and resettlement plan, face significant social and economic risks.
- **Water Usage:** Agriculture is the largest consumer of water in sub-Saharan Africa and a rapidly rising population is increasing demand for water resources. Climate change will exacerbate this trend versus non-dam-farmed farmers in Ethiopia found that displaced farmers mean income decline by 72%.
- **Inland labor supply:** According to the ILO, 85 per cent of jobs across Africa are in informal labor. 91 per cent of agricultural jobs in Africa are informal. Supposed for ARAP portfolio companies may use informal labor and/or child labor. ARAP will ensure that its portfolio companies comply with the OECD Due Diligence Guidance on Human Rights, ILO Conventions, and international human rights standards, and compliance with local labor and employment laws.
- **Deforestation:** Agriculture is the leading cause of forest loss in Africa, generating about 75 per cent of Africa’s carbon emissions. ARAP intends to diligence companies for risks tied to forest areas, agroforestry practices, and conservation efforts.
- **Biodiversity Loss:** Biodiversity loss in Africa causes severe damage to the environment, exacerbates climate change impacts on water resources, and undermines food security. Traditional agricultural practices and human-driven land disturbances has directly created biodiversity loss and harmed carbon sequestration potential. ARAP ESG Action includes queries and analysis of potential biodiversity loss and present and opportunistic mitigation.
- **Indigenous Peoples:** ARAP will ensure that its portfolio companies do not have adverse impacts on Indigenous Peoples.

### ESG Technical Assistance

214. ARAP’s ESG TAs support to portfolio companies to enhance their environmental and social impact, mitigate environmental and social risks, and build organizational capacity. The TA can provide consulting support in helping the company to identify and implement best practices, or provide training for staff.

215. ARAP aims to use technical assistance to conduct in-depth analysis of projects with E&S category B characteristics.

### G.2. General assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

#### EXECUTIVE SUMMARY

**218.** ARIF is deeply committed to investing and technical assistance activities promoting gender equity in the African agricultural sector. The team has developed a gender strategy, informed by research, and vulnerable farmers. ARIF II intends to expand and scale the successful gender strategy of ARIF I to new geographies, companies, initiatives, and insights.

**219.** To create a coherent ARIF gender strategy, the team collaborated with key gender experts to develop project-specific gender strategies for each country program. The team also informed the gender lens investors' strategy and the gender action plan that are detailed in Annex 8.

**220.** The team is conducting extensive stakeholder engagement across ARIF II including meetings with government officials, private sector companies, NGOs, and civil society organizations. The ARIF II team continues engaging with local gender experts and civil society organizations on local gender challenges and opportunities that informed our gender lens investing strategy and gender work.


### FINDINGS

**221.** Sex-disaggregated data: Researchers and investors alike have shared a common frustration about the lack of actionable and reliable gender data on the agriculture customer level. This partially stems from the majority of laborers in the agricultural sector being women, but also because many of these women are not literate or do not speak English. These factors make it difficult for researchers to collect sex-disaggregated data.

**222.** Barriers for female farmers: The gender lens investors have been able to identify two main barriers that prevent women from achieving their full potential in the agricultural sector. First, women are often excluded from accessing credit and other financial services because of a lack of reliable information on their income or assets. Second, women face significant challenges in accessing land ownership and inheritance rights.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 135

## Women Farmers are disproportionately harmed by climate change.

Women are less likely to have access to climate information, inputs, extension services, training, farming land, technologies, and markets. **[1]** Climate variability and extreme weather events also impact women’s livelihoods through increased workload and reduced income from their agricultural activities.

### Across Sub-Saharan Africa, women farmers face climate vulnerability and inequalities that are not shared or experienced by men.

Women spend more time on unpaid work of low productivity than men. Women do 60% to 80% of the world's work in agriculture but receive less income compared to their male counterparts. This is especially unfortunate because a majority of women who are engaged in agricultural activities are smallholder farmers, and they produce about 75% of food worldwide.

### Social and cultural dynamics across several geographies also mean that women are Women Farmers face multiple constraints beyond those of men farmers, including the burden of reproductive roles within the family and limited access to education and healthcare facilities, and discrimination that can limit access to credit and financial services.

### Women farmers often work in subsistence-oriented farming that lead to lower yields and smaller profits meaning less ability to invest in their own residence or schooling their work. **[2]** Research shows women farmers’ productivity is affected by lack of access to inputs, extension services, training, markets, and technology. The number of women farmers who are engaged in agriculture increased up to 30% and reduce hunger up to 17%. Researchers also found that women tend to do more domestic work than men.

## Barriers for women in agri-businesses

### ARAF has the privilege of working with a number of companies on improving their gender outcomes. ARAF aims to work with companies with businesses that have a strong commitment to gender equity within their operations and the work they do with customers. ARAF II aims to ensure that each company has a gender action plan in place. **[3]** The goal is for companies to be able to demonstrate that they are working towards gender equality by supporting an equal and safe workplace, improving opportunities for women in the workforce (including hiring more female staff), reaching more female customers, or collecting better sex-disaggregated data.

### Data shows that women working in agri-businesses are more likely to be part-time or work in vulnerable positions. In emerging economies, women waged employees make less than their male counterparts.

## Barriers for female entrepreneurs in agriculture

### Women face barriers when starting and scaling their agri-businesses in Africa. Accessing finance has been a barrier for many women-led agri-businesses. **[4]** Women also have less opportunities to find startup funding, mentorship, or training than men. Women are more likely to be engaged in subsistence farming rather than male counterparts, which means they have less access to potential investors and opportunities to learn from successful entrepreneurs.

## ARAF Gender Strategy

### ARAF II intends to replicate and scale the successful gender strategy of ARAF I. ARAF II intends to positively impact the climate resilience and livelihoods of female farmers and women in climate vulnerable communities, improve gender equity in agri-businesses, and draw insights across the sector.

### ARAF II needs to build a pipeline with a gender lens. Both during fund development and implementation. ARAF II aims to find new entrepreneurs with impactful and scalable businesses. Moreover, the team intends to look for

## G. Financial management and procurement (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The financial management process is centered on three entities with distinct and complementary responsibilities:

- **Acumen Resilient Agriculture Fund, LP** - with primary responsibility for investment management and oversight.
- **Fund Administrator** - responsible for administration of all financial statements, cash management, reporting, and compliance with the GCF and AMU policies for fund operations.
- **The Accredited Entity, Acumen** - responsible for fiduciary oversight for Fund GP and Accredited Entity.

Acumen Resilient Agriculture Fund, LP and Acumen have internal policies and procedures in place with regards to financial management. These are subject to regular review by the Board of Directors and are independently audited on an annual basis by an independent external auditor in accordance with internationally recognized financial reporting and auditing standards.

## G.A. Disclosure of funding proposal

The Information Disclosure Policy (IDP) provides that the GCF will apply a presumption in favor of disclosure for all information and documents relating to the GCF and its funding activities. Under the IDP, project and programme funding proposals will be disclosed on the GCF website, simultaneously with the submission to the Board of Directors. The IDP also states that the GCF will not disclose any document containing specific, negotiable portions unless it is in the public interest or there is a compelling reason for disclosure.

No confidential information: The accredited entity confirms that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may be disclosed in full by the GCF, as no information is being provided in confidence.

**LETTER OF NO OBJECTION TO THE GREEN CLIMATE FUND FOR A PROJECT TITLED "ACUMEN RESILIENT AGRICULTURE FUND II (ARAF II)**

We refer to the above Programme as included in the funding proposal submitted by Acumen for the Acumen Resilient Agriculture Fund II (ARAF II) for funding by the Green Climate Fund.

The undersigned is the duly authorized representative of the Ministry of Finance, Planning and Economic Development, the National Designated Authority/focal point of Uganda

Pursuant to the GCF decision B.08/10 and B.13/21, the content of which we acknowledge to have reviewed, we hereby communicate our no objection to the programme as included in the funding proposal.

By communicating our no objection, it is implied that:

(a) The Government of the Republic of Uganda has no objection to the Programme as included in the Funding Proposal;

(b) The Programme as included in the Funding Proposal is in conformity with Uganda's National Priorities, Strategies and Plans of Uganda;

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP252

## Proposal name: Acumen Resilient Agriculture Fund II

### Accredited entity: Acumen Fund Inc.

### Countries: Côte d'Ivoire, Egypt, Ghana, Morocco, Nigeria and Uganda

### Project/programme size: Medium

## Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1. Overview

1. Acumen Resilient Agriculture Fund (ARAF) is a private sector venture capital fund focused on investments increasing the resilience of smallholder farmers along the whole agriculture value chain (AGRiVC) – potentially in eight countries located in North Africa (Egypt and Morocco), East Africa (Kenya, Uganda and the United Republic of Tanzania) and West Africa (Ghana). At the time of submission of this assessment (proposal 05 August 2024), only Côte d'Ivoire, Egypt, Ghana and Morocco had issued no-objection letters to the project. The other four countries have not yet received NOIs from Acumen. In addition, Nigeria and Uganda have also expressed interest in participating.
2. The accredited entity (AE) is Acumen Fund Inc., The executing entities will be the Acumen Resilient Agriculture Fund Company LLC (an LLC formed under the laws of the state of Delaware, United States of America) as the manager; Acumen Resilient Agriculture Capital Management Limited (ARACML) (registered in Nigeria and licensed by the Central Bank of Nigeria) as the general partner, and ARAF II (established as an Ontario limited partnership under the laws of the province of Ontario, Canada) as the limited partner.
3. ARAF is expected to impact 165,019 direct beneficiaries and 15,799,672 indirect beneficiaries.
4. ARAF is a facilitated facility, seeks to raise a total of USD 132 million, with USD 120 million committed as equity capital from Acumen Fund Inc., USD 8 million in senior debt, USD 30 million in junior and/or senior equity, an additional USD 4 million in the form of grants. Other sources include USD 57 million in equity capital, USD 69 million in debt capital, and USD 8 million funding in grants. ARAF is a scale-up of ARAP (FP190) (ARAF I). ARAF II is an US$42M investment fund with Acumen Fund Inc. as the lead investor and additional equity capital. It is continuing to make additional investments in 2024 as its investment operations are ongoing.
5. Given the demonstrated success of ARAP, LARAF seeks to replicate and scale up on the investments using the same strategy and modality (it is a venture, early-growth and growth-stage investment fund). The fund aims to invest USD 132 million in equity capital over five years with ticket sizes. It aims to cover additional countries (Côte d'Ivoire, Egypt and Morocco) and expand the fund size and number of beneficiaries, which should result in a commensurate rise in impact.

1 ARAF (FP190) was approved by the GCF Board in 2018 and has been in implementation since 2019.


---
**Page 156**
---

6. ARAF will have the same investment strategy as AFAR I, focusing on the same three themes: aggregator platforms, digital platforms and innovative financial solutions.

7. The AFAR II theory of change is that if “The ARAF II fund provides financial and technical support to enable agricultural services to scale up, improve their services to meet the needs of smallholder farmers and become more sustainable, it will be possible for them to better serve climate-resilient and smallholder farmers will be better equipped to address climate-change-related challenges. This will lead to a virtuous cycle in which these companies are able to scale up innovative agribusiness models, thereby providing critical services to smallholder farmers and enhancing their livelihoods.”

### 1.2 Impact potential

**Scale: Medium to high**

#### 1.2.1 Impact potential

* Facilitate the efficient and effective flow of climate- and weather-related data and information between producers, traders, processors, financial institutions, commodity inputs, technology, advisory, finance, and investments and markets;
* Create an e-commerce system for the trading of agricultural commodities and the exchange of services;
* Nurture the development of partnerships and the deployment of innovative climate-friendly or climate-smart technologies, business products and services along the AgriVC; and
* Enhance food supply and security; reduce the costs of agricultural inputs and services; increase revenues and profitability; improve the quality of smallholder farmers’ lives; accelerate the adoption of climate-resilient practices by smallholder farmers and other AgriVC stakeholders (entrepreneurs and micro, small and medium-sized enterprises) in order to build a vibrant, sustainable and resilient agricultural sector.

It is estimated that as a result of this digital ecosystem North East and West Africa, the original target region for ARAF operating in Kenya, Ethiopia would reach 1839.0576 million beneficiaries; 4551.196 live directly (approximately 0.5 per cent of the total population) and 15279.961 indirectly (approximately 1.5 per cent of the total population).

There is one risk to potential impact, as follows:

* Limited monitoring and evaluation time frame: The ability to monitor and measure the value chain and its impact on smallholder farmers will be limited in ARAF II. This is a developmental concern; According to the AE, since the ARAF has fully exited company, ARAF will no longer have information rights so ongoing monitoring and measurement will not be possible. However, the companies that are currently supported by ARAF are expected to continue even following exits by ARAR. This expectation is based on the belief that with investment from ARAR, these companies will become stronger, more sustainable, and better equipped to deliver their goods and services, thereby contributing to smallholder farmers’ and provide adaptation

2. See footnote 3 in paragraph above.
3. See footnote 4 in paragraph above.

1.3 Paradigm shift potential Scale: Medium to high

The creation of a digital infrastructure and ecosystem in the agricultural business value chain can enable smallholder farmers to access inputs (e.g., seeds, fertilizers, pesticides), post-processing, finance, logistics, agri commodities and inputs trading, information dissemination, and policy advocacy.

Alternative finance. ARIF II will explore investments in financial services that offer inclusive options that are alternatives to traditional cash loans for the smallholder farmers. These include (a) microfinance institutions (MFIs) that provide credit to farmers where investment is made in exchange for farmer’s produce (e.g., rice and (b) the lease-to-own model with pay-off of the loan through future harvests). ARIF II will explore these options but not be the default or only mode of financing or lending, especially to small farmers. Some of these financial services are provided by Islamic banks that offer interest-free loans, zero-interest payments and are thus likely to be acceptable to both non-Muslim and Muslim farmers (Islamic finance interest-bearing loans and speculative insurance products, deeming them to be unethical).

Climate-smart technologies and services. ARIF II will enable the development and deployment of climate-smart agricultural technologies and services that can be adopted by all AGVic stakeholders, to leverage traditional approaches to production, post-production, logistics and access markets. These include (a) mobile-based solutions that provide real-time solutions that connect smallholder livestock farmers to source inputs and allow them to directly access markets; (b) drone and satellite imagery in managing, optimizing and monitoring crop cultivation; (c) solar-powered irrigation systems; (d) cold chain storage facilities for perishable goods; (e) solar-powered water pumps; (f) cold storage facilities; and (g) continuous cooling without power or sunlight.

Agri-business services. ARIF II will enable the development of a suite of timely and tailor-made hand-held services to meet various needs of farmers along the AGVic. This will enhance the capacities of farmers and all other AGVic stakeholders to make climate-informed decisions, increase their productivity, and accelerate their transition towards adopting climate-smart agriculture along the AGVic.

The following are some of the potential benefits:

Insufficient confidence among investors. If ARIF II is not able to (1) attract private sector investments at significant scale, performs poorly in terms of delivering financial returns, has limited impact on increasing agricultural productivity and/or (2) demonstrates a track record of investor companies, then this may undermine investors’ confidence about investing in these types of climate-smart projects.

Investor concerns. Poor performance, underperformance, overpromising, and excess hype of the investor companies’ climate-related products and services provided to farmers can also arise. Customers may undermine the efficacy of a particular product or service, or a bundle of such services, creating an unsound perception of the products and services being not fit for purpose.

All responses to questions from the IAF

26. Overall, the paradigm shift potential is medium to high.

### Sustainable development potential

#### Scale: Medium to high

1. **Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs):** The funding proposal cuts across several of the SDGs. ARIF II will contribute to the achievement of SDG 7 (Climate Action), which is “integrated climate change adaptation and mitigation” and SDG 6 (“Clean Water and Sanitation Development”).<sup>12</sup> ARIF II will also contribute to SDG 1 (No Poverty), SDG 2 (“Zero Hunger”), SDG 3 (“Good Health and Well-being”), SDG 4 (“Quality Education”), SDG 5 (“Gender Equality”), SDG 8 (“Decent Work and Economic Growth”), SDG 12 (“Responsible Consumption and Production”) and SDG 17 (Partnership for the Goals).

2. **Ecosystem-based adaptation aggregators platform:** These themes approach enable partnerships along the AgriFin. As presented in the funding proposal’s case studies, the investee companies create new business models, business partnerships, and commercial or trading activities along local and global AgriBCFs.

3. Additionally, the use of modern technologies (e.g., drones and satellites) and digital infrastructure (e.g., mobile phones and tablets) will enable a potential for increased productivity through timely personalized crop management and irrigation recommendations, web- and mobile-based business models, and improved access to markets. ARIF II will support the creation of partnerships among farmers, entrepreneurs, financiers and investors, supporting them to carry out more business activities on the local scale.

### Expected outcomes

1. **Empowerment of smallholders:** The advice on climate-smart agriculture will enable knowledge-sharing concerning improved farming approaches (e.g., regenerative farming). Additionally, ARIF II will support the development of new value chains for crops (e.g., horticulture and aquaponics)<sup>13</sup> that cross-cutting climate-smart interventions that can support the decarbonization of AgriFin.

2. **Social impact:** The project’s various potential social co-benefits, which may enhance the socioeconomic conditions of the ARIF II communities and the smallholder farmers involved, include: (a) increased food security and improved nutrition for households (e.g., better health and more quality time for the family or oneself); and (c) increased resilience against climate shocks (e.g., better understanding and application of climate-smart agricultural skills and practices).

3. **Gender-sensitive development impact:** ARIF II has submitted a gender action plan and a revised business model that includes women as key stakeholders in its target countries (Togo, Egypt, Ghana and Morocco). According to the AE, the portfolio companies mainstream and operationalize gender in their investments. ARIF II recognizes the impact of climate change on women’s livelihoods and seeks to ensure that women are included in all financial and investment assessments in the target companies and requires them to prepare gender action plans as part of their business models. ARIF II will support the scaling up of these gender-sensitive interventions<sup>14</sup>. However, no sample of a completed gender action plan from an investee company (under ARIF I or for ARIF III) is available for review by the TAF.

27. There is risk to sustainable development potential, as follows.

28. Uncertain SDG impact assessments. As mentioned in paragraph 11 above, there is a risk that, upon the exit of the investors in the ABIF II investor community, it will not be possible to effectively monitor and measure the resilience and development impacts on the target farmers and their families, as well as the broader rural communities.

29. Overall, the sustainable development potential is assessed as medium to high.


### 1.5 Needs of the recipient

#### Scale: Medium

**Stakeholder engagement plan:** The funding proposal contains a stakeholder engagement plan for ABIF II with a list of targeted stakeholders[30] to be consulted for each NUL country. The list covers women's groups; non-governmental organizations; academia; the private sector (including agribusinesses, input providers, and agricultural service providers); cooperatives, associations and unions; governments (e.g., national designated authorities, agriculture ministries, and rural development agencies); and financial institutions; and development agencies. This will help with identifying and understanding the needs of each NUL country.

**Need for bundled solutions:** There is a need to tie-in the AgriVC. The ABIF II approach to bundle products and services directly addresses the activities of smallholder farmers, including access to inputs (e.g., seeds, fertilizers, and pesticides), agricultural advice,[31] training (e.g., on soil and crop management) and inputs;[32] climate-informed weather advisory services; insurance solutions for droughts and floods; credit; value chain facilitation; and climate and weather crop insurance services. The integrated and holistic investment strategies will help to address the needs of smallholder farmers.

**Needs of the private sector:** From the perspective of the private sector, ABIF II readily addresses the critical funding requirements of early-stage and growth-stage start-ups that offer value-based solutions for smallholder farmers in NUL countries. This will enable them to become more resilient to climate change. Given that enabling policies and market conditions in these African countries are still under development, there is a need to proactively work with the agricultural sector as a high-risk undertaking; there is a need to proactively work with investors into these proposed aggregator platforms and innovative financing solutions and services for smallholder farmers.

**Alternative finance needs of farmers:** As mentioned in paragraph 14 above, the financing needs of smallholder farmers along the AgriVC are diverse. ABIF II enables innovative and inclusive financing modalities.

34. There are three risks to the needs of the recipient, as follows:


---
**Page 160**
---

39\. **Needs of local smallholder farmers**. A review of the stakeholder lists and engagement meetings shows that (a) there is limited to no participation of local mandated climate change authorities (e.g., meteorological agencies); (b) questions and discussions around climate change are often framed in terms of weather variability, rather than climate challenges; financing and agricultural market barriers is general in nature; and (d) while there are some generic concerns about the impacts of climate change on smallholder farmers, private sector and communities, there is little to no in-depth discussion of the context of vulnerability of the target population. In addition, there is limited information on hazards (e.g., droughts and storms), the most pressing challenges at the local level, or the most urgent or highest-priority needs in addressing this climate vulnerability. Thus, it is not sufficiently clear whether the available adaptation measures are appropriate for these farmers.

40\. **Need for more robust local climate change assessments to feed into adaptation measures**. (1) The majority of the funding proposals are based on a review of existing literature-based studies, with very little data on the target population’s context of vulnerability, on how climate change is currently impacting them or on how it will impact the agricultural sector in the future. There is also limited information about the most pressing challenges at the local level and how climate change is impacting the AGRI in similar regions. The climate change assessments conducted so far are not sufficiently robust to inform adaptation measures.

41\. **These localized assessments should inform the baseline of the farmers’ context of vulnerability and the existing as well as potential array of climate adaptation measures that may reduce their exposure to climate risks**. For example, the four original NOLs (Côte d'Ivoire, Egypt, Ghana and Morocco) present limited information on the context of vulnerability of smallholder farmers. The identified climate adaptation and resilience measures were selected for these countries.

42\. **Observed limitations of the Agriculture Resilience Investment Screen (ARIS)**

**Toward a more robust ARIS tool**: The ARIS Tool is limited in its ability to provide an assessment of potential investment opportunities (i.e., the investor companies). The ARIS Toolkit is limited in its ability to assess climate risks and resilience, and it does not have a clear use by investment managers looking to invest in a company.

43\. According to the funding proposal, the ARIS Toolkit provides: *a* Consistent, rapid and robust methodology for assessing the potential of an agricultural business to create resilience opportunities; and has the capability to (i) Assess climate change and weather risks to a business’s operations and supply chain; (ii) Identify key climate-related operational risks; (iii) Assess the climate vulnerability of the target population and evaluate how a business model addresses these vulnerabilities, including through adaptation measures; (iv) Assess potential negative impacts of a business that would exacerbate climate risks and outweigh benefits; (v) Generate a score for each investment; (vi) Allow for comparison between potentially different investments. However, it is not clear whether the ARIS Toolkit is assessing the climate potential of an investor’s product, services or technology solution, mentions the need to assess the context of vulnerability of smallholder farmers involved.

44\. Specifically, the cited authorities on climate-related matters are limited to open data sources. For temperature, fresh-water availability, precipitation, sea level rise, flooding,

**Footnote 1**: See funding proposal executive file note 3: “Stakeholders engagement List” (Côte d’Ivoire, Egypt, Ghana and Morocco).

**Footnote 2**: See funding proposal file Annex 6: “Stakeholders Engagement Meeting Minutes” (Côte d’Ivoire, Ghana and Morocco).

**Footnote 3**: The section of funding proposals on climate change such as demand-driven rainfall, potential increase in the use of irrigation methods to increase resistance to climate shocks in the sector.


---
**Page 161**
---

landslides and the general climate and development profiles of a country, the ARIS Toolkit relies on the World Bank Climate Change Knowledge Portal. For storms, which covers precipitation and hail, the toolkit uses CarbonBrief.org. For wind, it uses GlobalWindData.com. For natural disasters such as tsunamis, earthquakes, and volcanic eruptions, which cover water availability, flooding and hydrological flows, it uses the World Resources Institute (WRI.org). Also for these types of events, it uses the National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration - National Geophysical Data Center (NOAA NGDC) website (https://www.ngdc.noaa.gov/seg/earthquakes.html). For tsunamis, it also uses the Pacific Tsunami Warning Center (http://www.tsunamitools.org/). For earthquakes, it uses the United States Geological Survey (USGS) website (https://earthquake.usgs.gov/earthquakes/search/). For volcanic eruptions, it uses the Global Volcanism Program (GVP) of the Smithsonian Institution (https://volcano.si.edu/volcano.cfm?vuln=102740).

For other issues such as pests and diseases, no specific sources are provided. Despite the fact that in Ghana, for example, there is a high risk of malaria, it is not clear whether ARIS also coordinates with authoritative international organizations such as the World Health Organization (WHO) or the Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO) to the World Health Organization. From the stakeholder lists for each nati, OUN, it appears that there are no references to these organizations in the list of stakeholders from meteorological agencies.

The ARIS Toolkit also mentions a "NEX" analysis for guidance on matters related to: (a) climate change impacts and adaptation; (b) natural hazards and ecosystem services; (c) livelihoods of people and communities; (d) food, water and health; and (e) other", which is not explained.

### Assessment of the ARIS Toolkit by the ITAP

The following issues are observed:

- The target focus of vulnerable sectors is very general, which results in the bundling of all sectors into one. This makes it difficult to identify specific vulnerabilities that should be addressed by the ARIS Toolkit asks: "In location of target geography how likely is this type of risk to either become more severe or less severe over time?"

- In the ARIS Toolkit, there is no indication of whether climate factors that will be assessed in the project area (temperature, storms, etc.) are aligned with the historical and projected climate data. This information should come from a locally mandated government agency (e.g., meteorological agencies) or other governmental sources.

- The specific content of vulnerability of the smallholder farmers is not adequately captured or clearly described, as can be seen from the question "To what degree would these changes impact the livelihoods and food security of smallholder farmers?" This is a key issue in terms of vulnerability of the farmers together with the ecosystems, the built infrastructure and investments.

- Under Resilience Benefit Tab (b), while the target sectors are identified separately, the proposed adaptation and resilience measures are generic, and it is not adequately explained how these measures will address the specific vulnerabilities previously identified under existing locality-specific adaptation and resilience plan; and

- While there is a Wizmark verification, there is no reference made to whether this assessment has been reviewed or confirmed by other local climate change experts.

42. Overall, the needs of the recipient are assessed as medium.

1.6 Country ownership

### Alignment with national climate strategy and priorities

Similar to AFAR I, the investment strategy of AFAR II will be aligned with each country's national adaptation plan (NAP) and other relevant national strategies, such as Kenya's National Climate Change Action Plan (CCAP). AFAR II will maintain direct communication channels with key government officials in these countries. In addition, increased coordination and collaboration in these areas, AFAR II will also conduct consumer surveys to gather feedback and share lessons learned.

### Strong private sector interest

Based on the list of stakeholders met during consultations, there is a strong presence of agribusiness representatives along the AgriVG (e.g., logistics, input supply, value chain development, growth stage companies and technology providers, and farmer associations). There is one risk to country ownership as follows:

- Limited engagement with national meteorological and climate-smart authorities in country, and alignment with local adaptation plans and strategies. 1.7 Efficiency and effectiveness Scale: Medium to high

Efficiency of risk-taking scheme. As proposed, the GCF USD 30 million junior and/or senior equity, which will act as first-loss capital, will attract USD 90 million of senior equity (3x leverage) and USD 240 million of junior equity (8x leverage), for a total investment of USD # in funding from other investors (2x leverage).

Efficient and effective allocation use of the AARF II technical assistance fund (TAF). As per the proposal, the TAF will be allocated at "10 percent of the targeted fund size of USD 120 million", this is very much aligned with industry benchmarks. Application of the TAF will be guided by a TAF Investment Committee, which will consist of representatives from outsourcing expertise to conduct activities in support of climate adaptation and gender inclusion. The TAF will also provide technical assistance to the investor companies, as well as other relevant business operational needs of the target investee companies.

Target internal rate of return (IRR). According to the ACF-ARAF II it is confident that it can deliver the projected financial returns based on the track record of ARAP I.

There are six risks to effectiveness and efficiency, as follows:

1. Monitoring and reporting: As outlined in section 11 above, the inability to monitor and measure the other long-term goals of resilience and the developmental impacts of AARF II (and ARAP I) following the exit of the investors in the AARF II investor companies is a concern.

Risk of limited climate change capacity. While the investment management component of the GCF has been designed with climate change as an explicit goal, there is perceived weakness by TAP in the climate assessment process and governance system. The funding proposal states that ARAP II will have an in-house climate change expert to ensure both appropriate climate risk assessments are conducted at each stage of the decision-making process. ARAP II will also have an independent panel of climate experts to review all investment proposals, with a minimum of two years' experience in the investment committee before approval of any transactions. However, the proposed budget for the in-house climate expert reported as USD 100k per year is less than one percent of the total budget. Moreover, under proposed budget activity (A) "Investment Management", there are no specific activities related to the assumption that specialized climate expertise is already present in the positions of partners, directors and/or other senior management roles within TAP. There may be a need for a mitigation specialist, which could be considered important, given that there are mitigation components in some projects under ARAP I and ARAP II; (b) that the assessment and/or monetization of climate change risks is not currently part of the investment process; and (c) that some of the investor companies will have cross-cutting climate risks and opportunities that need to be properly assessed. Additionally, no clear or documented local partnerships have


---
**Page 164**
---

be forthwith with authorities on climate and/or agriculture (e.g., meteorological agencies or development agencies such as the FAO) to support the climate / environmental, social and governance (ESG) analysis). All these observations pose a risk and concern that a general junior climate and/or agricultural officer may not have sufficient experience in assessing climate change experience as well as quality time allocated for climate assessments, and therefore be insufficiently equipped to make an informed decision on whether to invest. The results of the climate assessment produced by the consultants and/or the open-source climate data tools are likely to be too complex and detailed for a junior officer to understand. The product/service tendering process to address local climate may not be articulated well enough to the investment committee.

### Limitations of the phone based survey

As mentioned (see para 38 above), IAMF will use the ARIS Toolkit to assess the climate risks of the target sector and of farmer end users of the investment proposal. However, this tool is an approximation and does not fully capture all the costs and vulnerabilities of the farm/farmer specifically. The ARIS Toolkit is mainly centred around investment management, products and services. As it is a rapid assessment tool, it does not provide a detailed analysis of climate risks. It also does not address the priority needs of local smallholder farmers, taking into account the array of other factors that may be important to them such as access to credit, extension service partners or government. The "List sheet", which provides a list of climate impacts and proposed adaptation measures, is limited in scope. Only 10 adaptation measures were selected, nor how they compare to a particular baseline in the project area.

### Limited questions of the phone based survey

The funding proposal contains the results of a phone-based survey conducted by IAMF. The survey was designed to capture views on climate change issues experienced by farmers and their families. However, it may not fully capture all the costs and vulnerabilities of the farm/farmer specifically. The ARIS Toolkit is mainly centred around investment management, products and services. As it is a rapid assessment tool, it does not provide a detailed analysis of climate risks. It also does not address the priority needs of local smallholder farmers, taking into account the array of other factors that may be important to them such as access to credit, extension service partners or government.

### Need for climate impact metrics for participation in the carry

The proposed management structure and governance arrangements for the investment proposal (see para 54 above) are consistent with the GCF. The IAMF comments the inclusion of both financial targets and impact metrics as triggers for the general partnership in the carry. However, the current climate metrics are primarily focused on broad sustainability indicators such as access to livelihoods (individuals reached) that are somewhat tangential to the climate mitigation and adaptation outcomes that GCF funding is intended to support. We recommend that the Secretariat collaborates with


---
**Page 165**
---

All to identify and incorporate climate-specific metrics into the impact criteria that would trigger a carry-out.

### Need for more evidence-based data on fund performance

This funding proposal builds on a previous ABAP programme (FP078) operated by the AE. In such cases, the IFAP assessment methodology is used to evaluate the performance of the programme. We believe it is good practice for the Secretary to assess the performance of initial programmes and to use this information in future evaluations.

#### Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

The ITAP recommends this funding proposal for approval by the Board.
To further strengthen the submitted funding proposal and address the aforementioned issues that need to be addressed, the following recommendations are presented to the AE for serious consideration:

- Seek to incorporate an impact metric as discussed in paragraph 51 below;
- Increase the scope and diversity of consultations with smallholder farmers in each of the NOL countries to address issues raised in paragraphs 35, 36, 41(i), 41(ii), 41(iv) and 48 above;
- Strengthen the ABIS Toolkit by integrating more comprehensive climate risk assessments in support of its results, including content on the vulnerability of farmers and pastoralists to climate change; and measures to address issues raised in paragraphs 35, 36, 40, 41, 55 and 56 above;
- Explore ways to strengthen the survey methodology, going beyond phone-based surveys to gather information from farmers and pastoralists. This could include directly track, monitor, evaluate and report results (including potential collaboration with technical partners in climate change information management) to address issues raised in paragraphs 57 and 59 above;
- Expand the questions in the survey to include the following matters: climate-smart agricultural practices; other climate-smart agriculture adaptation and/or mitigation measures being implemented in the same country or region; whether farmers are implementing these practices at the same sector or subsector; losses and damages experienced by farmers (assets, income, crops, etc.); and access to formal and informal financing of farmers to address the issues raised in paragraph 57 above;
- Utilize lean data approaches to include medium-to-long-term data and information from other sources, such as satellite imagery, to better understand the climate sector, including rapid- and slow-onset events; cooperating with local climate change authorities in each country to address the issues raised in paragraph 57 above;
- Generate a set of guidelines for the AE to use when working with designated authorities and in-country centres of excellence to provide guidance on climate assessments, and explore parallel and continued monitoring of adaptation and resilience

### Impact potential

The ITAP’s medium to high rating of the program's impact potential is noted. As captured in the commentary, ARAF II intends to continue along with the work done by ARAF I and anticipate that the program will be able to address several of the bottlenecks along the AgBVC. Specifically, providing information to farmers, including weather-related data and climate-smart farming knowledge; efficiently connecting farmers to markets; and creating a more sustainable income stream for the farmers through the creation of value chains. ARAF II also intends to continue with distribution mechanisms for poultry that help farmers increase and diversify their incomes. The aim is to provide smallholder farmers in these countries with access to inputs, credit, and training to improve their productivity/yields, income and livelihoods. We acknowledge the observation around the potential of ARAF II to create more value for smallholder farmers than its predecessors following its exit from the country and would like to highlight the following considerations:

- The smallholder farmers who engage with investors are paid suppliers or paying customers, depending on whether they are selling their produce or buying inputs. The expectation is that companies form mutually beneficial relationships with the farmers that they work with. ARAF II will continue to monitor the performance of the farmers they work with – ARAF screens for this intentionality towards farmers from the beginning of the investment evaluation and continues to resolve this with companies during the due diligence process. ARAF II also intends to continue to be financially accountable serving these suppliers and customers, and it is a plausible assumption that such sustainability will continue beyond the project period.

- Given a 6-year investment period and a 5–7 year holding period, it is envisaged that majority of the exit will occur within the last few years of the project lifecycle, and so we expect to see a significant increase in the number of smallholder farmers served by the project; and

- There are recurring costs associated with ongoing monitoring that will necessarily taper and, at project term, no longer be available to our M&Es teams or our past investors.

# GENERAL CONTEXT

Côte d'Ivoire is located in West Africa along the Gulf of Guinea, with the Atlantic Ocean running along its southern coast. It is bordered by Liberia, Guinea, Mali, Burkina Faso, and Ghana. Côte d'Ivoire has a population of 28.6 million of which 48% are female and 52% male in 2021.* While 53% of the population aged between 15-64 is economically active, women represent only 47%. The female population with 41% of the population aged between 0-14, 57% aged between 15-64 and only 2% aged 65% and older.

Côte d'Ivoire is the largest economy in the West African Economic and Monetary Union and acts as a regional economic hub and host country for many nations in the region. It has a significant manufacturing sector and is the world's top exporter of cocoa and raw shea butter nuts and a net exporter of coffee, palm oil, and rubber. The country's GDP per capita was $2,805 (in 2019), having grown 6.7% in the prior year* and the World Bank highlights that Côte d'Ivoire is experiencing one of the fastest sustained economic growth rates in Sub-Saharan Africa over a decade.* Poverty is estimated at 34.3% of the population, with women being disproportionately affected.* According to UN Women data, 24.1% of employed women over the age of 15 are below the international poverty line, compared to only 9.8% for men.* The country's gender equality index is 0.634*, ranking low in the human development category* at 160/193 countries and territories*.

According to UN Women, Côte d'Ivoire's dedication to gender equality and women's empowerment is evident through its ratification of international instruments such as the Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action. These include instruments that specifically address women's rights, such as the Protocol on Women's Rights within the African Charter, and broader human rights conventions like CEDAW.* Additionally, their commitment was further demonstrated by the adoption of the National Action Plan on Gender Equality in 2018, and the UN Security Council Resolutions focused on Women, Peace and Security*.* In terms of the legal status of women, Côte d'Ivoire's commitment to gender equality took a legal step forward in 2019 with the adoption of the law on equal access for women to economic resources and their entry into the labour market*. Furthermore, in 2019, a law stating that all political parties must include at least 30% of women in their list of candidates for elected assemblies was adopted by the Côte d'Ivoire

# Health data and analysis

## What is the maternal mortality rate, infant mortality rate, and life expectancy (disaggregated by sex)?

As of 2020:
- **480** women die per 100,000 live births due to pregnancy-related causes, which is slightly better than the regional average for Sub-Saharan Africa at 536 deaths per 100,000 live births[1].
- The World Health Organization (WHO) estimates that **48%** of maternal deaths in Côte d'Ivoire are caused by complications related to pregnancy and childbirth.
- The Maternal Mortality Ratio (MMR) alongside other West African countries like Ghana, Senegal, and Gambia. The MMR in these countries is 506 per 100,000 live births[2].
- **96** deaths per 1,000 live births for girls aged between 15–19.
- Life expectancy: **62 years for females**, and **59 years for males**.
