2. Sexual Exploitation, Abuse and Harassment (SEAH) laws, policy, trends, and data

i. Any info on SEAH in the workplace? Trends on incidents?

According to the World Bank Women, Business and the Law, Côte d'Ivoire (with 77.5 points out of 100, with 100 being the best) is placed higher in the legal frameworks score than the global average of 68.2 points. The country scores highest on freedom of movement, but has a perfect score in many indicators such as freedom of movement, laws affecting women's pay, property and inheritance, the lowest score for Côte d'Ivoire comes from the legal frameworks supporting the "safety" of women. There is no specific legislation to protect women against sexual harassment, domestic violence or femicide, and there is no government entity responsible for the monitoring and implementation of laws protecting women's rights in Côte d'Ivoire. The country has a law on domestic violence but it does not apply to women. There is legislation in place related to child marriage, namely Loi No. 2019-576 du 26 juin 2019 relative au mariage. Arts. 2 et 3 Code Penal, Art. 439.

According to UN Women, as of December 2020, only 47.6% of indicators needed to monitor the implementation of laws and policies on women's rights were available, and there are gaps in key areas, including physical and sexual harassment[1].

Some key statistics include the following:

| Indicator | Measure (Côte d'Ivoire) |

[1] World Bank, Women, Business and the Law, 2024
[2] World Bank, Women, Business and the Law, 2024
[3] UN Women, 2020
[4] UNFPA 2024 World Population Dashboard Côte d'Ivoire, 2024


---
**Page 177**
---

Moreover, Sexual Exploitation, Abuse and Harassment (SEAH) are serious issues affecting workplaces in Côte d'Ivoire. According to a study done by Appiah in 2022**, the prevalence of violence and harassment in workplaces can be attributed to both internal organisational dynamics and broader societal influences. **This study found the following reasons that contribute to SEAH in the workplace:

* *The Power Relationship*: Unequal power structures are a major reason why violence and harassment occur. This is because employees often feel intimidated by their superiors, especially those with legal authority, which can create situations where employees feel less empowered to speak up against inappropriate behaviour.
* *The Ineffectiveness of Laws*: The study's data suggests that job insecurity significantly contributes to violence and harassment. Many workers, fearing job loss, find themselves in precarious situations. Employment becomes a privilege, not a right, leading some to tolerate abusive behavior.
* *Impunity*: The study identified impunity as a major factor behind workplace violence and harassment. According to data collected from those who had experienced SEAH in the workplace, many felt that their complaints were not taken seriously by the justice system as biased and unlikely to deliver justice. This lack of consequences discourages reporting and weakens trust in institutions. Furthermore, the cycle of impunity emboldens perpetrators to reoffend while deters bystanders from intervening, ultimately creating a climate where workplace abuse thrives.
* *The Insufficiency of the Laws*: The study found that although each law has Labour Code articles addressing SEAH, there is no mention of penalties for perpetrators and the law is incomplete. Specifically for the workplace, there is no text protecting vulnerable groups including women, those employed in the informal sector, and domestic workers.
* *The Ignorance of the Law and Protection Mechanisms*: The study's survey results also suggest that a lack of knowledge about legal rights and protective measures contributes to workplace violence and harassment. Many employees are unaware of their rights or how to seek protection from such incidents.
* *The Problem of the Burden of Proof*: The burden of proving the guilt of the perpetrator falls on the victim, which is often a prohibitive factor in reporting violence and harassment in the workplace. This both discourages reporting and emboldens perpetrators to become repeat offenders.


**Appiah, 2022


---
**Page 178**
---

- *Weak Monitoring Capacities of Labor Regulators***: The bodies in place to monitor progress against SEAH in the workplace and ensure legislation are not equipped adequately to efficiently address it, further exacerbating the feelings of impunity of perpetrators and the vulnerability of victims.

Thus, this study found that the factors of "the power relationship", "the precariousness", *"impunity",* "the insufficiency of laws", "the ignorance of the law and protection mechanisms", "the problem of lack of awareness", and *"weak monitoring capacities of labor regulators"* contribute to SEAH in the workplace.

## 3. Political and governing data and analysis

### i. What is the legal status of women in the country of intervention?

ii. Are there any opportunities to promote the leadership of women in local governance/political systems and formal/informal institutions? If not, what are some of the constraints that hinder women from assuming leadership roles?

Women received the right to vote in 1960, and women have equal rights in terms of access to freedom of movement, and nearly equal rights in terms of access to justice*. According to UN Women, Côte d'Ivoire has ratified numerous international agreements on women's rights at both the national and the international stage*, This is evidenced by the ratification of numerous agreements that address women's rights and broader human rights principles*. These include the Protocol on Women's Rights and Gender Equality, the Charter for CEDAW, the African Union's Maputo Protocol Being Declaration and Platform for Action, and the UN Security Council Resolutions on Women, Peace and Security. Furthermore, in 2019, a law stating that all political parties must include women in their lists of candidates was adopted by the National Assembly of Côte d'Ivoire (parliament)* However, regardless of these commitments, there are still challenges that hinder progress towards gender equality in Côte d'Ivoire. According to the World Economic Forum, 12th out of the 146 countries assessed in terms of political empowerment overall, 122nd in terms of women in parliament and 85th in terms of women in ministerial positions*. To date, there has never been a female head of state in Côte d'Ivoire*

# Employment data

## Labor force participation rate (disaggregated by sex), employment rate (disaggregated by sex), unemployment rate (disaggregated by sex)

As of 2023, the labor force participation rate among women is 56.5%, and 72.2% for men[1]. The gap between women and men is slightly larger in Côte d'Ivoire than in the rest of Sub-Saharan Africa (60.7% for women and 72.8% for men)[2]. Workers in vulnerable employment are the least likely to have formal work arrangements, social protection and safety nets and are therefore more likely to fall into poverty[3].

# Social and cultural gender norms

## What are commonly held beliefs, perceptions, and stereotypes related to gender in the project/program footprint area or the country of intervention?

### i. What are the differences among women and men in the project/program footprint area and/or the country of intervention? – Research on decision-making caregiving, household chores etc.

### ii. What assets (economic, financial, physical, social, and other assets) do women and men have access to? Who manages or controls access to these resources?

### iv. To what extent do women and men from vulnerable communities participate in decision-making processes at the community level? What are the constraints (social, cultural, economic, political) that restrict women's active participation in household and community-level decision-making processes?


Côte d'Ivoire has a Social Progress Index (SPI) of 48.31 out of 100 and is classified as a Tier 5 Country, rated as 131 out of the 176 ranked countries and territories[2]. The statistics suggest that the basic needs such as nutrition and medical care, housing, water and sanitation safety are below global standards, and also that social progress has stagnated in Côte d'Ivoire during the previous year[3].

In Côte d'Ivoire, a woman is considered to be the Head of family in the same way as a man. This is due to the legal provisions that would require a man to marry his wife by her husband and a woman can also obtain a judgment of divorce and choose where to live in the same way that a man can[4]. However, there are still some laws that discriminate against women in Côte d'Ivoire which continue to discriminate against women; for instance, over 62% of women and girls aged 15 to 24 in the Western part of the country are of the opinion that wife beating can be justified, while less than half (48%) of men share this view[5].

Men and women have equal administrative power and ownership rights to immovable property, including land, and sons and daughters and female and male surviving spouses have equal rights to inherit assets. However, in practice, women are often discriminated against when inheriting their male counterparts[6]. There are currently government-led programmes that provide support to female entrepreneurs such as access to finance and a national government plan with a focus on women's access to financial services (Plan National d'Accès des Femmes à la Finance et au Crédit, Plan Nationale d'Inclusion Financière 2019-2024)[7].

Côte d'Ivoire is the largest economy in the West African Economic and Monetary Union (WAEMU) or, in French, UEMOA - Union Économique et Monétaire Ouest Africaine) and acts as a regional economic hub. It has one of the highest rates of urbanization in Africa, with 50% of its population living in cities. Côte d'Ivoire is also the world's largest cocoa producer, the leading sector and in the world's top exporter of cocoa and raw cashew nuts and a net exporter of oil. The GDP per capita has grown at an average rate of 4.8% over the past decade, one of the highest prior years and the World Bank highlights that Côte d'Ivoire is experiencing one of the fastest sustained economic growth rates in Sub-Saharan Africa over a decade. Poverty is estimated at 9.7% of the population aged between 15-64 years old. The country has also made significant progress on gender equality, with 38.2% of employed women over the age of 15 are below the international poverty line, compared to 10.8%

of men.[1] With a Human Development Index (HDI) of 0.534, it ranks "low" in the human development category at 166/193 countries and territories[2].

The World Bank Gender Data Portal Gap Report places Côte d'Ivoire as the 122nd country amongst the 146 that it has assessed as part of the research, with a score of 0.650[3]. Women's economic participation and opportunity varies to some extent with a labour force participation rate of 68.5% for women compared to 97.5% for men in Côte d'Ivoire. The average monthly regional average of 65.7% for females and 72.8% for males[4]. Wage equality for similar work is scored at 0.690 by WDI, with a range from 1/7 - best), suggesting that women do not get paid the same wages and therefore have less economic power than men in Côte d'Ivoire compared to other developing countries. Women and men are assessed as having near-equal rights in terms of access to financial services[5]. However, women face significant challenges when it comes to accessing land and non-land assets[6]. With regards to employment in senior and middle management women represented 22.2% in 2017[7].

According to the World Bank, 34.7% of women in Côte d'Ivoire have accounts with a balance versus 64% of men[8]. The female rate in Côte d'Ivoire is nearly the same as Sub-Saharan Africa (49% of women own accounts versus 61% of men)[9]. In 2021 in Côte d'Ivoire, 27.5% of women and 35.8% of men used a smartphone to access the internet at least once in the past 12 months[10]. The gap in internet usage between men and women in Côte d'Ivoire, 16.9%, is much larger than the gap in Sub-Saharan Africa of 3.3% (19.4% for men, and 14.1% women)[11].

Agriculture is the foundation of the economy in Côte d'Ivoire, representing 22% of GDP (versus 48% at Independence). The country has a population of approximately 30 million people. Of these, 15 million farmers are women. Seventy-five percent (75%) of hornians depend on agricultural activities for their basic livelihoods while 77% are employed in the informal sector for instance in self-employment or small-scale agriculture.

According to the World Bank, Côte d'Ivoire is the world's largest producer of cocoa, with over 40% of the world's total production. The country also is the world's largest producer and exporter of cashew nuts, as well as the second-largest producer of palm oil in Africa (after Nigeria), and the second largest producer of Robusta in Africa (7th in the world). The World Bank reports that Côte d'Ivoire produces a wide variety of crops including rice, maize, sorghum, millet, yam, cassava, and plantain bananas, with exports growing to the sub-region.

The strong agriculture economy is built on a rich natural resource base. Approximately 75%, or 24 million ha out of the country's 32 million ha territory is used as agricultural land[10]. Of this, 3.5 million ha is arable land and 6.8 million ha is permanent pastureland (including forest reserves and pastures)[11].

The agricultural sector is dominated by subsistence and rainfed labour-intensive manual cultivation practices produced by smallholder producers, who work 48% of the arable land, with low levels of mechanization, irrigation, and use of fertilizer and other inputs[12]. More than 90% of all farms are very small holdings; the average agricultural holding in Côte d'Ivoire is between 0.5 to 3 ha[13] and there are no large-scale commercial farms. The largest concentration of these small farmers is in the south of the country[14].

Agriculture in Côte d'Ivoire remains labour intensive, with infrequent input use and a low level of mechanization. Only about 2% of the total cultivated area is irrigated, which is much lower than the Sub-Saharan African average of 60%. Yields are frequently low except in the case of rubber, oil palm, and cotton, for which most of the fertilizer in the country is used[15]. The majority of farmers grow a mix

### Climate change and agriculture

i. How does climate change affect female farmers vs male farmers?  
ii. What are the inequalities in access to resources between social groups in the project/program footprint area? How do these inequalities affect people's capacity to adapt to climate change?  

iii. Are there existing gender inequities that may be exacerbated by climate change impacts in the proposed project/program footprint area?

Sub-Saharan Africa’s economies, heavily reliant on agriculture, are significantly impacted by climate change due to the sector's climate sensitivity**. The agricultural sector in Côte d'Ivoire is hampered by a confluence of challenges, including deforestation (at an alarming rate), the decline of reefed soils, and erosion of land and water resources, as well as desertification.


Côte d’Ivoire faces a multitude of interconnected challenges/threats due to its geographical location**. These challenges include unmanageable socio-economic consequences of floods, land degradation, rising sea levels, coastal erosion, declining agricultural productivity, increased vulnerability to endemic diseases like malaria and meningitis, and a rise in the frequency of droughts**.

According to the FAO (2018), over the five decades from 1960 to 2010, the national average temperature has risen by 1.4°C in the Horn of Africa. Climate projections further suggest continued warming, with potential increases of 1.9°C and 2.7°C by 2050 and 2070, respectively. Notably, these increases are expected to be most pronounced in the northern regions, where malnutrition rates are already high.

Increased temperatures affect rainfall. Rainfall patterns are changing dramatically, with the FAO reporting a decline in rainfall amounts, shorter rainy seasons with delayed starts, longer dry seasons, and rising temperatures (1). These disruptions throw farming cycles into disarray and stress crops (2).

UN Women (2022) states that women and girls face multiple and intersecting climate crises, coupled with persistent gender inequality, creating a global challenge; it threatens the livelihoods, health, safety, and security of women and girls worldwide. According to UN Women (2022), women depend more on natural resources than their male counterparts, despite having less access to them.

In many areas, women are responsible for securing essential resources like food, water, and fuel for their families (3). This burden is especially heavy in low- and lower-middle income countries where agriculture is a primary source of income. When droughts or unpredictable rainfall occur, these women, who often have limited access to education and training, struggle to find ways to secure resources for their families (4). This can force girls to leave school to help, limiting their educational and future opportunities.

10. Vulnerable Subgroups

Could there be a short section on vulnerable subgroups, e.g., children, girls, women and men with disabilities, the elderly, widows, indigenous? Any specific info on them?

According to Doctors with Africa (CUAMM) (2023)**18**, many people with disabilities face challenges getting the healthcare they need. These challenges can include high costs, a lack of available services, and social barriers such as stigma or discrimination**19**. Health workers in some settings may not have the proper training or understanding**20**. There are organisations present trying to tackle these issues, such as the Rehabilitation Centre at Mother Teresa Verzini in Ivory Coast which helps children with severe psychomotor disorders**21**.

Since 1971, SOS Children's Villages has provided a haven for children and young people in Côte d'Ivoire. The organisation is not free of doing so**22**. In Côte d'Ivoire, 40% of the population lives below the poverty line**23**. According to GAP Missions an Orphan Ministry, Côte d'Ivoire had 130,000 orphans in 2022**24** and 22% of children, particularly those between 5 and 14, are forced into child labour**25**. In 2020, the country was ranked as having the highest number of people who are chronically undernourished. In 2020, Côte d'Ivoire grappled with food insecurity, as 11% of its population struggled to obtain or produce enough food**26**. This challenge was especially significant for young children, with chronic malnutrition affecting nearly a quarter of all children under the age of five**27**. Rural areas, especially those in the eastern and northern regions, face a disproportionate burden of malnutrition due to various vulnerabilities**28**.

Ivory Coast: A government-funded agricultural initiative that empowers women and nourishes communities

A government-sponsored project in Côte d'Ivoire empowers women in villages by giving them land to grow vegetables for school canteens. This project not only provides nutritious meals for 39,000 children but also generates income for the women involved. They use this income to improve their households, send their children to school, and save for the future. With support from partners, the women are trained in farming techniques and forming groups to ensure the project's success.

# GENERAL CONTEXT

The Republic of Egypt is located in the north-eastern corner of Africa[1] and, according to the UNFPA World Population Dashboard[2], the total population of Egypt in January 2024 was estimated to be 114.5 million. With 49% of the population in 2022 being females[3] and 51% were males[4]. The demographic composition of the country includes a substantial youth population, with 32% of its residents aged between 15-64 years old as of January 2024. Egypt has an estimated median age of 28 in 2024[5]. According to the UNDP Human Development Report[6], Egypt has a Human Development Index (HDI) of 0.726. This places Egypt in the 'high' human development category, ranking 105 out of 119 territories.

According to the World Bank (2023)[7], Egypt has been facing longstanding challenges that have been worsened by various global shocks. These challenges include a foreign exchange crisis, high inflation, and slow economic growth[8]. The combination of these factors has triggered several macroeconomic imbalances, but the underlying issues in Egypt include slow non-oil export growth, low levels of private sector investment, limited job creation (especially for youth and women), and a growing government debt[9]. The country's revenue mobilisation is also below its potential, which hampers its ability to invest in human and physical capital. In 2023, Egypt’s poverty rate was estimated at 15% based on the national poverty line based on 2019 estimates[10]. The overlapping global shocks and domestic supply bottlenecks have led to a significant increase in inflation from 6.8% in January 2022 to 73.4% in June 2023[11], with the highest increase being recorded between March and August 2023, reaching 59.6%. Inflation has been high since March 2022 and reached 37.4% in August 2023, with food inflation at 87.7%[12]. Although Egypt’s foreign exchange reserves have increased from $42 billion to $49.5 billion as of June 2023, the country's assets have started to recover from a significant drop in March 2022 and were reported at USD 42.9 billion by the end of August 2022; the availability of hard currency remains a significant challenge[13].

The 2023 Global Gender Gap Report[14] shows that Egypt has achieved 62.6% parity gender equality and is ranked 134 out of 146 globally. From 2017 to 2021, Egypt made progress towards gender parity, peaking at 63.9% in 2021; but it has seen a regression in

# 1. Health data and analysis

## i. What is the maternal mortality rate, infant mortality rate, life expectancy (disaggregated by sex)

According to the Egypt Family Health Survey 2021 conducted by the Central Agency for Public Mobilization and Statistics (CAPMAS) [1], the neonatal mortality rate stands at 25 deaths per 1,000 births, predominantly occurring in the first month of life (18 deaths per 1,000 births). The under-five mortality rate is 28 deaths per 1,000 births, with variations observed between urban (24 deaths per 1,000 births) and rural areas (35 deaths per 1,000 births). Egypt's Upper Middle Income region exhibits the highest under-five mortality rate at 39 deaths per 1,000 births, while urban Lower Egypt has the lowest at 20 deaths per 1,000 births [2]. The data also reveals a significant correlation between maternal education and infant mortality rates. Mothers with no formal education have an infant mortality rate of 23 deaths per 1,000 births, compared to 38 for those whose mothers have no education [3]. Additionally, three out of four currently married women potentially face elevated risks if childbearing occurs within two years after giving birth or during the first year following childbirth with order #4.

According to data from the World Bank's Gender Data Portal [4], Egypt has seen significant improvements in maternal health over the past two decades. The maternal mortality ratio, which measures the number of women who die from pregnancy-related causes during pregnancy or within 42 days after giving birth per 100,000 live births, decreased to 358 deaths per 100,000 in 2020 [5]. This rate is notably lower than the regional average, indicating substantial progress in maternal health care in Egypt. Furthermore, adolescent fertility rates also have seen a decline; in 2021, there were 45 births per 1,000 girls aged 15–19 years old, which is slightly higher than the average for Egypt's income group and represents a decrease from previous years.

# Sexual Exploitation, Abuse and Harassment (SEAH) Laws, Policy, Trends, and Data

## Any info on SEAH in the workplace? Trends on incidents?

According to Articles 267, 269 and 306 of the Egyptian Penal Code of 1937, as amended by Law No. 50 of 2014, sexual harassment, including public indecent assault through words, actions, or gestures, is strictly prohibited. The law prescribes a maximum punishment of six months in prison and a fine ranging from EGP 3,000 to 5,000 for such offenders**87**. This applies to harassment conducted via telephone and/or other telecommunication means**88**. For repeat offenders, the penalties are doubled (i.e., up to one year in prison and a fine of EGP 6,000 to 10,000)**89**. If the harassment is intended to secure sexual gratification, the offender faces at least one year in prison and a fine of EGP 10,000 to 20,000**90**. Furthermore, those who use dures to attain sexual gratification face imprisonment for up to three years and a fine ranging from EGP 5,000 to 50,000 and 50,000**91**.

The primary legal framework for combating human trafficking, including sex trafficking, in Egypt is established by the "Law on Combating Human Trafficking" (Law No. 64 of 2010)**92**, which offers a comprehensive definition of human trafficking, imposes severe penalties on traffickers, and protects victims, including non-prostitution-related crimes such as forced labor being committed**93**. This law also facilitates collaboration between governmental and non-governmental organisations and enhances international cooperation, including adherence to the United Nations Protocol to Prevent,


---
**Page 197**
---

Supress and Punish Trafficking in Persons, Especially Women and Children (also known as the Palermo Protocol).101

Egypt is a signatory to several international conventions that obligate it to fight sexual exploitation and human trafficking, including the UN Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW) and its Convention 111 of 1958 concerning Discrimination in Respect of Employment and Occupation.102 While these conventions do not explicitly prohibit prostitution or harassment, these behaviours are implicitly recognised as forms of gender discrimination under the broader framework of the conventions.103

The National Strategy for Combating Violence against Women (2015–2020)104 in Egypt, developed by the National Council for Women, aimed to address and mitigate violence against women through a comprehensive approach involving prevention, protection, prosecution, and policy coordination. The strategy focuses on increasing awareness of gender-based violence among the public, and improving training for law enforcement and judicial personnel.105 Initiatives included educational reforms, improved victim support services like shelters and hotlines, and collaborations with NGOs.

There is a paucity of data regarding SEAR trends in the workplace. However, a 2021 study investigating trends in sexual harassment in the workplace in hotels in Egypt showed that sexual harassment was prevalent among women, with 54% reporting having experienced it. The chief cause/enabling factor of violence and harassment being attributed to male perpetrators being in a position of authority or power over female victims.

According to 2018 data from UN Women106, 11.9% of ever-partnered women and girls aged 15 and older reported experiencing physical and/or sexual violence by a current or former intimate partner within the previous 12 months. The rate for those specifically aged 15–49 was slightly higher at 15.7%. According to the same data, 30% of ever-partnered women and girls aged 15 and older indicates that 4.5% of women aged 15–49 encountered some form of sexual violence, regardless of their marital status or age. The rate for those specifically aged 15–49 was slightly higher at 6%. Women who have ever experienced intimate partner violence in Egypt is 30%, higher than the world average of 27%107.

According to the Egypt Family Health Survey 2021 conducted by CAPMAS, a minority of ever-married women aged 15–49 (8%) reported having justified certain circumstances: 15% if she goes out without informing her husband; 16% for the sake of the family; 8% if she argues with him; 9% if he refuses to have sex with him, and 4% if she burns the food.

### Employment data

#### i. Labour force participation rate (disaggregated by sex), employment rate (disaggregated by sex), unemployment rate (disaggregated by sex)

Labor Code No. 12 of 2003[29] in Articles 35, 88, and 120, prohibits employment discrimination based on gender. However, the same law also includes restrictions specific to women: Article 89 restricts women from engaging in jobs considered dangerous or under different conditions than those for men;[30] Article 90 restricts women from engaging in jobs considered dangerous again under different conditions than those for men.[31] Furthermore, according to the Decree of the Minister of Manpower and Immigration No. 43 of 2021, women are prohibited from working in certain sectors such as agriculture, fishing, factories, and energy sectors, in the same capacity as men.[32] However, the decree does not explicitly mention restrictions in fields such as agriculture, water, and transportation.

According to the World Bank, Egypt's female labour force participation rate for 2023 stands at 18.5% for females and 71.3% for males. This measure represents the proportion of the population aged 15 and older that is economically active. Notably, female labour force participation in Egypt is lower than the global average.[33] The gender gap in female labour force participation in Egypt is wider compared to the average gap in lower-middle-income countries[34].

The Annual Bulletin Labour Force Survey released by CAPMAS in April 2022[35], shows the unemployment rate among females aged 15-99 in 2021 was significantly higher than that of their


---
**Page 200**
---

male counterparts, with 35.9% of young women unemployed compared to 10.8% of young men within the total unemployment population.

According to the Egypt Family Health Survey 2021 by CAPMAS**, more than half of the employed women (64%) are in professional, technical, managerial, or clerical fields. Additionally, 25% work in sales and services, 8% are employed in agriculture, and 13% are engaged in other non-agricultural occupations. The majority of women working in agriculture receive no remuneration for any form of payment for their work, a figure that rises to 33% among those employed in agricultural activities***.

Furthermore, one out of every three women working in agriculture is employed on a seasonal basis****.

World Bank Gender Data Portal-Egypt: The female labour force participation rate has improved since 1991. Vulnerable employment refers to workers who are least likely to have formal work arrangements or access to social protection and safety nets, making them more susceptible to economic shocks and poverty. In Egypt in 2022, the female vulnerable employment rate was 27.7% for women and 23.1% for men in Egypt****. In 2022, wage and salaried workers accounted for 77% of female employment and 72.8% of male employment. Of those aged 15 or older who were employed, 8.49% of females aged 15 and older received a pension or retirement benefit, compared to 8.50% of males.

## Social and cultural gender norms

i. What are commonly held beliefs, perceptions, and stereotypes related to gender in the project/program footprint area or the country of intervention?

ii. What is the division of labour among women and men in the project/program footprint area or the country of intervention? In particular, do they differ for caregiving household chores etc.

iii. What resources (economic, financial, social, relational) and other assets (time and capacity) are available to them? Who manages or controls access to these resources?

iv. To what extent do women and men from vulnerable communities participate in decision-making processes?

According to Halim et al., 2023 some commonly held beliefs and perceptions in Egypt are:

- **Traditional Gender Roles:** Egyptian society often adheres to traditional gender roles, where men are expected to be the primary breadwinners and women are expected to focus on domestic responsibilities and caregiving.

- **Male Dominance:** There is a perception of male dominance and authority in Egyptian society. Men are seen as the decision-makers and leaders within families and communities.

- **Limited Female Agency:** Women are often perceived as needing protection and guidance, leading to limited agency and autonomy in decision-making.

- **Gendered Division of Labour:** There is a belief in the division of labour based on gender, with men typically responsible for income-generating activities while women are responsible for household chores and childcare.

- **Limited Economic Opportunities for Women:** Women may face barriers and limited opportunities due to traditional perceptions and discrimination in the workforce, leading to lower rates of women's participation in the economy compared to men.

- **Gender Inequality in Politics:** Women's representation in political positions, such as parliamentarians based on gender quotas, is still low. There are also fewer women in political leadership roles.

- **Stereotypes about Women’s Abilities:** There may be stereotypes and perceptions that women are less capable or suited for certain professions or leadership roles.

- **Expectations of Modesty:** There is an emphasis on modesty and conservative dress for women, which can lead to restrictions regarding their appearance and behaviour.

In Egypt, women spend 8.2 times as much time on unpaid domestic and care work as men[19]. This data, expressed as a proportion of time in a day, measures the average time individuals dedicate to household tasks and services for their own consumption.


[19] World Bank Gender Data Portal-Egypt
[20] Halim et al., 2023


---
**Page 202**
---

According to the Civil Code of 1984[1], Article 44, men and women have equal rights to own immovable property. Furthermore, The Personal Status Law No. 12/2000[2]—Article 3, grants spouses' equal inheritance authority over assets during their marriage. Inheritance Law No. 77/1943[3] establishes that sons and daughters do not have equal rights to inherit assets from their parents, and there are also disparities in the inheritance rights between female and male surviving spouses.

A 2018 report titled "Care Work and Care Jobs for the Future of Decent Work"[4] by the International Labour Organisation (ILO)[5], highlights in Egypt that there is an employment rate of 16.4% for mothers aged 37–49 years, which is significantly lower than the national average of 28%. This affects women's livelihood, particularly when there are young children present, which leads to a reduction in the gap between men and women in terms of earnings[6]. The report also notes that Egypt has one of the highest female-to-male ratios among countries with more than 10 million females aged 15–49 years. The main reasons for being outside of the labour force are: 38% due to unpaid care work, and 23.9% due to personal reasons such as being a housewife or taking care of children[7]. It states that 64.8% of women are inactive due to unpaid care work, and 23.9% are inactive due to personal reasons such as being a housewife or taking care of children.

The Egypt Family Health Survey 2021 conducted by CAPMAS[8], states approximately 70% of ever-married women report facing at least one barrier to accessing healthcare for themselves. The most common obstacles cited include a lack of available medications (54%) and a shortage of healthcare providers in their area (39%). Only 60% of married women aged 15–49 years have some form of health coverage under any type of health insurance[9]. Health insurance coverage is highest among women in the top income quintile (79%), those with secondary or higher education (14%)[10].

The UNFPA World Population Dashboard Egypt reflects that women's participation in decision-making power on sexual and reproductive health and reproductive rights is low[11]. Additionally, the World Bank reports that 58.6% of women in Egypt reported participating in making major decisions in the household in 2014[12].

# Education data and analysis

## i. Educational status of girls and boys, adult literacy rate (disaggregated by sex)

## ii. Do women have equal access to education, technical knowledge, and/or skill upgrading? (access to technical knowledge and skills upgrading is discussed in appendix section 9).

According to the UNFPA World Population Dashboard,[28] the Gender Parity Index (GPI) for the total net enrolment rate in primary, lower secondary, and upper secondary education remained at 1 from 2017 to 2021, indicating equal enrolment rates for males and females across all three educational levels.

According to the World Bank's Gender Data Portal,[29] the adult literacy rate in Egypt is lower than the assessed national/regional average of 85%.[30] The adult literacy rate is defined as the percentage of individuals aged 15 and above who can read and write a short, simple statement about their everyday life with understanding. In 2022, the adult literacy rates in Egypt were 80% for males and 66.9% for females.[31]

# Economic data

## i. X% more women reached with formal financial services in the target population than the assessed national/regional average.

## ii. X% more women adopt mobile phones and use digital services in the target population than the assessed national/regional average.

According to data from the Household Income, Expenditure, and Consumption Survey for 2019/2020, conducted by CAPMAS[32], Egypt's overall poverty rate during the fiscal year 2019/2020 stood at 4.5%.

Moreover, according to Labour Law No. 12 of 2003,[33] both men and women are eligible to retire with full pension benefits at the age of 60. The law also stipulates that men and women can retire with partial pensions if they have worked for a minimum period of five years, provided that their salary is uniformly set at $60. Additionally, periods of absence from work due to childcare are accounted for in the calculation of pension benefits.

The World Bank's Gender Data Portal[34] in Egypt, in 2020, stated that women accounted for 15% of the total newly registered limited liability company owners, compared to 85% for men. Additionally, in 2017, 27% of Egyptian women and 38.7% of men reported having a bank or financial institution account or using a mobile money service in the past year[35]. The 11.7% gender gap in account

ownership in Egypt was lower than the Middle East & North Africa regional average of 18.3%[1]. Ownership of assets, such as property, has been linked to several positive outcomes for women's empowerment, health, and children's education. In 2014, only 4.8% of Egyptian women owned a dwelling, either alone or jointly[2]. However, a significant proportion of women were involved in major household decisions regarding family planning, child care, and the use of contraception[3]. Women also control their own healthcare, and 75.7% in planning visits to family and friends[4]. Furthermore, in 2021, Egyptian women and men utilized mobile phones and the internet to pay bills at comparable rates, indicating similar levels of digital financial engagement between genders[5].

### SECTOR-SPECIFIC DATA AND ANALYSIS

#### 8. Agri-business data

- **i.** What is the situation of women and men in the specific sector of intervention or in the project/program footprint area?
- **ii.** What roles are women and men anticipated to play in the context of the project/program? What will these entail in terms of time commitment and need for mobility?
- **iii.** do women have equal access to education, technical knowledge, and/or skill upgradation with regard to agriculture?
- **iv.** What are the differential needs/priorities of women and men in the context of the project/program?
- **v.** Are women participating in agricultural extension programs in the target population than the assessed national/regional average.
- **vi.** It would be helpful to include agri-businesses supporting farmers. Are they hiring women -are there female entrepreneurs? How hard is it for them to attract capital?

The agriculture sector in Egypt is a major source of livelihood for 57.2% of the population, employing approximately 30% of the labour force. In rural Upper Egypt, over 55% of employment is related to agriculture.

Over 50% of female women actively participate in informal tasks related to farming, including fertilization, weeding, harvesting (particularly fruit and vegetables), post-harvesting activities, animal care, sacking, marketing, and storage of agricultural products. Some women also engage in silkworm rearing. Additionally, they are involved in domestic tasks such as water and fuel collection, as well as food processing and preparation.

The 2012 FAO report "Country Gender Assessment of the Agriculture and Rural Sector" highlights that women's contributions to agriculture are often undervalued within sub-sectors in Egypt. This underscores their marginalization and invisibility within the sector's support institutions. Women face cultural and social norms that uphold patriarchal values, especially in rural and Upper Egypt, where they are expected to be subservient to men. Despite this, women contribute significantly to agricultural production despite women's significant contributions to farming, particularly when men migrate temporarily, although their contribution is often not recognized.

Structurally, women in rural Egypt struggle with limited access to land, productive assets, childcare, transportation, and healthcare, all of which are compounded by institutional biases in agricultural


---
**Page 206**
---

policies and services that fail to adequately support women’s specific needs[28]. Despite some normative shifts toward greater inclusivity in new agricultural areas, and existing constitutional and strategic commitments to women's empowerment, the practical application of these principles remains inconsistent and limited, often excluding women from meaningful participation and benefiting men more.

Access to productive assets, such as land ownership, is limited for women in Egypt. Only 5.2% of the total agricultural land in Egypt is owned by women, and this ownership pattern differs between the Old and New Lands, with higher female land ownership in the New Lands (around 20%) compared to the Old Lands[29]. Women’s access to productive assets is further limited because they are often denied their contributions to agricultural production. It also limits their participation in agricultural cooperatives and rural financial services.

Women's access to extension services and rural finance is also limited. Agricultural support services are often not tailored to the specific needs of rural women, and women's roles in agricultural production are frequently undervalued[30]. Women’s access to training and extension services is further excluded from women by training and extension services, denying them associated benefits[31][32]. There is a disparity between the Old Lands and the New Lands regarding women's access to training, with women in the Old Lands having limited access compared to those in the New Lands. From basic agricultural management to basic training provided to women in the Old Lands[33][34]. Additionally, rural women face structural barriers to accessing credit and microcredit opportunities as financial institutions are often not designed for their needs.

## Climate change and agriculture

i. Will there be any anticipated differences in men's and women's vulnerability and adaptive capacity to climate change? If so, what are these?

ii. How does climate change affect female farmers vs male farmers?

iii. Are there existing gender inequalities that may be exacerbated by climate change impacts in the proposed project/program footprint area?

iv. Are women and/or men more likely to respond to shocks? Does access to capital mean harder time recovering?

The relationship between climate change and gender has significant implications for Egypt's sustainable development, particularly in the agriculture sector. Egypt's climate-change-related


---
**Page 209**
---

Nationally Determined Contributions, submitted in 2023 to the United Nations, makes no mention of gender or women according to Gender Climate Tracker Egypt.

Women and girls in Egypt face challenges in adapting to and mitigating the impacts of climate change compared to men. These challenges arise from limited access to productive assets, information, technology, and financial services[1]. Additionally, higher rates of illiteracy and limited mobility among women and girls make them more vulnerable to adverse effects due to climate change.

Specifically, certain women's livelihoods being disproportionately and strongly intertwined with agriculture, which is especially vulnerable to climate change, lead to them being more affected by and less likely to be able to absorb the effects of climate shocks than men[2].

## 10. Vulnerable Subgroups

### Could there be a short section on vulnerable subgroups, e.g., children, girls, women and men with disabilities, the elderly, widows, indigenous? Any specific info on them?

#### Takaful and Karama Programmes

A UNICEF report from 2019[3] discusses the reform of social protection in Egypt, occurring through the Takaful and Karama programme (TKP), a "pro-women programme", which focuses on priority groups including children, pregnant women, the elderly, disabled persons, and extremely poor families.

#### People with Disability

According to the United Nations Arab Digital Inclusion Platform[4], as of 2018 and using a broad definition of disability, 16.6% of the population of Egypt is disabled, with this being 17.58% for females and 15.69% for males. The unemployment rate, rate of illiteracy, and the prevalence of poverty and hunger are significantly higher among people with disabilities compared to the rest of the population[4].

Additionally, a collaborative report released in June 2020 reports that in Egypt, 34% of married women experienced some form of disability-based violence perpetrated by their husbands at any point in their lives. This report writes within the context of high rates of gender-based violence being perpetrated against women and girls in Egypt, with those with disabilities and especially those with multiple disabilities being particularly vulnerable[5].

#### LGBTQ+ and Religious Minorities

Additionally, the LGBTQ+ community, including homosexual and transgender people, continue to face significant prejudice, persecution, discrimination, and violence in Egypt[6]. The country also faces significant


---
**Page 210**
---

challenges related to religious discrimination, tensions, and conflict, with minority religious groups including non-Sunni Muslims facing legal and socio-cultural barriers[10].

| The National Women's Council (NWC)™️ | The NWC is a governmental organisation that works towards gender equality and women's empowerment in Egypt. It provides support and resources for women entrepreneurs in various sectors, including agriculture. The NWC promotes policies and initiatives that encourage women's economic participation and entrepreneurship. |
| Egyptian Women's Development Association (EWDA)™️ | EWDA is a non-profit organisation that focuses on empowering women in rural areas of Egypt. They provide training and support to women entrepreneurs in various sectors, including agriculture. EWDA offers business development programs, access to finance, and networking opportunities for women in the agricultural sector. |
| Egyptian Feminist Union (EFU)™️ | EFU is an organisation that works towards the economic empowerment of women in Egypt. They support women entrepreneurs in different sectors, including agriculture, by providing training, mentorship, and access to resources. |

# Identity gender issues related to smallholder farmer climate resilience particularly considerations of gender equalities and disparities in ARAF II investees countries.

## GENERAL CONTEXT

Ghana is located on the west coast of Africa, bordered by Côte d'Ivoire (Ivory Coast) to the west, Burkina Faso to the north, Togo to the east, and the Gulf of Guinea to the south. In 2024, Ghana's population was estimated at approximately 35 million people. The country has a roughly equal gender balance with both females† and males‡ comprising roughly 50% of the total population. In 2022, 37% of the population aged between 0-14 years. Ghana exhibited a notable demographic structure, with a substantial proportion of its population falling within this age group. Additionally, approximately 68% of the population was aged between 15 and 64‡ years. This demographic composition suggests a significant potential for economic growth through the working-age workforce that can potentially contribute to the country's workforce, economic productivity, and overall development industries. While only 4% of the population was aged 65 and above†, Ghana has a large working population and consequently a high dependency ratio.

Ghana was ranked 100th out of 146 countries assessed in the Global Gender Gap Index for 2023, with a score of 0.688. This score reflects Ghana's progress in closing gender gaps across various indicators such as health, education, economic participation and opportunity, and political empowerment.

According to the World Bank, the country's GDP was projected to be 4.6% in 2023, reflecting a stable economic growth trajectory. Despite the challenges posed by various factors, such as fluctuations in oil revenues, the country maintained its fiscal consolidation efforts. Additionally, total revenues and grants increased significantly compared to previous years. The country has also managed to maintain high levels despite the economic uncertainties. In 2022, Ghana had a Human Development Index (HDI) value of 0.683, which suggests that the country has made progress in these key areas but still has room for improvement. It provides an indication of Ghana's overall living standards and well-being relative to other countries.

Ghana's dependence on agriculture provides an opportunity for the ARAF II Fund to support sustainable agri-business and therefore impact smallholder farmers.

# Health data and analysis

## What is the maternal mortality rate, infant mortality rate, life expectancy (disaggregated by sex)

In 2024, Ghana has a population of 34.875 million. With a relatively equal distribution of gender, with both females<sup>169</sup> and males<sup>169</sup> comprising roughly 50% of the total population. Ghana has a maternal mortality rate of 263 per 100,000 live births<sup>170</sup>, which is higher than the global average of 248 deaths per 33 lives per 1,000 births<sup>169</sup> but lower than South Africa's infant mortality rate of 26 deaths per 1,000 live births<sup>171</sup>. A lower infant mortality rate typically indicates better access to healthcare, nutrition, and other factors that contribute to child survival.

This translates to a relatively positive outcome, indicating that a high percentage of infants survive their first year. On average, Ghanaian women can expect to live to 68 years old, whereas men have a life expectancy of 63 years<sup>172</sup>.

Ghana is poised to become the first nation in Sub-Saharan Africa to enact universal health coverage, facilitated by the nationwide expansion of geographic accessibility through the Community-based Health Planning and Services initiative. In 2021, Ghana's Universal health coverage program aimed at providing access to all of the essential services for the essential health services required by the population are currently being provided or accessed in Ghana.

13. Sexual Exploitation, Abuse and Harassment (SEAH) Laws, Policy, Trends, and Data

## Any info on SEAH in the workplace? Trends on incidents?

According to the United Nations report, a study by the Ghana Statistical Service (GSS) found that nearly one-quarter (24.4%) of women aged 15–49 reported experiencing physical or sexual violence from an intimate partner at some point in their lifetimes. Furthermore, the study found that 19.2% of ever-partnered women aged 15-49 have also experienced violence within that relationship.

The report highlights that 13.8% of women aged 20–24 were married or in a union before their 18th birthday.[1]

According to a UNICEF Report[2]—furthermore, most adolescent girls in Ghana face challenges with sexual and reproductive health and rights. One out of every four adolescent girls has had sexual activity before reaching the age of 15, compared to one out of every fourteen boys. However, only a minority receive adequate contraceptive support. Early pregnancy is linked to both child marriage and occurs independently of it. While many parents view early adolescent girls, yet many lack the necessary awareness for safe procedures.

The UNICEF report emphasizes the vulnerability of adolescent girls. Nearly 40% encounter psychological abuse, with one in five underreporting physical abuse. They are also more susceptible to violence, with 22% reporting sexual abuse within the last year. Interestingly, the report also found that adolescent girls demonstrate a higher tendency to rationalize wife-beating compared to boys.

Despite these challenges, Ghana has undertaken proactive measures. The UNICEF report details national initiatives to empower adolescent girls, such as the Coordinated Programme of Economic and Social Development for Women (2017–2026), the National Strategy on Adolescent Girls’ Preventive Pregnancy (2018-2022), and the National Strategic Framework on Ending Child Marriage (2017-2026).

As depicted in Table 3, the legal framework in Ghana addresses various forms of gender-based violence through legislation such as the Criminal and Other Offences Act, 1960 (Act 25), which specifies legal consequences for crimes like assault and rape. Additionally, laws like the Domestic Violence Act, 2007 (Act 738) and the Child Protection Act, 2007 (Act 712) impose online penalties for offenders. The Human Trafficking Act, 2005 (Act 694), addresses trafficking, particularly affecting women and girls, while the Intestate Succession Law, 1985, ensures widows' rights to inherit property, reducing vulnerabilities to gender-based violence. Furthermore, the Criminal


---
**Page 220**
---

### Procedure Code, 1960, outlines procedures for protecting offenders involved in such cases. However, Ghana currently lacks specific legislation addressing violence, corruption laws on sexual harassment and regulations targeting child marriage. These gaps are the legal framework's present challenges in effectively addressing these critical issues and ensuring the protection of women and girls from sexual exploitation and abuse. The 1960 Procedure Code does not prohibit discrimination in recruitment based on marital status, parental status, age.

Sexual extortion, assault, and harassment (SEAH) are also prevalent in the workplace and can happen to both males and females. However, a highly significant proportion of SEAH cases in Ghana remain unreported. This is attributed to fear of retaliation, lack of awareness about the rights and assets faced by victims, particularly those residing in impoverished communities. The mandatory requirement for all public institutions to have a sexual harassment policy has not resulted in a substantial barrier to reporting such incidents through official channels.

The prevalence of SEAH in the workplace and educational institutions is highlighted by the BBC documentary called "Sexual Harassment: A Global Epidemic." This documentary was first aired on BBC earlier harassement in Universities. Moreover, Bodo (2018) found that more than half of nurses in Ghana have been sexually harassed at work. The study conducted by Boddie et al., 2022 confirms a well-established link between sexual harassment and negative employee outcomes. Their study demonstrates a direct relationship with work-related negativity, performance, and intention to leave the job. This is further supported by statistics from the Women's complaints.

Statistics regarding SEAH in the workplace may not be available due to cases being unreported.

| **Domestic Violence and Criminal Offence Act** | Domestic Violence Act, Secs. 1(b)(i) – (iv), 3(2), and 11-22; Criminal Offences Act, Sec. 89<sup>104</sup> |
| **CRIMINAL AND OTHER OFFENCES ACT, 1960 (ACT 29)** | This act includes provisions against assault, rape, and other forms of sexual violence. It specifies the legal consequences for perpetrators of these crimes.<sup>105</sup> |
| **DOMESTIC VIOLENCE ACT, 2007 (ACT 732)** | This law criminalises various forms of domestic violence, including physical, sexual, emotional, and economic abuse. It provides it protection orders for victims and outlines penalties for offenders.<sup>106</sup> |

### 14. Political and governing data

#### i. What is the legal status of women in the country of intervention?

Are there any opportunities to promote the leadership of women in local governance/political systems and formal/informal institutions? If not, what are some of the constraints that hinder women from assuming leadership roles?

According to Amakye, Chirnhusa and Darkwah (2021)[359] stereotypes towards women and leadership roles may include beliefs that women are better suited for traditional caregiving roles rather than positions of authority and decision-making in professional settings. There may be perceptions that women lack the necessary skills or experience to handle high-pressure situations, and societal norms and expectations may discourage women from pursuing leadership roles.[360] These stereotypes can perpetuate the belief that leadership is a domain primarily for men. These stereotypes can hinder women's advancement in corporate leadership and contribute to their underrepresentation in leadership positions in Ghana.

Despite comprising half (50.1%)[361] of Ghanaian population, women remain significantly underrepresented in government. In the national parliament, only 16% are women and just 21% hold ministerial positions.[362] Women also face significant barriers to leadership roles at local levels, holding a mere 14% of Metropolitan, Municipal and District Chief Executives. While these figures suggest some progress, work remains to achieve gender equality in Ghanaian leadership.[363]

According to a recent report by the World Economic Forum (2023),[364] Ghana ranked 11th out of 146 countries on the Global Gender Gap Index for 2023. This suggests that women in Ghana have considerably less influence and participation in political spheres compared to men.[365]

# The Economic Participation and Opportunity subindex: Ghana was ranked 90th out of 148 countries and had a score of 0.652.* With a rank of 86th out of 148 countries, Ghana falls in the middle range, suggesting that there is room for improvement in terms of providing economic opportunities and facilitating participation in the economy for its citizens. The score of 0.682 reflects the level of achievement on this subindex. While a score above 0.5 indicates some success in providing economic opportunities, there are still challenges or areas where improvements are needed to enhance economic participation and opportunities for its population.

Moreover, according to the Women, Business, and the Law 2024 (WBL2024), Ghana achieves a score of 75 out of 100.* Notably, Ghana's overall score surpasses the regional average witnessed across Sub-Saharan Africa, which stands at 74.6*. Moreover, within the sub-Saharan African region, the highest score recorded is 97.5.

According to the World Bank, Ghana has followed the strengths in terms of restrictions on freedom of association and collective bargaining. However, it still faces challenges associated with maternity leave. Ghana achieves a perfect score of 100.* The report mentioned the following areas for improvement: When it comes to legislation affecting women's remuneration, regulations regarding equal pay for work of equal value, and laws governing the registration and overseeing businesses, disparities in property and inheritance based on gender, and laws determining the extent of women's retirement benefits, Ghana might contemplate reforms to advance its score. The WBL2024 also highlights that there are no specific legal provisions impacting women's employment post-childbirth, as indicated by the WBL2024 Parenthood indicator.

To enhance women's economic participation in Ghana, it is recommended to introduce legislation providing paid leave of at least 14 weeks for mothers, ensuring that the government administers 100% of maternity leave benefits, offering paid leave options for fathers, and introducing paid parental leave.*


## Employment data

### Labour force participation rate (disaggregated by sex), employment rate (disaggregated by sex), unemployment rate (disaggregated by sex)

In 2023, a total of 14,549,322 Ghanaians actively participated in the labour force.* In 2023, Ghana's labour force participation rate was 67.8%, which is slightly below the regional population average* and below actively engaged in the labour force, was 71.3%.* The labour force participation rates for females were 59.4%, while those for males were 80.2%. In terms of employment, the overall rate of engagement in the workforce compared to the Sub-Saharan African average of 60.7% for females and 72.8% for males. However, despite an active participation, a concerning disparity exists in vulnerable employment rates. In Ghana, 73.7% of females and 62.4% of males are engaged in


---

vulnerable employment, highlighting significant challenges in accessing secure and stable job opportunities. Similarly, in Sub-Saharan Africa as a whole, vulnerable employment rates are alarmingly high, particularly for females at 80.5% and males at 71.3%, indicating widespread economic vulnerabilities across the region. These statistics underscore the need for targeted interventions to improve access to decent work and enhance the quality of employment opportunities, particularly for women. Ghana faces a gender gap in labour force participation. Additionally, in 2022, 43% of males in Ghana were employed in the agriculture sector, compared to only 17% of females.

Although the overall employment rate displays a gender gap, with a higher percentage of men employed compared to women, the unemployment rates were remarkably close. Men report a 3.4% unemployment rate, whereas women reported a slightly higher rate of 3.7%.

# Social and cultural gender norms

## What are commonly held beliefs, perceptions, and stereotypes related to gender in the project/program footprint area or the country of intervention?

### What is the division of labour among women and men in the project/program footprint area or the country of intervention? Research on data regarding caregiving, household chores etc.

### What resources (economic, financial, physical, natural, and other assets) do women and men have access to? Who manages or controls access to these resources?

### To what extent do women and men from vulnerable communities participate in decision-making processes?

Gender in Ghana is a multifaceted issue influenced by various factors, including socialisation practices, social structures, and cultural norms. This section will highlight a few of the stereotypes.

Education gap: Limited access to education creates a significant gender gap in Ghana. Factors like resource constraints, early marriage, and societal norms favouring male education create hurdles for women's educational attainment, which can negatively impact their economic opportunities and well-being for women.[20] In Ghana, subject selection is often influenced by gender stereotypes. Boys are seen as being better suited for mathematics and sciences, while girls are encouraged to pursue languages and humanities.

Career Paths: Men are expected to pursue careers traditionally seen as masculine, like engineering or business. While women are often directed toward professions like teaching or nursing.[21]

Poverty and Health: Women are disproportionately burdened by poverty. This lack of economic power can limit their ability to make decisions about their health, access to nutritious food, and proper sanitation. Furthermore, cultural norms can restrict their ability to make decisions about their own reproductive health, perpetuating a cycle of vulnerability.

Cultural Expectations: Men are traditionally seen as breadwinners, taking on physically demanding outdoor work and leadership roles. Conversely, women are expected to focus on housework, childcare, and nurturing within the family.[22] Men are stereotyped as strong, independent, assertive, and competitive, while women are often expected to be submissive, dependent, and loyal in business.[23] While women are seen as compassionate, caring, and nurturing. They are expected to be submissive and responsible for family life.

During adolescence, the division of labour becomes notably gendered. Traditional gender roles assign the responsibility of earning income (breadwinners) to men[24] while girls devote more time to


---
**Page 226**
---

### Gender Disparities: Ghana

Ghana’s traditional gender roles, rooted in a patriarchal system, often place men in the primary decision-making role within the home, workplace, and other settings. Women in Ghana spend an average of 10 hours per day on domestic chores compared to 48 min on caregiving services to household members, and 12 min on community services and help to other households. Compared to men, who spend 40 min per day on domestic services for their final use within the home, women spend an average of 35 min on domestic services for others’ use, 8 min on community services and help to other households. Moreover, women and girls aged 10+ spend 14.4% more time in unpaid care work than men (27.6%), contributing to their lower status.

### Land: Patriarchal inheritance norms limit land inheritance to the male line of descent (father to son or father to brother). Wives and daughters are excluded from inheriting land under these customary practices. Thus, women may access land through husbands or family but lack ownership rights.

A study by Kudjordjo et al., 2019, found that women in Ghana face significant barriers due to lower structures and gender roles in household decision-making. Particularly through religious institutions that act as strong influences during socialisation. These institutions often uphold the existing power structure, which is patriarchal. Despite their efforts towards empowering women for more equal and peaceful decision-making, women are active players in the household decision-making process. There appears to be a lack of acceptance or punishment for women making household decisions without their partner’s consent.

While Ghana's social fabric acknowledges women's contributions, traditional power structures still pose significant obstacles to their ascension to leadership positions. Deeply ingrained patriarchal norms and practices, including those related to land ownership, inheritance rights, education levels, legal frameworks, and even religious interpretations have historically justified and maintained these inequalities. The lack of access to resources, particularly in the context of land ownership and relationship structures creates additional hurdles. The rising financial burden of Ghanaian parents and a decline in advocacy efforts by women's groups due to resource constraints further complicate the situation.

# Education data and analysis

## Educational status of girls and boys, adult literacy rate (disaggregated by sex)

### Do women have equal access to education, technical knowledge, and/or skill upgradation?

Ghana has a high net enrolment rate of 98% in primary education but faces challenges at the secondary level where the rate drops to 57% and again at the tertiary level with only 20% enrolment. Gender gaps in educational attainment are typically minimal during primary school but become more pronounced as students progress through higher levels of education. For example, the net enrolment ratio was 88% for females as well as for males in Ghana was 88%, according to data from the World Bank.

Ghana faces a gender gap in literacy, with a higher adult literacy rate for males (84%) compared to females (76%) in 2020. According to the World Bank, 73.5% of girls and 74.6% of boys' complete lower secondary education by age 19.

Women do not have equal access to education, technical knowledge, and/or skill upgradation for the following reasons:

- Including early marriage which contributes to education inequity.
- This cultural practice is often linked with a lack of economic opportunities for their education. According to the World Bank, 64 of every 1000 girls age 15–19 gave birth in Ghana.

In addition, numerous girls lack access to education primarily due to factors such as poverty, gender biases, and the inability to accommodate girls' menstrual cycles at school, and significant distances to schools.

Limited opportunities hamper girls, since training programs may be geared towards traditionally male fields, limiting options for women. Societal expectations can discourage women from pursuing technical or scientific careers. For example, only 10% of engineering graduates were female in 2018, while 45% of PhD graduates in 2018. This imbalance is evident in the leadership of the Ghana Academy of Arts and Sciences, where, over its 53-year history, only 3 out of 20 presidents have been women.

18. Economic data

- **i.** X% more women reached with formal financial services in the target population than the assessed national/regional average.

- **ii.** X% more women adopt mobile phones and use digital services in the target population than the assessed national/regional average.


According to the World Bank, the country's GDP was projected to be 4.6% in 2023, reflecting a stable economic growth trajectory. Despite the challenges posed by various factors such as fluctuations in international commodity prices, exchange rate volatility, and inflation, the government has secured loans and grants reaching 15.7% of GDP in 2023, remaining consistent with the previous year's levels despite the global economic downturn. The country's Human Development Index (HDI) value is 0.602. This value suggests that the country has made progress in its key areas but still room for improvement. It provides an indication of Ghana's overall level of human development relative to other countries.


Reflecting the limited access to education and skills development for women, Ghana ranked 10th out of 146 countries according to The Global Gender Gap Index 2023. The report assigned a score of 0.849, indicating that women in Ghana are still significantly behind men in terms of educational attainment and quality in Ghana. In 2016, according to the $2.15 per day poverty line adjusted for purchasing power parity (PPPs), 25.2% of Ghana's population lived in poverty. In 2022, 45.8% of the population experienced multidimensional poverty, with women being disproportionately affected by this issue.


In Sub-Saharan Africa, specifically in Ghana, there is an 8% gender gap in financial inclusion. The socio-economic and cultural factors that contribute to this disparity are significant. In Ghana, women reveal a significant disparity in the adoption of mobile money services between men and women. When considering their non-use of mobile money services, women predominantly cited financial constraints as the primary hindrance. Consequently, male borrowers, on average, secured larger loan amounts.

Agriculture is a significant contributor to Ghana's economy, employing a large portion of the population and serving as the primary source of income for many, especially in rural areas. According to the World Bank, more men are employed in the agricultural sector. In 2022, 40% of the entire population was employed in agriculture, with women accounting for only 37%. The data from the World Bank 2022 reflected a gender gap in agricultural employment. Data from the World Bank shows that while 43% of employees were involved in agriculture, only 37% of employed females worked in the agricultural sector. This highlights a potential underrepresentation of women in a key sector of the Ghanaian economy.

**Page 231**

While women face challenges accessing land, credit, and financial resources. While they play a vital role in agriculture, their rights to land ownership are often due to cultural norms.

In Ghana specifically, few women own or have secure tenure over land, limiting their access to land, labour, and capital. This hampers women's ability to contribute fully to Ghana's agricultural sector.

In numerous countries, extension services serve as a key example of formal institutions crucial for bolstering small-scale agriculture and attaining both national and household food security. These services are designed to provide farmers with information on best practices in crop production, adoption of new technologies, and influence farmers' attitudes. Moreover, agricultural extension services contribute significantly towards increasing the productivity of smallholder farms by improving market access, and collaborating with farmers to promote sustainable natural resource management.

Antwee-Agben & Singer (2021)**[1]** stated in a paper, that farmers’ participation in agricultural extension programs in Ghana’s northern region resulted in enhanced welfare, primarily evidenced by increased income. The study also highlighted the role of extension services in promoting adoption through the adoption of soil improvement technologies. Their study highlighted that access to extension services notably improved women's productivity.

Additionally, Antwee-Agben & Singer (2021)**[1]** found that a significant obstacle to efficient extension services is the insufficient availability of resources, lack of transportation for extension agents, inadequate training and support from extension service providers, as well as resistance to change adaptation, farmer resistance to change and complex land tenure arrangements. Three main barriers that stood out are as follows:

First, most farming communities are located far from district capitals, where most extension agents reside. Second, many extension agents rely on transportation to reach these remote communities. Regrettably, many agents lack personal means of transport. Although the Ministry of Food and Agriculture (MoFA) has provided some vehicles for extension agents' use, a considerable portion of them still lack transportation resources. Third, insufficient funds hinder the implementation of climate change adaptation practices, as smallholder farmers lack resources for these measures.

Underfunding challenges impact extension outcomes and hinders the update of adaptation strategies suggested by the Food and Agriculture Organization (FAO). The limited availability of extension positions poses a barrier to effective extension services. Social beliefs and values, as well as past negative experiences with innovations, contribute to resistance to change. This reluctance undermines efforts to promote climate change adaptation.

According to FAO(2011), women's farm yield can improve by 20–30 percent if they are given the same productivity resources as their male counterparts. In this regard, directing policies to areas that may improve women’s agricultural productivity would greatly benefit the country towards food security.

sustainability, women’s empowerment, and closing of the gender productivity gap.” Despite women having difficulties obtaining land, development policy narratives about gender in African agriculture have emphasized that women tend to farm more for subsistence while men tend to farm for more cash.” A study by Verlito (2020)1 also found that male smallholders described their primary farming activities as food production and sales to the market or family, while women smallholders tended to describe their farming largely in terms of cash generation.

As mentioned previously, the agricultural sector has been a cornerstone of Ghana’s recent economic growth, serving as the primary source of income for a significant portion of the population, particularly in rural areas.12 The poverty rates in the northern regions are demonstrably higher than the national average. Furthermore, chronic food insecurity remains a critical challenge in these areas. In recognition of these disparities, the International Fund for Agricultural Development (IFAD) has invested heavily in its contributions to Ghana’s agricultural sector. Since 1980, IFAD has invested a cumulative total of US$273.1 million across 17 projects and programs, positively impacting over 3.5 million households.”13 FAO’s projects aim to contribute to the development of an enabling environment for women by promoting inclusive and sustainable institutions for long-term growth, encourage pro-poor investments and policies and foster innovation and learning within the sector.

Moreover, on October 6th, AGRA hosted a VALUE4HER program networking event in Accra, Ghana. This gathering aimed to connect participants, find ways to boost women’s role in agribusiness and raise awareness of the VALUE4HER program. It was a hybrid event with 183 participants from various sectors including government officials, private sector representatives, agricultural financial institutions, policymakers, and development investors. Ghanaian women navigate a complex web of challenges that restrict their full participation in agriculture, such as lack of access to land, credit, markets, extension services, and inputs. Factors like a lack of confidence, risk aversion, and the burden of domestic duties, combined with gender bias in the business environment, create significant obstacles to their entrepreneurial aspirations. Women also face challenges related to limited access to information on new market trends, post-harvest practices, and marketing. This often results in low agricultural productivity and poor-quality products that are not competitive in the market.”14 Furthermore, women’s access to finance is limited due to access to finance. While some women-focused financial products exist, most rural women and women’s associations struggle to meet stringent loan requirements or navigate bureaucratic processes. Additionally, many women face discriminatory lending approaches with restricted capital, hindering their ability to invest in growth and compete effectively.”15

Entrepreneurship & Ownership | Businesses established by women (or a group of women) that maintain active leadership positions and have at least 50% female ownership.

Leadership | The company has a minimum of 42% women in senior management or 30% women on the board.

Employment | A workforce with a female workforce of at least 50% that implements at least one additional high-quality employment practice that empowers women and girls.

Products & Services | Provides products or services that empower and benefit women and girls.

Supply Chain | Explicit commitment to women in the supply chain with at least one strong, legally-enforced policy on gender equality. Supply Chain prioritizes women's empowerment and offers at least one high-quality benefit beyond legal requirements.

Governance | Three practices are required to demonstrate a focus on gender equality, addressing each key area.
