## Climate change and agriculture

i. Will there be any anticipated differences in men's and women's vulnerability and adaptive capacity to climate change? If so, what are these?

ii. How does climate change affect female farmers vs male farmers?

iii. Are there existing gender inequalities that may be exacerbated by climate change impacts in the region?

iv. Any research on how women respond to shocks? Does less access to capital mean harder time recovering?

Ghana, situated along the Atlantic Ocean and sharing borders with Togo, Cote d'Ivoire, and Burkina Faso, spans a total land area of 235,460 square kilometres and is also positioned near the equator. The landscape consists mainly of savannahs and forests, but about one-third of Ghana's land has been converted as irrigated agricultural land. The country's heavy reliance on agriculture poses challenges to livelihoods, particularly worsening from the coastal regions to the Northern Savannah. Furthermore,

* *2X Criteria — 2X Challenge


---
**Page 234**
---

the issue of climate change poses a threat, promising disruptions to various sectors including the electricity system, cash crop production, urban migration, livelihoods of smallholder farmers, and the coastlines.

The negative impact of climate change has been widely documented by policy makers and researchers such as the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC). Evidence shows that the direct threats to societies, individuals, and classes are more prone to the threats and impacts of climate change.49

The impacts of climate change are often felt more acutely by women than by men. This disparity arises from a combination of factors including gender inequality in access to resources such as financial capital, entrenched cultural norms and social beliefs, along with existing political and social discrimination, disadvantage women. Their lower socio-economic standing often translates to limited decision-making power over household resources.

In Ghana specifically, the situation for female farmers regarding climate change is particularly concerning due to the interplay of traditional gender roles and environmental challenges. The section below outlines some of these challenges.

Unequal land access: Ghanaian customary land ownership typically favours men. Women may have farms on plots granted by husbands and/or fathers, which are often less fertile and further from their homes49. This results in reduced crop yields, increased vulnerability to crop failures, and heightened economic insecurity.

Additionally, limited access to resources, finance and decision making also hampers women. Modern democracies often lack gender balance in leadership, resulting in policies that may favour men's livelihoods over those of women. Women are less likely than men to have access to land or to plant, which impacts use, and fertilizer application depend heavily on factors bargaining power, access to credit and other resources.49 Furthermore, financial constraints and restrictions restrict women’s access to these crucial factors, limiting their ability to participate on equal footing with men.50 Climate change adaptation often requires investment in new technologies and practices. This could include the purchase of seeds or fertilizers, irrigation systems, improved storage methods, access to loans or financial resources, women struggle to adopt these strategies, hindering a woman's ability to adapt to climate change.49 Women also face barriers to soil conservation and measures to mitigate the effects of climate change on their crops.50 Investment in climate- resilient technologies like drought-resistant seeds or irrigation systems.

Furthermore, economic disadvantages and wage discrimination: Women are more likely to be unemployed than men. They often earn less money for similar work as men and women then lack the resources to adapt to climate change, making them more likely to experience severe impacts. Knowledge Gap: Women may have less access to training and education on climate-

### Vulnerable subgroups in Agriculture:

Disability people are one of Ghana's largest oppressed groups. In Ghana, over 700,000 individuals have disabilities. The proportion of households with disabled members experience a poverty rate exceeding ten times that of other households.*48*

A 2019 Global Burden of Disease (GBD) study indicates a substantial prevalence of mental health conditions in Ghana. The study estimates approximately 2.6 million Ghanaians, constituting 10.7% of the population, have been diagnosed with schizophrenia, bipolar disorder, or major depressive disorder. Despite this significant number, access to treatment remains a critical challenge. Only an estimated 2% of Ghanaians with a mental health condition receive the necessary care.*49*

People with disabilities in Ghana face many challenges including, lack of access to education and employment opportunities, stigma and discrimination, physical barriers that make it difficult to get around, and lack of access to healthcare and other essential services. However, several organisations are working to help people with disabilities in Ghana. Here are a few of them:

1. The Ghana Disability Rights Organisation (GDRO) is an organisation that represents the interests of people with disabilities in Ghana. It works to promote the rights of people with disabilities and to improve their access to education, employment, and other essential services.
2. The Presbyterian Relief Services (PRS) is a Ghanaian non-governmental organisation (NGO) that works with people with disabilities. It provides a variety of services, including education, skills training, and microfinance loans.*50*
3. The Leprosy Research Institute (LRI) is a Ghanaian research institute that works to improve the lives of people affected by leprosy. It provides a variety of services, including medical care, rehabilitation, and social support.*51*
4. On 5th of March 2023, Ghana's Disability Rights organisation called on policymakers to implement policies that will promote the inclusion and transition disability inclusivity.
5. The Ghana Enterprise Agency (GEA) launched a Persons With Disabilities (PWD) Enterprise Support Programme in collaboration with the World Bank. This project is expected to create over 150 businesses for persons with disabilities by 2030. It will focus on areas such as agriculture and logistics, hospitality and Information Communication Technology, and construction firms.*52*

# GENERAL CONTEXT

Morocco is a country located in the northwest corner of the African continent, bordered by the Atlantic Ocean and the Alboran Sea to the west, Algeria to the east, and Western Sahara to the south. The country has a population of 38.2 million inhabitants in 2024. According to data from the United Nations Population Fund (UNFPA), the demographic distribution of Morocco's population reveals that around 51% are aged between 15-64 years old, representing the largest portion of youth within the country. Additionally, around 68% fall within the working-age bracket of 15–64 years[1]. The median age in Morocco is 23 years and the population is aging slowly with a low proportion of people aged over 60 years or older.[2] There is an equal gender distribution in Morocco's population, with approximately 50% males[3] and 50% females.[4]

In 2022, Morocco had a Human Development Index (HDI) value of 0.688 (Higher values generally indicate greater levels of development). A value of 0.698 suggests that Morocco falls within a certain range of human development.

Over the past 15 years, Morocco has experienced significant economic and developmental advancements. The country's Gross Domestic Product (GDP) per capita has shown steady growth, averaging 3.2%. Additionally, the country is benefiting from a demographic dividend. This is a situation where a country experiences a period of high economic growth due to its population structure. Despite a reduction in population growth to 1.25% between 2004 and 2014, the dependency ratio has decreased by half and is expected to remain low until 2030.[5]

Morocco's dependence on agriculture provides an opportunity for the ARIF II Fund to support sustainable livelihoods initiatives that have a direct impact on smallholder farmers.

# Health data and analysis

## What is the maternal mortality rate, infant mortality rate, life expectancy (disaggregated by sex)

Accessing quality medical care remains a notable challenge, especially in rural regions. The uneven allocation of resources, insufficient funding, shortages of healthcare professionals, and the increasing prevalence of non-communicable diseases present significant hurdles. To address this issue, Morocco has embarked on a comprehensive health system reform, underscoring its dedication to developing human capital. This reform is set to enhance health outcomes and the quality of healthcare services by fostering improved responsiveness and efficiency in the delivery of care.

Maternal mortality rates have significantly declined over time, with Morocco's maternal mortality ratio (MMR) having improved from 246 in 2000 to 72 in 2020. Maternal mortality in Morocco is higher than its regional average, which stands at 13 deaths per 1,000 live births. However, the decline in maternal mortality rates reflects a slight gender disparity revealing that male infants faced a slightly higher mortality rate of 17 deaths per 1,000 live births compared to females, who experienced 14 deaths per 1,000 live births.

Despite these improvements, Morocco still faces challenges. The average life expectancy at birth is males expected to live an average of 73 years and females 76 years. These statistics underscore the need for targeted interventions to reduce maternal and infant mortality rates further and promote longer, healthier lives for all members of the community.

Morocco has a Universal Health Coverage (UHC) of 69 in 2021. This indicates that around 69% of Morocco's population have access to essential health services without encountering significant financial obstacles. This suggests a moderate level of healthcare accessibility and affordability within its population. However, there is still room for improvement as the UHC score refers to improving healthcare infrastructure, expanding service coverage, and addressing disparities in access across different regions and socio-economic groups. Therefore, while a UHC index of 69 reflects progress, Morocco still has the need for ongoing efforts to ensure that all Moroccans can access quality healthcare services when needed, regardless of their financial status.

## Sexual Exploitation, Abuse and Harassment (SEAH) Laws, Policy, Trends, And Data

### Any info on SEAH in the workplace? Trends on incidents?

According to a study done by Bouthour (2020), the legal system in Morocco faces the challenge of reconciling Western democratic ideals with deeply rooted Islamic beliefs and cultural traditions. This duality presents obstacles to the implementation of policies aimed at defining gender roles to protect women from sexual exploitation, abuse, and harassment.

Despite efforts made by the government to implement equality policies, women's rights continue to be inadequately protected, primarily due to shortcomings within the judicial system and public administration. Stemming from longstanding patriarchal traditions, women encounter persistent obstacles in their quest for equal treatment under the law and domestic violence. Their political consciousness and willingness to challenge such violence are hampered by societal norms, pointing toward continued gender inequality.

Bouthour (2020) also stated that, per-capita domestic violence in Morocco appears in various forms and arises from multiple factors, including, but not limited to, substance abuse, a deeply entrenched patriarchal society, and economic disparities. Additionally, Morocco's legal framework


---
**Page 248**
---

fails to recognise marital rape or psychological abuse as criminal offences, with rape being categorised more as a moral transgression than a violation of individual rights, as outlined in Articles 460/483 of the Criminal Code.

Morocco has integrated international human rights standards into its legal system, but challenges persist due to gender biases. Reforms to the Family Code and the adoption of legislation such as “Elimination of Violence against Women” (EVAW) have been slow in implementation and lack quality. However, traditional norms, including polygamy and child marriage, hinder effective protection against domestic violence. According to Bouhout (2020), access to justice is impeded by patriarchal attitudes, lack of awareness among women about their rights, inadequate enforcement issues persist, contributing to underreporting and unresolved cases of domestic violence. Despite these reforms, barriers and societal norms continue to undermine women’s rights and access to justice in Morocco.

However, non-governmental organisations (NGOs) play a crucial role in advocating for women's rights and bridging the gap between the legal system. These organisations contribute significantly to raising awareness about providing support services, and advocating for policies that promote better protecting women from gender-based violence.

In September 2018, Morocco passed a new law aimed at combating violence against women. This law includes provisions such as increased penalties against sexual harassment in public places, and tough penalties for certain forms of violence. However, SEAH against women still occurs in the workplace, particularly among young female students.

According to the International Labour Organisation, the longstanding issue of sexual harassment against female students in Moroccan universities has resurfaced due to a recent scandal. Professors at a business school in Oujda are accused of demanding sexual favours for good grades. This incident highlights the need for stronger measures and policies to address such issues.

Fear of social stigma and legal limitations often prevent victims from reporting the abuse. Official statistics on workplace violence and harassment in Morocco are difficult to find, with articles about SEAH against women in universities, particularly among students, being rare.

In evaluating the status of women's rights and well-being, several pertinent statistics offer insight. Notably, 12.7% of women aged 20-24 were married or in a union before the age of 18, indicating a prevalence of early and potentially coerced unions.

A 2019 national survey on gender-based violence and as reported by UN Women indicates that over the previous 12 months, 57% of women had been victims of various forms of GBV; this is a decline


---
**Page 249**
---

from 62.8% in 1999.*^40* However, economic violence against women increased from 8% to 15% and sexual violence from 9% to 14% from 1999 to 2019.*^40* 6.1 million (32%) of women reported experiencing intimate partner violence in the past year; both men (55%) and women (73%) indicated that they were “under the impression” that GBV had increased.*^40*

Furthermore, the adolescent birth rate remains high at 19 per 1,000 women aged 15–19,*^40* reflecting potential gaps in access to reproductive health services and education. Encouragingly, 72% of women of reproductive age (15-49 years) had their need for family planning satisfied with modern methods.*^40*

## Political and governing data and analysis

### i. What is the legal status of women in the country of intervention?

#### a. Are there any opportunities to promote the leadership of women in local governance/political systems and formal/informal institutions? If not, what are some of the constraints that hinder women from assuming leadership roles?

According to the World Economic Forum, Global Gender Gap Report 2023, Morocco is ranked "90th" out of 146 countries in the Political Empowerment subindex and has a score of 0.165. The rank of "90th" out of 146 countries indicates Morocco performs relative to other countries in terms of political empowerment. The Political Empowerment subindex has a maximum possible score of 1.0, with a minimum index value of 0.0. Morocco's score of 0.165 is below the global average of 0.273 and well below the regional average for North Africa of 0.489. The rank of 0.165 suggests that there are significant gaps in political empowerment between men and women in Morocco, with room for improvement in areas such as women's representation in political leadership roles.

The Women, Business, and the Law (2024) WBL index assesses the legal framework for women’s participation in the economy across 190 economies, focusing on a woman’s working life cycle. Morocco is ranked "83rd" out of 190 countries in the WBL index, with an overall score of 57.6%. The highest score in the region was 91.3%, achieved by Libya.

In terms of constraints on freedom of movement, laws influencing women's decisions to work, and restrictions on property rights, Morocco scores "48th" out of 190 countries with a score of 25.7%. This indicates that the legal framework in these areas supports women’s participation in the workforce and entrepreneurship without significant hindrances. However, in areas such as laws impacting women's employment opportunities, marriage, regulations affecting women's equal joint post-childbirth, gender disparities in property and inheritance, and laws influencing the size of women's

# Law Description

## Code du Travail, Art. 181
A woman cannot work in a job deemed dangerous in the same way as a man.

## Code du Travail, Art. 179
A woman cannot work in an industrial job in the same way as a man.

## Code de la famille, Arts. 89, 98 et 114
A woman cannot obtain a judgment of divorce in the same way as a man.

## Code de la famille, Arts. 129-136
A woman does not have the same rights to remarry as a man.

## Code de la famille, Arts. 342 et 348-351
Sons and daughters do not have equal rights to inherit assets from their parents.

## Code de la famille, Arts. 342-344
Surviving female and male spouses do not have equal rights to inherit assets.

### Table 20: Laws in Morocco

According to UN Women, in February 2021, only 28.5% of seats in parliament were held by women. On July 31, 2021, the Moroccan Parliament passed a reform that, despite not garnering significant attention, marked a significant stride in narrowing the country's gender disparity. This reform entailed an amendment to the 2019 Women, Business and the Law (WBBL) law, amending and complementing Law 17-95 aimed at fostering gender-balanced representation in corporate governance bodies. It introduced mandatory quotas for female representation on the boards of


---
**Page 252**
---

publicly traded companies, with a target of achieving at least 30% representation by 2024 and 40% by 2027.

The enactment of this law reflects exemplary collaboration among the government, parliament, and civil society, bolstered by the establishment of a task force by UN Women. This task force comprised women actively engaged in gender advocacy and several representatives from the 'Club des Femmes Administratrices'.

The World Bank, in alignment with its commitment to advancing economic inclusion for women and enhancing female participation in corporate governance structures, endorsed this commendable initiative. The World Bank also supported Morocco's first National Action Plan on Gender and Financial Inclusion II budget support program to the Moroccan government in 2021.[39] According to the World Bank, several factors such as inadequate job creation, limited employment opportunities, gender-based violence, and cultural norms, particularly affecting married women, serve as barriers hindering women's participation in the labour market.

Opportunities: Women’s representation in Moroccan political affairs remains inadequate, and even when elected, parliamentary procedures often hinder female Members of Parliament (MPs) from gaining significant influence.[40] Although the gender quota system permits successful candidates to contest elections, their ability to secure seats is limited. In parliament, however, parliamentary communications and outreach primarily spotlight plenary sessions, where male MPs dominate discussions. Women are more active in committees and working groups, where women are more active and have greater opportunities.

In collaboration with the Moroccan Parliament, the Westminster Foundation for Democracy (WFD) has worked to strengthen women's voices, participation, effective influence, and recognised role in decision-making processes within parliament.[41] WFD also supports parliamentary strategic engagements, which will be explained.[41] Training on women’s political leadership: A two-day working group was held with the Moroccan Parliament to support women parliamentarians in amplifying their voices and augmenting their impact. As well as Media training for women parliamentarians; WFD organised a training workshop for women MPs focused on refocusing communication strategies, media relations, and public speaking skills.[42] This is particularly in promoting women’s political leadership. It further strengthens the enduring relationship between the Parliament of Morocco and WFD, which has been established since 2018[43], and is vital to reinforcing this partnership and continuing to prioritise the advancement of women's political leadership in Morocco.[43]

# Employment data

## Labour force participation rate (disaggregated by sex), employment rate (disaggregated by sex), unemployment rate (disaggregated by sex)

In 2022, Morocco's labour force participation rate, as estimated by the International Labour Organisation (ILO), stood at 47.7%, reflecting the percentage of the total population aged 15-64 actively engaged in the workforce (World Bank Data Portal, 2023). The labour force participation rate also varied significantly between males and females: males had a higher participation rate than females (89% vs. 71%). Among those aged 15+ as a percentage of population ages 15+) (modelled ILO estimate) is 71.3% for males and 19% for females. Moreover, in Morocco, the labour force participation rate among females is 19.8% and among males is 62.0%.

In 2023, Morocco's employment landscape showed stark gender discrepancies. The overall employment-to-population ratio for individuals aged 15 and above was 40%, with males at 62.4% and females at 27%. Despite this disparity, the unemployment rate among women is lower than that of men: females experiencing a rate of 11.2% of the female labour force and males at 9.3% (World Bank Data Portal, 2023).

Individuals engaged in vulnerable employment face a higher likelihood of lacking formal work agreements, social security coverage, and safety nets to buffer against economic uncertainties, rendering them more susceptible to poverty. In Morocco, vulnerable employment affects 53.9% of women compared with 46.1% of men (World Bank Data Portal, 2023). This indicates a higher vulnerability among women in Morocco compared to the average rate across the Middle East and North Africa region (Male: 25.8% and Female: 22.7%). It is important to note that Morocco's vulnerable employment for females has improved since 1991 (Males: 53.6% and Females: 53.0%),[...]

According to Sadig (2011) [589] in Morocco, cultural norms play a significant role in shaping expectations for both men and women. These expectations can influence social interactions and language use. [589] Traditional Gender Roles: According to Sadig (2011) there is a prevailing belief in traditional gender roles where men are expected to be the primary breadwinners and decision-makers, while women are expected to prioritise family responsibilities and domestic duties. [589] Limited Economic Opportunities for Women: Despite progress, there's still a perception that certain jobs or tasks are more suitable for men, leading to limited economic opportunities for women. This stereotype can affect women’s participation in the workforce and their access to

# Pressure for Marriages and Motherhood

There’s a cultural expectation for women to marry and fulfill traditional roles as wives and mothers at relatively young age. This pressure impacts women's autonomy and decision-making abilities, their education, and professional lives.

## Unequal Access to Resources and Economic Empowerment

Women often face barriers in accessing resources necessary for economic empowerment, such as land ownership, credit access, and financial services. These factors contribute to a gender wage gap and hinder women’s professional advancement.

### Gender Disparities in Education

In Morocco, girls are less likely than boys to complete secondary education, leading to disparities in literacy rates and educational attainment. Moroccan women also bear the primary responsibility for caregiving and household chores, including childcare, cooking, cleaning, and tending to family members with disabilities or illnesses. This division of labor reinforces traditional roles within the family.

### Women's Time Allocation

Women and girls in Morocco aged 15+ spend 20.8% of their time on unpaid care and domestic work, compared to 19% spent by men.

## Education data and analysis

### i. Educational status of girls and boys, adult literacy rate (disaggregated by sex)
### ii. Do women have equal access to education, technical knowledge, and/or skill upgrading? (access to technical knowledge and skills upgrading is discussed in annexure section B)

In primary education, both boys and girls in Morocco demonstrate high rates of enrollment and completion, with a total gross enrollment rate of 95%[62] and primary completion rates exceeding 100% for females and males.[63] However, gender disparities become more apparent in lower secondary education, where completion rates for females (78%) surpass those of males (68.8%). Despite a slightly higher gross enrollment rate among girls compared to boys at the age of 15–19 years, both genders still demonstrate strong participation. However, completion rates for males are notably lower than for females.

In upper secondary education, the total net enrollment rate decreases (77%), indicating some challenges in retaining students beyond lower secondary levels. Furthermore, in tertiary education, while females[64] have a slightly higher literacy rate compared to males,[65] both genders still demonstrate strong participation.[66]

Regarding literacy rates among adults, males[67] have a higher percentage compared to females (69%), highlighting a gender gap in literacy despite progress in education at the primary and secondary levels. The World Bank Open Data[68] suggests that increasing tertiary enrollment could further advance educational equity in Morocco.

The World Bank recognizes that ongoing disparities in access to education and resources impede the progress of women's human development.[69]

The most recent survey data available for estimating Morocco’s Multidimensional Poverty Index (MPI) are from 2017/2018. According to these estimates, 6.4% of Morocco's population, equivalent to 3,588 thousand people in 2021, live in multidimensional poverty. Additionally, 19.9% of the population, or 4,028 thousand people in 2021, are classified as vulnerable to multidimensional poverty. The proportion of individuals living in multidimensional poverty is higher among those in multidimensional poverty, at 42.0%. The MPI value, which adjusts the share of the population in multidimensional poverty by the intensity of deprivations, is 0.027. By comparison, Tunisia and Libya have MPI values of 0.138 and 0.159 respectively.

In 2017, 16.8% of females and 41.5% of males in Morocco possessed an account. The disparity in account ownership between genders in Morocco, standing at 24.7, exceeds the gap across the Middle East & North Africa region, which is 18.8. Account ownership indicates the proportion of individuals who have a bank or other financial institution account, or who personally utilised a mobile money service within the last year.

There were 63.47 million mobile connections in Morocco in January 2021. It is important to note that users had more than one mobile connection; so figures for mobile connections may exceed 100% of the total population. According to the World Bank, more men than women used a mobile phone or the internet to pay bills within the last year. In Morocco, in 2019, 35.8% of males and 27.4% of females used it; both the Middle East & North Africa average and that of the lower-middle-income group. Internet usage refers to the percentage of respondents who report using a mobile phone or the internet to pay bills within the last year. In Morocco, in 2019, 35.8% of males and 27.4% of females used it; both the Middle East & North Africa average and that of the lower-middle-income group. Internet usage denotes the percentage of respondents who report using a mobile phone or the internet to pay bills within the last 12 months.

According to the World Bank Data Portal, more females work in the agricultural sector than males. This is due to women having limited ownership and control over larger agricultural landholdings.

Individuals engaged in precarious employment often lack formal job arrangements, social safeguards, and financial safety nets, leaving them more susceptible to economic instability and poverty.

The World Bank has highlighted that women face lower access to finance, primarily due to disparities in ownership and control of physical assets between men and women. Moreover, gaps in human and social capital significantly influence women's ability to access finance. Additionally, the lack of access to credit can lead to a vicious cycle where women often limit their access to improved inputs, subsequently leading to lower yields in agriculture.

A study done by Rachida & Meryem (2023) found that female workers in Morocco’s agricultural sector are frequently engaged in subsistence farming and have limited access to land. Women mostly focus on housework, caring for the family and children, and involved in some subsistence farming. According to the UN Women Data Hub, women and girls aged 15 and older allocate an average of 60% of their time to unpaid domestic work compared with men who dedicate only 3% of their time to similar tasks. The World Bank reported that in Morocco, women spend 7 times as much time on unpaid domestic and care work than men.

Women also have less access to improved inputs, subsequently leading to lower yields in agriculture.

In Morocco, the International Fund for Agricultural Development (IFAD) provides loans to help poor people in rural areas improve their livelihoods. This includes small farmers who own no land at all, small livestock producers, women in rural areas, and unemployed young people. Their focus is on the poorest households, which are often the most vulnerable to poverty and food insecurity. This program works alongside the Moroccan government's Plan Maroc Vert to improve agriculture. The program achieves this by involving rural communities in planning and by giving them the skills they need to manage their own projects. It also provides training for women entrepreneurs, helps them access credit, and gives them the opportunity to sell their products more effectively.

# Morocco's Climate

Morocco’s climate is diverse due to its varied topography, encompassing the Rif Mountains in the north, the Atlas Mountains in the center, plateaus in the east, plains and coastal areas in the west, and desert regions in the south. The majority of Morocco, especially along the coastline, features a typical Mediterranean climate characterised by mild, rainy winters and hot, arid summers. Running through the centre of Morocco, the Atlas Mountains serve as a natural barrier, separating the Mediterranean climate of the northern coast from the southern regions, which border the Sahara Desert.

## Climate Change Vulnerability

According to The World Bank (2021), Morocco faces significant vulnerability to climate variability and change, with impacts expected to be severe. These changes are particularly alarming for the agricultural sector. This trend will impact rural livelihoods and the overall national economy. The combination of climate and socio-economic factors in semi-arid regions exposes communities to multiple risks, including water scarcity, soil degradation, reduced agricultural practices, crop failures, and degraded meadows. Because of its geographical positioning, variability in rainfall is a natural feature of Morocco.

## Climate Change Impacts

In Morocco, the impacts of climate change are becoming more evident, with water scarcity, droughts, and floods emerging as pressing concerns. Climate change significantly alters the water cycle, leading to more frequent and severe droughts and floods, alongside escalating water scarcity issues. The impacts on agriculture are profound, affecting crop yields and livestock productivity, thereby reducing the resilience of forest ecosystems and agriculture, notably due to water scarcity. Efforts from The World Bank aim at enhancing Morocco’s climate change adaptation capacity in a context of limited resources.

## Key Areas of Focus

Key areas of focus include water resources, agriculture, forestry, energy, and healthcare sectors.

### Water Resources

Four-fifths of Morocco's most vulnerable inhabitants reside in rural regions, depending on rainfed agricultural practices. Climate change impacts these communities by reducing crop yields, impacting the livelihood of coastal fishing, posing economic and personal setbacks in areas where 65% of the population and 90% of industrial activities are concentrated. Climate change may prompt the migration of women from rural to urban areas.

### Women's Role

Women’s role in the agricultural sector is paramount, with many working up to 10 hours a day. This intense pace of work demands constant physical effort and can have implications on women's health or well-being. The World Bank (2023) notes that climate change leads to more extreme weather events, unpredictable growing seasons, and increased exposure to heat stress; hence, women working in agriculture may face additional challenges such as heat-related illnesses.

### Overall Challenges

Overall, studies indicate that women in Morocco face greater challenges during climate shocks due to limited access to financial resources. This then, in turn, leads to investment challenges, since women often lack access to seeds, fertilizers, or drought-resistant crops. Which limits their ability to adapt and rebuild livelihoods.

## World Bank's Role

The World Bank is assisting Morocco in both adaptation/resilience and mitigation efforts. To tackle water scarcity and its effects on agriculture, the World Bank is aiding Morocco in enhancing water

#### Organisation: IFAD Investing in Rural People

**Description:** Fatima-Aziza Zouhri transitioned from a Kandar-Solti Khair, a rural village in northern Morocco. Recognising the area's agricultural heritage and noting its absence of women-owned businesses, she established a weaving cooperative in 2013. Despite market challenges leading to its closure, she initiated another project with support from the PDRZM program by 2021. This expanded to include sheep rearing and beekeeping for income generation.

# GENERAL CONTEXT

Nigeria is located in West Africa and shares its borders with Niger to the north, Chad to the northeast, Cameroon to the east, and Benin to the west[1]. As of 2022, the population of Nigeria is estimated to be roughly 218.5 million[2]. Of this, 50.9% are male and 49.5% are female[3] The population is aged <15 years (37%), 15–64 years (59%) and >65 years (4%). Nigeria has a young age structure with the majority of its population being between 10 and 24 years old, representing a large working population, which significantly underpins Nigeria's development priorities and efforts. Nigeria's population identifies as roughly 50% Muslim and 50% Christian; and the country faces issues around religious tensions.

Nigeria’s economy has an 3.3% annual growth rate as of 2022[4]. As of 2018, the poverty headcount ratio at $2.15 per day was 39.9% in Nigeria[5]. According to the International Labor Organization, as of 2021 unemployment rate in Nigeria is 3.6% with this being 5.1% for females and 2.4% for males.

In terms of development, in 2022 Nigeria scored a Human Development Index (HDI) of 0.548[6], placing it at rank 139 out of the 193 countries in the world. In Nigeria, the leading causes of death (excluding neonatal conditions) are lower respiratory diseases, tuberculosis, diarrhoeal diseases and malaria[7]. The World Economic Forum's Global Gender Gap Report 2022 ranked Nigeria as having a gender gap that is 45% smaller than the country’s 2013 level[8]. Within Sub-Saharan Africa, Nigeria was ranked 30 out of 36 countries[9].

Nigeria faces several material development challenges, including poverty, security risks, policy uncertainty and corruption. The government has made some progress in promoting gender equality, although these are not considered comprehensive[10]. Reportedly, Nigeria's National Assembly rejected five bills aimed at advancing gender equality in 2022[11]. Legislation tackling gender-based violence is also lacking; for example, the Nigerian Institute of Social and Industrial Court of Nigeria’s (NISC) Civil Procedure Rules, Order 14, Rule 1 (a), (b), (c) and (d) which cover sexual harassment and violence in the workplace[12].

# Health data and analysis

## What is the maternal mortality rate, infant mortality rate, life expectancy (disaggregated by sex).

According to the World Health Organization, as of 2019, 3% of Nigeria’s GDP is allocated to health expenditure[65]. In Nigeria, the leading causes of death for females (excluding neonatal conditions) are lower respiratory infections (109.4 deaths per 100,000 population), diarrheal diseases (72.2 deaths per 100,000 population), and malaria (68.3 deaths per 100,000 population). In males, the leading causes of death in Nigeria (excluding neonatal conditions) are lower respiratory diseases (110 deaths per 100,000 population), tuberculosis (81 deaths per 100,000), and diarrheal diseases (72 deaths per 100,000). ### 34. Sexual Exploitation, Abuse and Harassment (SEAH) laws, policy trends, and data

#### i. Any info on SEAH in the workplace? Trends on incidents?

According to standards set by UN Women, 72% of the necessary legal frameworks that intentionally promote, enforce, and monitor gender equality (under SDG 5: Gender Equality), including those focused on gender-based violence, are in place in Nigeria[7]. According to UN Women, Nigeria has only 20% of its laws related to gender equality enforced. The Nigerian law is called the "Gender Discrimination Act". Certain laws implemented in Nigeria criminalise sexual harassment in the workplace[8]. Domestic violence and other forms of violence against women are also prohibited by the law[9]. However, Nigeria's civil remedies for gender-based violence are weakly enforced due to a lack of awareness about the law's efficacy in addressing issues of gender-based violence and harassment[10].

A 2022 Global Citizen article stated that Nigeria's Stand to End Rape organization published research showing that 85% of women surveyed said they had experienced sexual harassment at work, but only 37% reported it. According to Nigerian legal precedent, employers can be held responsible for the actions of their employees[11]. A 2020 study indicated that roughly 50% of women in Nigeria reported experiencing sexual harassment at work, but only 10% made a formal report or complaint[12]. This study indicated that the most common form of sexual harassment was "sexual comments, jokes, and innuendos" at 40%, followed by "sexually explicit language, inappropriate jokes and remarks" at 29%, "intimate acts, touching, kissing, grabbing" at 25%, "compromising invitations, offers or presents" at 23%, and "request for sexual favors in return for advantage" at 10%[13].

35. Political and governing data and analysis

### i. What is the legal status of women in the country of intervention?

#### ii. Are there any opportunities to promote the leadership of women in local governance/political systems and formal/informal institutions? If not, what are some of the constraints that hinder women from assuming leadership roles?

According to UN Women's metrics, Nigeria has 75% of the legal frameworks promoting, enforcing, and monitoring gender equality in place[68]. In line with this, Nigeria has significant gaps in the political and legal empowerment of women. These include:

- **Property laws**: Laws affecting women’s decisions on work, gender differences in property and inheritance laws.
- **Women's pay**: Laws affecting women's pay while working.
- **Children's custody**: Laws affecting children's custody after divorce.
- **Pension**: Laws affecting women's pension[69].

For example, the World Bank's Women, Business, and the Law Report on Nigeria states that there are restrictions to a woman’s ability to choose where she lives as well as how she can apply for a passport, with differences in legislation and legal precedent based on gender[70].

As of 2022, 4% of the seats in Nigeria's national parliament were held by women – one of the lowest levels in Africa. Women hold only 15 out of 36 ministerial positions (4%) and 18 out of 39 ministerial positions in Nigeria were held by women[71]. Notably, unlike much of the world and the majority of the African continent, Nigeria has no political gender quotas[72].

The World Economic Forum's Global Gender Gap Report 2023 reports that, since 2013, Nigeria’s political empowerment score has decreased from 11.9% to 4.1%, due to a recession in parity in both ministerial and parliamentary positions[73]. In line with this, according to the WEF Report 2023, Nigeria ranks among the lowest countries on the global index of political empowerment and political representation[74].

The WEF Global Gender Gap Report 2023 highlights that out of a total of 164 countries, Nigeria’s political empowerment score for women is 5.9%. Notably, the report points out that, in most metrics, Nigeria appears to be regressing rather than progressing in the journey towards gender parity in politics, such as decreasing numbers of women elected to national office[75].

Studies on Nigerian politics have consistently underscored the marginalization of women while identifying major obstacles against their political engagement[76]. These material constraints include:

### Employment data

#### i. Labor force participation rate (disaggregated by sex), employment rate (disaggregated by sex), unemployment rate (disaggregated by sex)

In Nigeria in 2023, the labour force participation rate for women is 52.2% and for men is 65.9%, according to the World Bank[64]. Notably, measuring since 1990, female labor force participation in Nigeria has increased from 38.7%. According to the World Bank’s Gender Data Portal, as of 2023, Nigeria's unemployment rate was 3.6%, with this being 5.1% for females and 2.4% for males[65].

Of employed women, only 14.2% were in wage and salaried employment in 2022, with this being 14.7% of the female labor force. The corresponding figures for men are 83.9%, or 84.5% of male employment or training, as a percentage of the youth population, was 18.9% for females and 8.86% for males, in 2022[66]. This shows a notable gap between employment for male youth and female youth.

The WEF Global Gender Gap Report 2023 ranks Nigeria at 54 out of 146 countries for women's economic participation and opportunity index (EPI)[67].

See Table 3a for a comparison of labour force participation rates, disaggregated by sex, for Nigeria and broader sub-Saharan Africa. This indicates particularly high levels of vulnerable employment for both men and women in Nigeria.

According to an estimate by the ILO, 93% of all employment in Nigeria is informal, with 95% of women working in the informal economy compared to 90% of men[86]. The ILO also reports that men tend to have diversified enterprises and are more likely to be self-employed or own their own businesses. Women are more likely to be informal employees, while more men own informal enterprises[87]. This could be linked to more men having access to capital and to the culture of patriarchy in Nigeria. Consequently, there are significant gender differences in the informal economy[88].

### What resources (economic, financial, physical, natural, other assets) do women and men have access to? Who manages or controls access to these resources?

#### To what extent do women and men from vulnerable communities participate in decision-making processes?

Gender-based inequalities are frequently recorded as a hindering factor in the human development of Nigeria. These inequalities are social, economic, political, and cultural, often leading to a social hierarchy based on gender[64]. Women being limited to certain types of jobs, being paid less than men, having limited autonomy over their income earning capacity, household monetary decisions, and other resources.

In a 2014 study published by Hagegan & Azeen on gender roles in Nigeria, several key gender-related findings were highlighted. The majority of men and half of the women surveyed believed that men should be responsible for financial matters[65]. Interestingly, 83% of respondents agreed with the fact that conflicts could arise when a woman earns more than her husband[66]. Interestingly, 53% of men and 60% of women believed that having a job is the best way for a woman to gain independence[67]. The study also revealed that 81.4% of men agreed with the statement that women are better at being housewives, 81.0% of men and 68.7% of women agreed that men make better political leaders which could explain the male dominance in politics[68]. Similarly, 74.5% of men and 65.0% of women agreed that men make better business leaders than women[69]. Interestingly, 31.2% of both genders agreed with the statement that being a housewife is just as fulfilling as working for pay[70].

These findings provide a snapshot of perceptions of gender roles in Nigeria.

The 2021 National Gender Report by Eurod’s National Gender in Nigeria Report recognizes Nigeria's pervasive unconscious bias against women and how this hinders women from taking leadership roles, linked to legal discrimination, social norms and social practices such as early marriage that hinder women from accessing education[71].

This National Gender in Nigeria Report[72] attributes Nigeria's lack of empowerment to social-cultural norms including:

- General socio-cultural practices including harsh and restrictive widowhood practices,
- Female genital mutilation/cutting, child marriage, and pervasive subjugating practices that diminish the self-confidence of women and girls.


---
**Page 279**
---

- Education access as a result of restrictive socio-cultural beliefs, referencing UNICEF’s reports that roughly 40% of the 10 million out-of-school children in Nigeria is female.
- Unequal property rights based on gender; for example, a man can own land and confer citizenship based on marriage, which is not the case for women.
The World Bank's Women, Business and the Law (2023) gives Nigeria a score of 66 out of 100 for women’s access to economic resources. The country ranks 85th in the world.
- The land ownership context in Nigeria is indicative of this; as of 2018, 88.5% of women do not own land, and 58.9% of men do not own land.
General access to education for women is also an issue in Nigeria with The World Bank estimating that as of 2018, 33.5% of women aged 15 to 49 in Nigeria report participating in decisions related to health care, purchases and visiting family[67].
A 2020 publication by the Government of Nigeria (related to climate change) discusses the underlying imbalances between men’s representation for men and women in social, political and economic sphere, and discusses how these imbalances contribute to policy formulation and implementation[68].
Based on data from 2018, UN Women reports that 35.5% of women in Nigeria participated in making decisions about household purchases, and 47.9% made decisions about their own health care; 35.5% made decisions about household purchases, and their own healthcare[69]. A 2023 academic article on women’s political participation and representation in Nigeria also notes that subservience in a woman is often seen as a desirable trait.
There are many reports of women's lives being significantly controlled by men in Nigeria. A 2023 article on access to information and communication technology discusses how some men in Nigeria control their wives’ access to the internet, either through technical means or corrupting media[70]. The article reports that a recent study found that roughly 50% of married men in Nigeria actively control their wives' access to the internet[71].

In Nigeria in 2018, the literacy rate for men was 71.3% and for women was 52.7%. This gap of 18.6% is larger than the regional sub-Saharan Africa aggregate, 12.7%. Notably, the World Bank reported no data on literacy rates in lower secondary school enrollment or higher education (disaggregated by sex).

However, a 2022 publication entitled Women's Economic Empowerment in Nigeria reported that primary school enrollment is 62% for boys and 52% for girls, with secondary enrollment being 52% for boys and 43% for girls. Only 17% of women aged 25–29 have a tertiary education. The level of education is a secondary education, 43% of adult women are unable to speak Nigeria's official language (English), and that the education statistics for both boys and girls are worse in rural areas as compared with urban areas.

According to the Statistical Report on Women and Men in Nigeria 2021, data from the National Commission for Mass Literacy. Adult and non-formal Education indicates that 48.19% of females have been literate since 2005, while only 37.6% were literate before 2005. This number increased to 50.20% in 2020 before increasing slightly to 46.10% in 2021. For males, these numbers were 50.81% in 2019, 54.09% in 2020 and increased to 53.70% in 2021. According to UNESCO, Nigeria is one of the country's children aged 5–14 years that are out of school. Only 6% of 8 to 11 year-olds regularly attend primary school and only 35.6% of children aged 36 - 59 months receive early childhood education.

According to The World Economic Forum’s Global Gender Gap Report 2023, Nigeria's parity on educational attainment has been fluctuating in recent years and has barely improved in the last decade. Nigeria's educational attainment parity is one of the lowest in the world at 42.9%.

The WEF Global Gender Gap Report 2023 ranks Kenya as 137 out of 146 countries for women’s educational attainment.

As of 2018, the poverty headcount ratio of 35.1% per day was 30.9% in Nigeria[16]. The poverty rate is estimated to have reached 38.9% in 2023, with an estimated 67 million Nigerians living below the poverty line – the world’s second-largest population living in poverty.

According to the WEF's Global Gender Gap Report 2023[17], Nigeria has a high level of representation of women in senior positions, at 64%, but a low gender parity in income with women earning only 50% of the average male wage. The report also notes that while 89% of men are employed, the female share of employment in senior and middle management in Nigeria is 57.2% as of 2022[18].

In 2021 in Nigeria, the proportion of men with an account (by themselves or together with someone else) at a financial institution was 69%, compared to only 43% for women. Women also had lower access to credit than men, with this being significantly lower for women, sitting at 35%[19]. As of 2021, 55% of the population of Nigeria were using the internet[20]. Additionally, as of 2021, 6.09% of women and 10.1% of men in Nigeria are in debt to pay bills. In all of these metrics, there is a clear discrepancy based on gender.

In terms of business ownership, UN Women reports that as of 2024 in Nigeria, 34% of business owners are women[21]. It has also been reported that women tend to lack knowledge or an understanding of the financial options available to them[22].

As of 2018 in Nigeria, 59.5% of men and 89.3% of women do not own a house[23]. Notably, 24.1% of men own a house alone, and only 2.5% of women own a house alone[24].

Nigeria has made significant progress in the digital divide since 2016, given that digital agency is a catalyst for development. Only 45% of women in Nigeria access information and communication technology (ICT) in the country as a whole, and across Nigeria, female ICT access is uneven and varies by region[25]. For example, while Lagos State has a high female ICT access rate of 60%, the northern region accounts for a considerable number (60%) of women who are digitally excluded, mostly in Sokoto and Kebbi States[26]. This contrasts with Lagos State in southwestern Nigeria, with a high female (85.7%) ICT access[27].

# SECTOR-SPECIFIC DATA AND ANALYSIS

## Agri-business data analysis

### i. What is the situation of women and men in the specific sector of intervention or in the project/program footprint area?

### ii. What roles do women and men anticipate to play in the context of the project/program? What will these entail in terms of time commitment and need for mobility?

### iii. Do women have access to education, technical knowledge, and/or skill upgradation with regards to agriculture?

### iv. What are the differential needs/priorities of women and men in the context of the project/program?

### v. What is their participation in agricultural extension programs in the target population compared to the assessed national/regional average?

### vi. It would be helpful to include agri-businesses supporting farmers. Are they hiring women - are there female entrepreneurs? How hard is it for them to attract capital?


In Nigeria, 28.9% of female employment was in agriculture as of 2022, with this being 44.9% for males**. The Government of Nigeria's National Action Plan on Gender and Climate Change for Nigeria states that "75% of women are engaged in agriculture as their primary occupation or an economic activity, with further dependence on rainfed agricultural practices, pastoral and nomadic animal husbandry activities". This is in line with a 2019 paper that discusses how women's agricultural productivity is lower than men's due to factors such as access to fewer inputs (such as fertilizers), less access to productive labour, and tend to farm less valuable crops, which are more difficult to market. Specifically, the two value chains in Nigeria that receive the largest budget allocations (rice, cotton, cocoa, and palm oil) are among those with the lowest representation and participation of women farmers**.

In Nigeria, more than 20% of working-age people participate in the agricultural sector, which accounts for about 45% of GDP. However, this is not evenly distributed across the country but significantly restricted female involvement, with women being less likely to oversee the plots they work on and less likely to be involved in decision-making processes**. Women farmers in Nigeria are almost 10% less likely to work in agriculture whether on their own plot or someone else's, when compared to men**. Additionally, women are 25% less likely to manage an agricultural plot than men**.

men, and those who do manage their own plot experience significantly lower productivity than male plot managers104.

Notably, female plot managers have vastly different household demographics than male plot managers, with the majority of female plot managers/owners (53%) being widowed, separated or divorced, whereas male plot managers are almost exclusively married (94%)105. Female plot owners also tend to be older, are more likely to have never attended school, and live in households with fewer adults106.

Female farmers in Nigeria yield 30% less per hectare than their male counterparts107. Male farmers use over twice as much fertilizer and herbicide on a plot compared to female counterparts108. Lower use of these inputs constrains the production of female plot managers because doubling the quantities of fertilizer and herbicide used on a plot in Nigeria increases agricultural productivity by 35%109.

Historically and at present, the majority of Nigerian women lack access to services including healthcare, education, water and sanitation, financial services, and information and communication technology (ICT) that are critical for increasing their productivity in agriculture. Additionally, Nigerian women were traditionally involved in subsistence level activities, empowering them with entrepreneurial and practical skills, if not formal education110.

Globally, women’s economic empowerment is recognized as a catalyst for development111. A World Bank study showed that women account for 50% to 50% of farmers and entrepreneurs who are growing crops, raising livestock, and selling food in local Nigerian markets112.

Women's economic growth, including in agriculture, is often limited through restricted credit access, with women’s lack of land ownership being a barrier due to a lack of collateral113. However, recent studies have indicated that 34% of the economic saving women do is through agriculture livestock114.

There is also a marked gender gap in access to agricultural extension services in Nigeria, with a 9% discrepancy between men and women farmers in direct participation115. Specifically, a World Bank study in Nigeria found that only 20% of female plot managers received any form of training during the planting season, with only 11% of women plot managers' households receiving the same, of whom only 8% were direct recipients116.

The 2X Criteria Thresholds™, which provide minimum requirements for global gender lens investment and organizational practices, specific to the agribusiness and food sector in Nigeria are as follows, shown in Table 30:

| Topic | Criteria |
| --- | --- |
| Entrepreneurship & Ownership | Founded by a woman (or group of women) that retain an active role OR at least 50% of shares owned by women. |
| Leadership | At least 50% of senior management is women OR 30% of board members are women. |
| Employment | At least 40% of employees/workers are women AND at least one quality employment indicator in place beyond what is legally required. |
| Products and Services | Products/services offered that enhance the well-being of workers/girls. |
| Supply Chain | Explicit commitment to women in the supply chain is demonstrated AND at least one quality employment indicator in place in the supply chain beyond what is legally required. |
| Governance | At least three practices that demonstrate intentional efforts to drive gender equality, representing one in EACH sub-dimension off: 1) Strategic action 2) Management systems 3) Data |

### 41. Climate change and agriculture
i. Will there be any anticipated differences in men's and women's vulnerability and adaptive capacity to climate change? If so, what are these?
ii. How does climate change affect female farmers vs male farmers?

**Page 285**
---

In societies where patriarchal privileges are prevalent, men often have a stronger adaptive capability to cope with the adverse impacts of climate change[1]. This is largely due to their privileged status in terms of education, economic, social, and political strength, which imbues them with a high level of flexibility for vocational mobility or change[2]. On the other hand, women, who are often conditioned to carry out domestic duties and have limited access to land and resources, face occupational mobility or change limits, making them more vulnerable to climate change threats[3].

In many societies of Nigeria, land ownership is often male-dominated[4], with a lack of access to land and land ownership making women more vulnerable to climate-related changes and weakening efforts to adapt. Women are responsible for the majority of food production in Nigeria, including water, food, and fuel for cooking and heating, tasks that are becoming increasingly challenging due to climate change[5]. In addition, women's disproportionate dependence on natural resources for their sustenance, coupled with traditional gender roles that discriminate against them, and a general lack of autonomy place women at a disadvantage when it comes to adapting to the impacts of climate change. It is therefore critical that addressing these inequalities is necessary to be able to enhance the adaptive capacities of the Nigerian people, and women in particular, to reduce vulnerabilities in the face of a rapidly changing climate[6].

As a reaction to the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) identifying Nigeria as a country particularly vulnerable to climate change and in recognition of gender considerations being a major determinant of vulnerability to climate change impacts, the Federal Government launched the National Action Plan on Gender and Climate Change for Nigeria in 2020[7]. This report acknowledges the gendered dimensions of climate change and its impact on women, men, children, youth, persons with disabilities, elderly people, farmers and grassroots communities[8]. The report discusses these groups' limited participation in decision-making processes and access to particular labour markets, as well as their exclusion from decisions in climate-related planning, policy-making, implementation, monitoring and evaluation[9].

Furthermore, this report cites unequal literacy rates, low political representation, and imbalanced economic empowerment as responses for women's disproportionate vulnerability to the impacts of climate change as a result of reduced adaptive capacity[10].

# Vulnerable Subgroups

Could there be a short section on vulnerable subgroups, e.g., children, girls, women with disabilities, the elderly, widows, indigent? Any specific info on them?

As of 2022, the population of Nigeria is estimated to be roughly 218.5 million people, with an age structure split of 50.5% under 14 years old and 39.7% aged between 15 – 64 and 3% aged 65 or older[1]. Nigeria’s population identifies as roughly 50% Muslim and 50% Christian, and the country faces issues of insecurity and religious tensions and conflicts[2].

Nigeria Solidarity Support Fund[3] identifies the most vulnerable groups in the country as being children under five years old, pregnant women, the elderly (aged over 65), and those who are ill or with pre-existing health conditions or disabilities. It reports that 70% of the most vulnerable people in Nigeria are less than 35 years old.

Women are systematically discriminated against in Nigeria, as discussed in the previous sections. Persons living with HIV/AIDS are heavily disadvantaged, being significantly more likely to experience extreme poverty than able-bodied people, facing almost double the likelihood of unemployment, and experiencing limited access to adequate healthcare services and educational opportunities[4].

In 2022, Nigeria’s National Health Insurance Authority Bill was signed into law, making health insurance compulsory for all Nigerians aged between 18–65 years old and for children under five years old. Despite the NHIS’s goal of providing affordable healthcare, access to medicine faces several challenges. Several challenges hinder access to medicine in Nigeria – inadequate funding, poor infrastructure, lack of trained personnel, and disparities in pharmaceutical infrastructure continue to impact access to medicine within the NHIS[5].

Nigeria has set up various initiatives to alleviate the challenge of unemployment and poverty, such as Youth Entrepreneurship Support Program (YESP), Social Intervention Fund (SIF), Graduate Internship Scheme (GIS), Subsidy Retirement and Empowerment Programme (SURE-P), and Youth Enterprise Innovation in Nigeria: Youth Empowering People Youth Initiative for Sustainable Agriculture[6].

Hey-Day (Izzi-Uwanji)
This is a cooperative society based in Enugu, Nigeria. It was established and duly registered on the 7th day of April, 1999.
The organization operates in various sectors including value addition to fruits and vegetables, cocoa, spices, essential oils, leather, textiles and clothing, creative industries, and organic farming. They produce newsletters, e-learning, and organic products. They are involved in community-based projects and closely monitored by the Federal Government of Nigeria. The cooperative contributes to the economic development of their local community.

Organization of Women in International Trade (OWIT)™
OWIT is a non-profit organization that became a member of OWIT International on October 26, 2018. It focuses on helping women entrepreneurs and small business owners in Africa.
Nigeria supports women to strengthen the entrepreneurial ecosystem through capacity building workshops, free and subsidized business support through webinars access to collateral-free and low-interest loans, and global exposure through international trade fairs and platforms for mentorship, entrepreneurship, and capacity building for competitiveness and sustainability.

Regamos foundation
This is a private, voluntary, non-profit, faith-based, non-governmental organization based in Lagos, Nigeria. Founded in January 2018 and incorporated on March 3rd, 2020, it aims to ease the plight of women, elders, and youth.
The foundation focuses on advocacy, education, economic empowerment, psychological support, entrepreneurship training, and capacity building for women. It collaborates with like-minded organizations for the benefit of its target beneficiaries. The foundation has carried out several intervention programs aimed at increasing access.

Association of Women in Trade and Agriculture (AWITA)™
AWITA is a non-governmental organization founded by Nigerian women involved in trade and agriculture. AWITA aims to harness the rich population of women in these sectors and related industries for economic empowerment.
The association promotes women's economic empowerment, gender equality, and sustainable livelihoods through agricultural engagements. AWITA's activities are designed to strengthen women's capacity and help them reach their full

It aims to develop the business, professional, and leadership potential of women through advocacy, education, mentoring, networking, research, training, and other programmes. It has affiliates in over 100 countries and includes members such as entrepreneurs, small business owners, executives, professionals, and young business and professional women. BPW Nigeria is non-partisan, non-sectarian, and non-profit. It has consultative status with the United Nations Economic and Social Council (ECOSOC) and other UN agencies.

# GENERAL CONTEXT

The Republic of Uganda is a landlocked country situated in East Africa, with a population of 49.9 million[1]. The country boasts a young youth population with 44% of the population aged between 0-14 years old and 25% aged between 15–64 years old[2]. Due to its youthful demographic composition, Uganda has a large working population and consequently needs to ensure sustainable job creation. 51% of the total population is female with women making up 39% of the workforce in agriculture while only 8% are employed in the industrial sector[3]. The country’s Human Development Index (HDI) is estimated to grow by 0.6 in FY24, while poverty is estimated to reduce from 41.3% (rate of $2.15/day international poverty line) to 40.1% in 2026[4]. Uganda reports a Human Development Index (HDI) of 0.595, placing it at the 178th position out of 191 countries on the HDI scale and at the 138th position on the Gender Inequality Index (GII)[5].

All the statistics above highlight the key developmental challenges Uganda faces, coupled with most jobs being in the agricultural and service sectors[6]. Uganda’s dependence on agriculture provides an opportunity for the ARAF II Fund to support sustainable agri-business and therefore impact smallholder farmers.

Uganda has made deliberate strides towards addressing gender inequality with the 1995 Constitution which guarantees equal rights of men and women. The country also ratified the law. Furthermore, there are legislations against domestic violence and sexual harassment in the workplace such as the Domestic Violence Act (2014), Sexual Offences Act (2014) and the Cyber (Sexual Harassment) Regulations 2012 and the Computer Misuse Act of 2011[7]. However, there is no legislation on child marriage or femicide. The Ministry of Gender, Labour and Social Development and the National Council for Women’s Affairs are responsible for gender equality issues in Uganda. The National Gender policy acts as a tool to guide and direct the planning, resource allocation and implementation of gender equality policies[8]. The government’s commitment has facilitated Uganda’s gender mainstreaming projects in all sectors of the economy. Additionally, on the World Economic Forum's Global Gender Gap Index in 2023, Uganda ranked 78 out of 146 countries with a score of 0.595. This is an improvement from its previous ranking of 80th place and the same economic participation, educational attainment, health and political empowerment as men in Uganda[9]. Given the persistent gender gap, ARAF II’s focus on the development of strategies to promote women’s economic empowerment through investments in companies in ARAF II's target countries is essential.

# Health data and analysis

## What is the maternal mortality rate, infant mortality rate, life expectancy (disaggregated by sex)

Uganda has an ambitious goal to become a middle-income country, with the government stating that Universal Health Coverage plays a key role and that the Ministry of Health aims to ensure comprehensive coverage for all Ugandans. The country faces significant challenges with leading causes of death reported by USAID being HIV/AIDS and Sexually Transmitted Infections at an estimated 13.2%, and tuberculosis and other respiratory infections at 12.6%. Women and children are disproportionately affected, accounting for 70% of all deaths from maternal and neonatal illnesses (1).

With 75% of the disease burden being due to preventable diseases:

Women's health data is key to understanding the health context in Uganda. The most recent effective health care coverage rate for women aged 15–49 years was 80%, with a total of 2,631 deaths per 100,000 live births, with accounting for 69% of maternal deaths worldwide (1). The World Health Organization (WHO) categorizes Uganda as having a high / moderate MMR alongside other East African countries. The maternal mortality ratio in Uganda is estimated at 425 deaths per 100,000 live births. For Uganda stands at 284 per 100,000 live births (1). With 40.5 deaths per 1,000 live births (1) and an adolescent birth rate for girls aged between 15-19 of 111 per 1,000 girls (2). Life expectancy is 66 years for females, 62 years for males.

2. Sexual Exploitation, Abuse and Harassment (SEAH) Laws, Policy, Trends, and Data

### i. Any info on SEAH in the workplace? Trends on incidents?

Gender-Based Violence (GBV) is a key issue globally; 42% of women in East and Southern Africa experience sexual or physical violence during their lifetime.

In Uganda, there are laws addressing gender-based violence, such as The Domestic Violence Act 2010, Secs. 2, 4 and 10-14; Penal Code Act, Arts. 123-125[68]. Additionally, there is in-depth legislation on sexual harassment in the workforce including "The Employment Act, Secs. 7, 70, 77, 95 and 96,"[69] "The Computer Misuse Act, 2011, Secs. 24-27"[70]. However, there are still many unlegislated areas regarding SEAH laws and there are no applicable laws regarding femicide or child marriage.[71] Furthermore, the law does not include protection for transgender individuals, people with disabilities, and those of non-marital status, or age.[72] The lack of this legislation not only affects women but other vulnerable groups.

Uganda's National Survey on Violence against Women and Girls (VAWG)[73] (2021) showed a high rate of gender-based violence. Fifty-six percent (56%) of women had a lifetime prevalence of physical or sexual violence, with 48% experiencing both forms.[74] The survey also revealed that 95% of women experienced sexual violence or physical violence or both.[75] With regard to vulnerable groups, childhood sexual abuse is extremely high at 59% for uneducated (never attended school) women and girls more likely to suffer.

As a vulnerable group, children in Uganda also face considerable challenges with 34% of females in child marriage before reaching 18 years[76] and 18% of children (5-17 years) engaged in child labour.[77] All ARAP II companies will follow GAPS that include SEAH policies to ensure the protection of vulnerable parties.

Uganda is the 23rd country in Africa and the 224th globally to ratify the International Labour Organization Convention 190 on Violence and Harassment in the workplace.[78] Additionally, the Ugandan parliament in 2023 passed the Employment (Amendment Bill) which mandates that all employers must work towards the prevention of sexual harassment, mistreatment, harassment, or

### Education data and analysis

#### i. Educational status of girls and boys, adult literacy rate (disaggregated by sex)

#### ii. Do women have equal access to education, technical knowledge, and/or skill upgradation? (access to technical knowledge and skills upgradation is discussed in agribusiness section 9)

Only 24.9% of girls and 27.4% of boys in Uganda have completed lower secondary school [10]. This is much lower than the average in Sub-Saharan Africa at 46% for boys and 43% for girls [8]. The adult literacy rate is 84.4% for males and 76.9% for females, representing a 7.5% gender gap [2].

7. Economic data

- X% more women reached with formal financial services in the target population than the assessment national average
- X% more women adopt mobile phones and use digital services in the target population than the assessed national/regional average

The country's poverty rate is up to 40%, down to 36% by FY24, while poverty is estimated to reduce from 41.3% (rate of $2.51/day international poverty line) to 40% in 2030[48]. The service sector has the highest contribution to the GDP at more than 40%, industry at more than 25% while agriculture contributes less than 20%. Uganda's Human Development Index (HDI) is ranked as one of the lowest in the human development category at '166/191 territories'[48]. Nominal expenditure is expected to grow from US$ 48.31 billion in 2022/23 to US$ 52.737 billion in 2023/24[48]. The national budget for FY2021/22 is estimated at US$ 69.5 billion, with the largest share of expenditure being on education. The biggest expenditure item in the Ugandan budget is debt servicing standing at 42% of the spending in FY2021/22[48].

Women's economic participation and opportunity in Uganda vary greatly with a labour force participation rate of 69.6% compared to 72.4% of males[48]. Wage equality for similar work is scored at 0.720 by WEF, which means that women do not get paid the same wages as men. There are no laws in Uganda with a gender pay gap. Access to credit is higher among men and women in Uganda with 52.7% of women with an account versus 66.1% of men. Uganda surpasses Sub-Saharan Africa's average of 40.3%[48].

In Uganda in 2017, 52.7% of women and 66.1% of men had ownership of an account at a financial institution or mobile-money-service provider. This gap of 13.4% is larger than the aggregate gap in Sub-Saharan Africa (SSA) of 15%,[48] In Uganda, 25.8% of men and 22.6% of women used a mobile phone or the internet to pay bills in the past 12 months.[48]. These are both higher than the average in Sub-Saharan Africa[48].

#### 8. Agri-business data

In Uganda, the agriculture sector accounts for 69% of total employment[104], 72% of all employed women in Uganda are working in the agriculture sector, compared with only 58% of all employed men[105]. Women face significant challenges in accessing land and credit. According to World Bank data, women farmers are increasing land values in Uganda, which results in the issuance of individual land titles and consequently, women who have less power and influence could suffer exclusion[106]. The issue of women’s access to land is compounded by the fact that in Uganda, as well as many other countries, few land is patrilineal[107]. Consequently, women rarely inherit land as it passes through male relatives despite legal provisions for equal inheritance.

Solutions to address women's lack of access to land and credit include:

- Special pathways for credit, gender-lens investment that facilitate the purchase of land, and lastly networking opportunities and associations that allow for pooled savings and provision of credit.
- Another challenge in Uganda face is that they are not heads of farming households. 23% of smallholder farms are headed by women[108]. Women who head their own farm enterprises are at odds with the fact that women account for 55% of the active population in agriculture, contribute to over 75% of all the farm labour and 90% of primary processing operations[109]. 49% of women engage in subsistence farming while only 28% of men do so. Women who head their own farm enterprises headed households carry out market-oriented agriculture (cash- crop etc.) compared to 19.5% of female-headed households.
