headed households.[10] These statistics highlight the gender disparity in profit-making agricultural ventures with women more adversely affected than men who are principally subsistence farmers.

Land access presents among significant hurdles for women in agriculture. As they typically depend on their husbands as household heads, lacking land ownership rights hampers their decision- making autonomy regarding crop choices aimed at improving environmental sustainability. While Uganda has made strides toward gender equality through its National Gender Policy, high levels of illiteracy and limited access to translated legal documents, which are primarily available in English, this lack of awareness hinders women from exercising their rights effectively. However, when these barriers are removed, women gain greater control over land resources, secure their legal entitlements and empowered with knowledge, they become unstoppable, unlocking their potential in agriculture.

There are also gender disparities in the level of quality and education of females and males in agriculture. Female plot managers complete an average of 1.9 fewer years of schooling than male managers, which is associated with lower productivity.[10] Women's participation in agricultural activities is boosted[10]. Additionally, female plot managers are less likely to receive extension advice from Uganda’s National Agricultural Advisory Service (NAADS) which could signify that Uganda’s extension services are not gender-sensitive and do not cater for women farmers’ needs such as type of activities, timing.[10] While the use of many non-labour inputs is low for both men and women, plots managed by men or jointly with family members are nearly twice as likely to use pesticides and organic fertilizers than those managed by women. This contributes to a gender productivity gap[10]. Solutions include ensuring that women apply appropriate quantities of non-labor inputs which can both reduce the productivity gap and increase the supply of food available for Ugandan households.[10]

To address the education gap and lack of relevant information, a possible solution is the provision of mobile phones to women farmers as shown in Figure 2. Using Uganda’s mobile technology, they have improved access to information that leads to an increase in bargaining power in the household[10]. Men on the other hand already had access to markets and information[10].

# Employment in agriculture (% of total employment)

## Table 34: Employment in agriculture in Uganda

The agriculture sector budget for the period 2016/17 to 2019/20 represented 4% of the total budget. Despite investment efforts, the agriculture sector has exhibited an inability to absorb the allocated budget and generate employment opportunities as intended. The analysis of the budget execution over these years reveals that the sector accumulated UGX 277.1 billion in unspent balances, largely under the development budget, amounting to UGX 256.4 (92%) of the budget.

Research indicates that government extension programs reach only 22% of farmers and do not regularly envisage female farmers as beneficiaries.

The sector budget does not specify the total amount of funds spent on women's empowerment specifically; however, several key service delivery interventions that facilitated women's empowerment were noted, including:

- Accelerating the transformation of agriculture from subsistence to commercial production by connecting smallholder farmers to the value-chain for the commercialization of agricultural products.
- The Uganda Coffee Development Authority (UCDA) in collaboration with the NAADS/OWC procured and distributed 1.035 billion coffee seedlings countrywide during FY2016/17–
- In FY 2017/18, a total of 1,065 farmer demonstration plots were established to enhance the capacity of vulnerable farmers, including women. UCDA invested substantially in coffee research and technology generation at the National Coffee Research Institute; and
- Recruitment of sub-county and district extension staff to increase farmers' access to agricultural delivery services.

Agricultural service access is characterized by wastage of cropping materials due to late deliveries when planting season is over, high defaulting loan rate due to harsh weather conditions, lack of extension advisory services to help in controlling pests and diseases; distribution of technologies that were not requested by beneficiaries; poor quality of inputs; inadequate volumes; and low outreach of the programs.

Through the Rural Electrification Programme (REAP), Gold also provides subsidies to individuals who wish to access energy through solar technology that is outside the grid from a radius of 100 metres. Under this arrangement, the government pays for 75% of the loan while the consumer pays the remaining 70%. This arrangement has for instances connected the Kalangala district, which is one of the hard-to-reach areas in Uganda, to power through a solar generator. The cost of electricity under this arrangement is UGX 128.4 per unit compared with high costs compared to the average income, with a unit of electricity at UGX 718.5. If on average a rural

9. Climate change and agriculture

v. Will there be any anticipated differences in men's and women's vulnerability and adaptive capacity to climate change? If so, what are these?

vi. How will climate change affect access to land and water resources by male farmers?

vii. Are there existing gender inequalities that may be exacerbated by climate change impacts in the proposed project/program footprint area?

viii. Any research on how women respond to shocks? Does less access to capital mean harder time recovering?


Uganda has a tropical climate, which is known for its stable rainfall patterns. However, it has recently faced significant climate shifts**. These changes include rising temperatures leading to more frequent and severe droughts and floods, as well as increased frequency of extreme weather events such as landslides, floods, and rising temperatures affecting various regions. There is a need for the development of adaptation strategies that address these challenges and reduce the adverse effects of climate change. This necessitates community-driven mobilization efforts, with numerous non-profit organizations undertaking projects to address climate challenges and assist local communities in adapting to these changes**. These organizations focus on empowering local women.

**Page 308**

communities, amplifying their voices, and cultivating leaders within the climate movement[10]. Through such initiatives, women actively involved in combating climate change are empowered, and these organizations aim to raise awareness about rural women's issues.

According to UN Climate Change (2023), [8] Women in rural Uganda are key to agriculture and natural resource management. Women are directly impacted by climate change, for instance, in rural areas where they are more likely than men to be displaced or killed as a result of natural disasters[9]. Additionally, they face greater challenges accessing finances, resources, services and information[10]. Consequently, they are less able to face and adapt to the challenges occasioned by climate change.

Women engaged in farming face a myriad of challenges, one of which stems from the impacts of climate change on their agricultural activities. Limited financial resources often restrict their access to inputs such as seeds, fertilizers, and tools[11]. They also lack access to targeted governmental information that may not adequately address their specific needs. This lack of tailored guidance exacerbates their vulnerability, particularly concerning their ability to adapt to changing climatic conditions. Women are less likely than men to receive support initiatives compared to their male counterparts[12].

Additionally, research shows that women perceive climate change and related shocks, but they have less access to information on the type of changes and the proper response options, and practical adaptations, leading to them being adversely affected[13].

### 10. Vulnerable Subgroups

i. Could there be a short section on vulnerable subgroups, e.g., children, girls, women and men with disabilities, the elderly, widows, indigenous? Any specific info into them?

#### Vulnerable and Marginalized Group Framework (VMGF)

Ethnic minority groups in Uganda, as identified by the World Bank's ESS7 on VMGs, include the Batwa (Twa or Pigmies), Ik, Benet (Nadorobs), and Tepeth (Soo)[14]. These groups face historical and ongoing challenges such as discrimination, limited access to resources, lower health and education standards[15]. Other marginalized groups in Uganda that face similar challenges include land dispossession, a focus on modern agriculture over traditional livelihoods, limited education access, lack of information about government programs, unequal development of social infrastructures, limited access to justice, and financial exclusion[16].

The Government of Uganda, through its Ministry of Agriculture Animal Industry and Fisheries (MAAIF), is currently preparing VMGF in collaboration with the World Bank under the Uganda Climate Smart Agriculture Support Project (UCSAP) Project 3.

The VMGF project intends to reverse the effects of climate change and sustainably increase agricultural productivity and household income while enhancing resilience to shocks through addressing the underlying causes of poverty in the most vulnerable communities. The targets are the most vulnerable and marginalized communities with low productivity and who engage in low-value economic activity that makes them more vulnerable to climate change shocks.

The project will be implemented across 10 districts, which will target addressing the primary drivers of poverty in the north-eastern dry lands (Karamoja); northern east savannah grasslands covering areas of east Acholi and northern Lango; Kyoga plains in areas of SE Lango. The project will also target the semi-arid western Uganda including Kasese, Kabarole, Southwestern drylands, Lake Albert crescent and eastern areas mainly Eghon highlands sub-regions of Bugisu, Busoga and Luwero. These areas are characterized by a pattern of increasing and high levels of poverty, land and natural resource degradation; as well as low value production, which are anchored in low productivity from engaging in low-value economic activities making them more vulnerable to climate change shocks. The project will support investments in technology and market-driven productivity increases in climate smart value chains. These investments will contribute to reversing the effects of climate change thereby sustainably increasing agricultural productivity and household income while enhancing resilience to climatic shocks.

Youth and children

Uganda boasts a population of 40.9 million[1]. With a large youth population with 44% of the population aged between 0-14, 54% aged between 15-64 and only 2% aged 65 and older[2], Children form part of the vulnerable groups in Uganda with the[3] indicating that 51% of the nation's children are living below the poverty line. This is a major concern as it is one of the leading cause of their vulnerability[3]. Moreover, malnutrition, diseases, lack of access to education and HIV treatment and violations of child protection regulations also occasion children’s vulnerability in Uganda[4]. Only 50% of these children are enrolled in primary schools while only 9% of these children can access education at inclusive schools and 10% at special schools[4].

People with Disability

The Ministry of Gender, Labour and Social Development are charged with ensuring the inclusion of Persons with Disabilities. The Persons with Disabilities Act 2023[5], aims to “foster resilience and

inclusion of Persons with disabilities in development.* This goal will be achieved through increased access to improved programmes, utilization of goods, services and facilities and adequate protection of people with disabilities facing abuse and neglect. The Mastercard Foundation (2023) reports that 3.2% of Ugandan Youth have disabilities, and youth with disabilities are twice as likely to experience poverty than their counterparts without disabilities. People with disabilities earn salaries at 74% lower than the earnings of people without disabilities Women with disabilities are also significantly worse off in employment indicators than their male counterparts with disabilities**

11. Organizations that Support Women Entrepreneurs in Specific Country (focus on the agriculture sector)

| Organisation | Description |
| --- | --- |
| **Support for Women Agriculture and Environment (SWAGEN)** | SWAGEN is an indigenous organization founded and owned by grassroots women’s groups in Uganda. It was established to go beyond engaging women at the National level due to the proliferation of women-focused NGOs at the national level. Its mission is to create strong women-led organizations that are sustainable, self-reliant, and meaningful partners to mainstream national development. SWAGEN advocates for a transition to agroecology as an eco-food production system in Uganda’s rural agriculture. Agroecology is a people-centred system of sustainable agriculture combining indigenous knowledge with contemporary science and technology to meet the needs of local and nearby communities. SWAGEN supports small-scale food production to build a sustainable, diverse, healthy, productive, and culturally appropriate food system for all Ugandans. |
| **Association of Uganda Professional Women in Agriculture and Environment (AUPWAE)** | AUPWAE is an association of professional women qualified and with vast experience in the fields of Agricultural Science and Technology, Environmental Science and Veterinary Medicine. Founded in 1992, it currently boasts a membership of 300 professional women working in both public and private sectors. The organization was established to achieve key objectives by enhancing the ability of rural women to acquire and utilize appropriate knowledge, technology and skills for sustainable livelihoods, resource mobilization and improved agricultural and environment conservation technologies. |
| **Rural Women Empowerment Network (ROWONET)** | ROWONET advances equity for women and girls throughout rural Uganda through research, advocacy, capacity building, and networking. Comprising 12 member organizations across Uganda that represent the communities ROWONET leads, all members share a common vision: equality and socio-economic justice for women in rural Uganda. The network’s mission is to promote climate-smart agriculture, enhancing access to clean water drinking water and sanitation services, and addressing other challenges facing women. UWT links the lack of economic empowerment among women to current development challenges such as gender-based violence; education (quality, quantity); health (nutrition, maternal mortality, among others). UWT has three approaches to women’s economic empowerment: |


---
**Page 312**
---

Building capacity of the women in agriculture for both household food security and having market income through their work and linking them to relevant services like agriculture training centres and marketing groups.

Building entrepreneurship skills among women and encouraging women to work as farmers and business owners.

Enhancing adult learning and skills development so that women can negotiate, participate and make informed decisions in all aspects of their social, political and economic lives.


ORUIDE is a grassroots NGO in south-eastern Uganda. Formed in 1997, the organization had been mobilizing and training rural women's economic groups (WEGs) to access credit from microfinance institutions (MFIs). The WEGs were using accounts with microfinance institutions (MFIs) from which they hoped their savings would be repaid.

Youth Go Green™

Youth Go Green is a youth-led umbrella organization of youth engaged in climate change, resilience, green growth, environment protection, SDGs, and other related issues. Formed in 2017, the organization's mission is to empower and engage young people in pursuit of low-carbon development pathways. The areas relevant to the project include climate change adaptation and mitigation, biodiversity conservation, green growth and youth skills and empowerment.

The Young Farmers' Federation of Uganda (YNFU)™

UNYFA was formed by a group of 10 farmers who met at an agricultural fair and launched in June 2017, as a duly incorporated member-based organization, with 54 members registered as District Young Farmers’ Associations (DYFAs). The organization has grown to over 300 DYFAs and more than 10,000 individual young farmers across the country. The organization is driven by the desire to create an enabling environment for young people in the sustainable economy; its target group is of youth between 12 to 39 years of age who are rural or urban agro-based and young farmers in and out of school/institutions. UNYFA aims at providing a platform where young farmers can share ideas, tailor-made training to enhance their livelihoods.

Young Farmers Champions Network (YOFCHAN)™

YOFCHAN is an organization for young farmers aged 14–40 years working together to shape the future of agribusiness. It was formed to support young farmers with the necessary entrepreneurial skills to run agri-businesses and provide affordable finance and link them to sustainable markets for them to embrace farming as a profitable business.
